11 October 2026 — Cancellation among close off-line pairs is a resonance in the bandwidth: a family of simple points with a large loss at one bandwidth, and, on a long enough range of bandwidths, one where the largest pair shows at full size

Working proofs for the flat window in the abstract complete-grid model; nothing is proved about actual zeta zeros. Resonance-free bandwidth. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Question. For off-line exclusion the Gram route lacked, beside its premise, a lower bound for the form when off-line pairs lie close together (many-pair bound). This cycle develops the queue's head through its three sub-ideas: how large the loss can be for simple points at one bandwidth, whether a change of bandwidth removes it, and the adaptive test vector. The test ends with proofs of the first two or a configuration that defeats the second.

New mathematics. Two statements for the flat window. (1) For \(n=2^k\) simple points at equal distance \(\delta\) with subset-sum heights \(\sum_{i\in I}q_i\pi/\Lambda_0\), \(q_i\) odd, the off-axis part at bandwidth \(\Lambda_0\) satisfies \(\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le(e^{2b}/2b^2)(2k+1)!\prod_i(q_i\pi/2b)^2+3n^2\), which is \(O_k(b^{-2k})\) times \(2n\kappa^2\). (2) For every configuration with off-axis points at pairwise distinct heights, least gap \(g\), total multiplicity \(M\) and a pair of largest \(\delta_*\) with multiplicity \(m_*\): every interval \([\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\) with \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\) and \(H\ge4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) contains a bandwidth with \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\ge\tfrac{\sqrt2}2m_*\kappa_\Lambda(\delta_*)-M\). No condition on the other \(\delta_j\) and no separation relative to \(\delta\) is needed; the gap \(g\) enters only through \(H\). The two statements use different benchmarks: (1) measures against all pairs, \(2n\kappa^2\), and (2) against the single largest pair, \(m_*\kappa\).

Goal progress. Unchanged. In the model, for configurations at distinct heights, a second-moment bound on a range of bandwidths of length \(4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) bounds the largest \(\delta\), clusters included; that length depends on the unknown configuration and can exceed the bandwidth itself by a large factor. The premise is as before: unavailable beyond bandwidth \(\log T\), and by the real-configuration lemma a consequence of the goal. So the Gram route is a reformulation in the model, with these qualifications, and still supplies no estimate. Bearing. At the goal's parameters this gives nothing, because the premise is not available; the cycle was spent on it because the queue's head asked for the many-pair loss and its answer shows that clusters are not what decides for or against this route.

General statement. thm:resonance-free-bandwidth, for every finite configuration with off-axis points at pairwise distinct heights, flat window, every \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\).

Setting

Flat window \(\varphi=1_{[-\Lambda/2,\Lambda/2]}\), complete grid, as in the two previous entries on this model. Off-axis points \(z_j=\gamma_j-i\delta_j\), \(\delta_j>0\), \(1\le j\le n\), multiplicities \(m_j>0\), \(M=\sum_jm_j\). The Gram form at bandwidth \(\Lambda\) is \(G_\Lambda=G^{\mathrm{on}}_\Lambda+B_\Lambda\), its on-line and off-axis parts. By lem:gram-second-moment-identity with \(\omega(s)=\Lambda-|s|\), \(\|\varphi\|_2^4=\Lambda^2\), and evenness of the integrand, \[\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2={2\over\Lambda^2}\int_0^\Lambda(\Lambda-s)\,|\Sigma(s)|^2\,ds,\qquad\Sigma(s)=2\sum_jm_je^{-is\gamma_j}\cosh(\delta_js).\] Write \(\|f\|_\Lambda^2=\int_0^\Lambda(\Lambda-s)|f(s)|^2ds\), so \(\Lambda\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\sqrt2\,\|\Sigma\|_\Lambda\), and \(\kappa_\Lambda(\delta)=\sinh(\delta\Lambda)/(\delta\Lambda)\). A solitary pair of multiplicity one has \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=2\kappa^2+2\). In general \(|\Sigma(s)|\le2M\cosh(\delta_*s)\) with \(\delta_*=\max_j\delta_j\), so \[\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le M\sqrt{2\kappa_\Lambda(\delta_*)^2+2}\qquad\text{for every }\Lambda.\]

Subset-sum resonance

Proposition (subset-sum resonance). prop:subset-sum-resonance. Let \(k\ge1\), \(\Lambda_0>0\), \(\delta>0\), \(b=\delta\Lambda_0\), and let \(q_1,\dots,q_k\) be odd integers whose \(2^k\) subset sums are distinct. Take the \(n=2^k\) points \(\gamma_I-i\delta\) with \(\gamma_I=\sum_{i\in I}q_i\pi/\Lambda_0\), \(I\subseteq\{1,\dots,k\}\), all of multiplicity one. Then \[\|B_{\Lambda_0}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le{e^{2b}\over2b^2}\,(2k+1)!\prod_{i=1}^k\Bigl({q_i\pi\over2b}\Bigr)^2+3n^2.\] For \(q_i=3^{i-1}\) and \(b\ge1\) the right side divided by \(2n\kappa_{\Lambda_0}(\delta)^2\) is at most \((3/2n)(2k+1)!\prod_i(q_i\pi/2b)^2+9nb^2e^{-2b}\), which is \(O_k(b^{-2k})\). Hence there are no constants \(c>0\) and \(A\) such that \(\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\ge c\,b^{-A}\sum_jm_j^2\kappa_j^2\) holds, uniformly in \(n\), for all configurations of simple points at a single bandwidth.

Proof. With \(\alpha_i=q_i\pi/\Lambda_0\), \(D(s)=\sum_Ie^{-is\gamma_I}=\prod_i(1+e^{-is\alpha_i})\) and \(\Sigma(s)=2\cosh(\delta s)D(s)\). Since \(4\cosh^2(\delta s)=e^{2\delta s}+2+e^{-2\delta s}\) and \(|D|\le n\), the part with \(2+e^{-2\delta s}\le3\) contributes at most \((2/\Lambda_0^2)\cdot3n^2\cdot\Lambda_0^2/2=3n^2\). For the rest put \(s=\Lambda_0-u\). As \(q_i\) is odd, \(e^{-i\Lambda_0\alpha_i}=-1\), so \(|1+e^{-i(\Lambda_0-u)\alpha_i}|=|1-e^{iu\alpha_i}|=2|\sin(u\alpha_i/2)|\le u|\alpha_i|\), and \(|D(\Lambda_0-u)|^2\le u^{2k}\prod_i\alpha_i^2\). Therefore \[{2\over\Lambda_0^2}\int_0^{\Lambda_0}u\,e^{2\delta(\Lambda_0-u)}|D(\Lambda_0-u)|^2\,du\le{2e^{2b}\over\Lambda_0^2}\prod_i\alpha_i^2\int_0^\infty u^{2k+1}e^{-2\delta u}\,du={2e^{2b}\over\Lambda_0^2}{(2k+1)!\over(2\delta)^{2k+2}}\prod_i\alpha_i^2,\] which is the stated main term because \(|\alpha_i|/(2\delta)=|q_i|\pi/(2b)\) and \(\Lambda_0^2(2\delta)^2=4b^2\). For the ratio, \(\kappa^2=\sinh^2b/b^2\ge e^{2b}/(6b^2)\) for \(b\ge1\) (as \((1-e^{-2})^2/4>1/6\)). The last sentence follows by taking \(2k>A\) and \(b\to\infty\). \(\square\)

The case \(k=1\), \(q_1=1\) is the configuration of prop:two-pair-cancellation, whose exact leading ratio \(3\pi^2/(4b^2)\) equals the present bound's leading ratio \(3!\,(\pi/2b)^2/2\), computed with \(\kappa^2\sim e^{2b}/(4b^2)\). The implied constant grows quickly with \(k\): for \(q_i=3^{i-1}\) it is about \((2k+1)!\,3^{k(k-1)}(\pi/2)^{2k}/n\), so the decay sets in only for \(b\) well above \(3^k\); in the control below the ratio for \(k=3\) at \(b=24\) is larger than for \(k=2\). For a fixed \(n\) the proposition excludes only exponents \(A<2\log_2n\).

Structural reason. An off-line pair's contribution to the second moment is concentrated in a window of length about \(1/\delta\) at the end \(|s|=\Lambda\) of the range, because of the weight \(e^{2\delta s}\). There the phases \(e^{-is\gamma_j}\) of points within height \(\delta\) of each other are nearly frozen, so heights chosen as subset sums of odd multiples of \(\pi/\Lambda_0\) make the sum vanish to order \(k\) exactly at that end. The same reason applies to every lower bound at one fixed bandwidth that is linear or quadratic in the points' exponentials: the solitary bound, the bound through \(\rho\), and any single test vector. It does not apply when the bandwidth may move by more than the phases' common period, which is what the theorem below uses, nor to the continuation criterion, which tests at complex \(s\) over all scales.

Resonance-free bandwidth

Theorem (resonance-free bandwidth). thm:resonance-free-bandwidth. Let the heights \(\gamma_j\) be pairwise distinct, \(g=\min_{j\ne l}|\gamma_j-\gamma_l|\) (no condition if \(n=1\)), \(\delta_*=\max_j\delta_j\), attained at an index \(j_*\) with multiplicity \(m_*\). Let \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\) and \(H\ge4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) (\(H=0\) allowed if \(n=1\)). Then there is \(\Lambda\in[\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\) with \[\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\ \ge\ {\sqrt2\over4}\,m_*\,{e^{\delta_*\Lambda}\over\delta_*\Lambda}-M\ \ge\ {\sqrt2\over2}\,m_*\,\kappa_\Lambda(\delta_*)-M.\] Consequently \(\|G_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\ge\tfrac{\sqrt2}2m_*\kappa_\Lambda(\delta_*)-M-\|G^{\mathrm{on}}_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\) at that bandwidth.

Proof. Split \(2\cosh(\delta_js)=e^{\delta_js}+e^{-\delta_js}\): \(\Sigma(s)=e^{\delta_*s}F(s)+R(s)\) with \[F(s)=\sum_ja_j(s)e^{-is\gamma_j},\quad a_j(s)=m_je^{-(\delta_*-\delta_j)s},\qquad R(s)=\sum_jm_je^{-is\gamma_j}e^{-\delta_js}.\] For \(s\ge0\): \(|R(s)|\le M\); each \(a_j\) is positive and nonincreasing with \(a_j(s)\le m_j\); and \(a_{j_*}=m_*\) is constant. Mean of \(|F|^2\). Let \(x_0\ge0\), \(H>0\). Expanding, \(\int_{x_0}^{x_0+H}|F|^2=\sum_j\int a_j^2+\sum_{j\ne l}\int a_ja_le^{-ix(\gamma_j-\gamma_l)}dx\). The diagonal is at least \(Hm_*^2\). For \(j\ne l\), \(f=a_ja_l\) is positive, smooth and nonincreasing, and integration by parts gives \(|\int_{x_0}^{x_0+H}f(x)e^{-i\nu x}dx|\le(f(x_0)+f(x_0+H)+\int|f'|)/|\nu|=2f(x_0)/|\nu|\le2m_jm_l/g\). So \[{1\over H}\int_{x_0}^{x_0+H}|F(x)|^2\,dx\ \ge\ m_*^2-{2M^2\over Hg}\ \ge\ {m_*^2\over2}\qquad(H\ge4M^2/(gm_*^2)).\] For \(n=1\), \(|F|^2=m_*^2\) identically. Average over bandwidths. Put \(Q(\Lambda)=\int_0^\Lambda u\,e^{-2\delta_*u}|F(\Lambda-u)|^2du\), so that \(\|e^{\delta_*s}F\|_\Lambda^2=e^{2\delta_*\Lambda}Q(\Lambda)\) by the substitution \(u=\Lambda-s\). For \(0\le u\le\Lambda_1\) and \(\Lambda\) ranging over \([\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\), the point \(\Lambda-u\) ranges over an interval of length \(H\) starting at \(x_0=\Lambda_1-u\ge0\). Hence, discarding \(u>\Lambda_1\), \[{1\over H}\int_{\Lambda_1}^{\Lambda_1+H}Q(\Lambda)\,d\Lambda\ \ge\ {m_*^2\over2}\int_0^{\Lambda_1}u\,e^{-2\delta_*u}\,du={m_*^2\over8\delta_*^2}\bigl(1-(1+2\delta_*\Lambda_1)e^{-2\delta_*\Lambda_1}\bigr)\ \ge\ {m_*^2\over16\delta_*^2},\] the last step because \((1+x)e^{-x}\le3e^{-2}<1/2\) for \(x=2\delta_*\Lambda_1\ge2\). So some \(\Lambda\) in the interval has \(Q(\Lambda)\ge m_*^2/(16\delta_*^2)\) (for \(n=1\) and \(H=0\) this holds at \(\Lambda=\Lambda_1\) directly). Conclusion. At that \(\Lambda\), by the triangle inequality in the weighted norm and \(\|R\|_\Lambda^2\le M^2\Lambda^2/2\), \[{\Lambda\over\sqrt2}\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|\Sigma\|_\Lambda\ge e^{\delta_*\Lambda}\sqrt{Q(\Lambda)}-\|R\|_\Lambda\ge{m_*e^{\delta_*\Lambda}\over4\delta_*}-{M\Lambda\over\sqrt2}.\] Multiplying by \(\sqrt2/\Lambda\) gives the first inequality, and \(e^{x}/x\ge2\sinh x/x\) the second. The statement for \(G_\Lambda\) is the triangle inequality. \(\square\)

What is and is not used. Distinct heights and the least gap enter only through the off-diagonal oscillatory integrals; two off-axis points at the same height and different \(\delta\) are excluded by the hypothesis and not treated, and \(H\to\infty\) as \(g\to0\). Nothing is assumed about the sizes of the other \(\delta_j\): pairs with \(\delta_j\) close to \(\delta_*\) are handled by monotonicity of the amplitudes, which the worst-case \(\rho\) of the many-pair bound could not do. The hypothesis \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\) serves only the constant. The benchmark is the single largest pair: against \((\sum_jm_j^2\kappa_j^2)^{1/2}\) a factor up to \(\sqrt n\) or more is not recovered by this theorem.

What it gives conditionally (a computation, not a theorem about \(\zeta\)). Fix a configuration at distinct heights and \(a\ge0\), and suppose \(\|G_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le Ce^{a\Lambda}\) for all \(\Lambda\) in an interval \([\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\) as in the theorem. Let \(K\) bound \(\|G^{\mathrm{on}}_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\) over the whole interval (the real-configuration lemma bounds it at each bandwidth, with cells whose length depends on the bandwidth). Then \(e^{\delta_*\Lambda}\le2\sqrt2\,\delta_*\Lambda\,(Ce^{a\Lambda}+M+K)/m_*\) at one \(\Lambda\ge\Lambda_1\), so for bounded \(\delta_*\), \(\delta_*\le a+O(\log(\Lambda_1(C+M+K))/\Lambda_1)\). In the other direction the Setting gives \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le M\sqrt{2\kappa_\Lambda(\delta_*)^2+2}\) at every bandwidth. So in the model the growth exponent in \(\Lambda\) of the second moment over such a range equals the largest \(\delta\) up to the stated error, for configurations at distinct heights. The range is the limitation: \(H=4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) depends on the unknown configuration, and with \(M\) the total off-line multiplicity up to height \(T\) it may be a power of \(T\) against \(\Lambda\) of order \(\log T\). A statement confined to bandwidths within a bounded factor of \(\log T\) is not obtained, and by the reviewer's examples below it is not to be expected in this form.

Numerical control

Nonvacuity. The script bandwidth_scan.py evaluates the registered closed form of \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\). For the proposition it takes \(\delta=0.4\), \(q_i=3^{i-1}\) and \((k,\Lambda_0)\in\{(1,30),(2,30),(2,60),(3,60),(3,120)\}\): the ratio to \(2n\kappa^2\) is \(0.0500,0.0465,0.00428,0.00620,0.000329\), each below the bound's ratio \(0.0514,0.0793,0.00495,0.0361,0.000564\); a wrong power of \(b\) or a missing factorial would break the comparison at \(k=1\), where the bound is within three percent of the value. For the theorem it scans 4001 bandwidths in \([\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\), \(H=4M^2/(gm_*^2)\), for 20 configurations: the five subset-sum families started at their resonant bandwidth, twelve random clusters (\(n=1,2,4,8\), gaps in \([0.02,0.2]\), multiplicities \(1\) or \(2\)) and three clusters whose two largest \(\delta\) differ by \(10^{-3}\). In every case the largest value of \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}-(\tfrac{\sqrt2}2m_*\kappa-M)\) over the scan is positive. A positive value on a grid confirms existence; a grid could miss a maximum, so the scan could not have refuted the theorem, and it checks the proof's direction and constants only.

What the scan does not test. It never needed the range \(H\). For the five subset-sum families the maximizing bandwidth is close to \(2\Lambda_0\) (\(57.8,61.8,121.4,122.3,242.2\) for \(\Lambda_0=30,30,60,60,120\)), where all terms of the product are in phase; for six of the nine random clusters with \(n\ge2\) and one of the three near-equal clusters it is \(\Lambda_1\) itself, and for the other five it lies below \(2\Lambda_1\). So these samples cannot distinguish the theorem's \(H\), which runs from \(112\) to \(23281\) here, from a much shorter range. The scan step is \(H/4000\), between \(0.03\) and \(5.8\), against a resonance width of about \(1/\delta_*\approx2.5\); for the largest \(H\) the step exceeds that width, so the reported smallest ratios \(\|B_\Lambda\|/(m_*\kappa)\) over a scan (between \(0.022\) and \(0.27\) for the subset-sum families, between \(0.18\) and \(0.34\) for the near-equal clusters, against largest ratios from \(2.8\) to \(11\)) undersample the resonances and are upper bounds for the true minima. The first run overflowed double precision at bandwidths in the thousands and failed eleven of the scans (archived session output rh-cluster-05-5461a5d732); the second multiplies every quantity by \(e^{-\delta_*\Lambda}\), which does not change the sign tested. All checks pass (output). The control is not registered as a claim.

Sub-ideas

  • Shorten the range of bandwidths in the theorem and find what is necessary: verify the reviewer's two examples (equally spaced heights at gap \(\pi/(2\Lambda_1)\), with unit and with Fejér multiplicities) showing that a range of length about \(\pi/g\) is needed and that against \(\sum_jm_j^2\kappa_j^2\) every bandwidth in \([\Lambda_1,2\Lambda_1]\) can lose a factor of order \(n\); and prove the theorem with \(H=C/g\) for equal \(\delta\) by the Montgomery–Vaughan mean value theorem, and with \(H=8(1+\ln n)\sum_jm_j^2/(gm_*^2)\) in general.

Follow-up. Continuing: the check asked for the loss with \(n\ge2\) pairs as a function of \(n\) and \(b\). At a single bandwidth the loss for simple points is \(O_k(b^{-2k})\) on the subset-sum family; over a range of bandwidths of length \(4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) the largest pair is recovered up to a constant. The length of the range needed is not determined and is the sub-idea above; it still bears on off-line exclusion, since it separates statements at bandwidths comparable to \(\log T\) from statements at far larger ones. Next attempt: that sub-idea, starting from the reviewer's examples. Record search. For that attempt the registry was searched for Hilbert inequality Montgomery Vaughan, large sieve, Riesz basis exponentials lower bound and almost periodic mean value average over bandwidth: no record on these objects beyond this notebook's own claims. Its sub-ideas as queued before this entry:

  • The family of \(2^k\) simple points is verified and its loss bounded above by the proposition, with the control's values. Between the exponents \(2\log_2n\) and \(2(n-1)\) the exact single-bandwidth loss is not determined, and nothing on the route depends on it. Follow-up. Developed: prop:subset-sum-resonance, with its control in research/results/rh_cluster_05/bandwidth_scan.json.
  • The question was whether, for equal \(\delta\), some bandwidth in \([\Lambda_0,2\Lambda_0]\) has \(\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\ge c\sum_jm_j^2\kappa_j^2\). The theorem answers a different question: it allows unequal \(\delta\), but needs the range \(H=4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) in place of \(\Lambda_0\) and recovers only the largest pair, \(m_*^2\kappa^2\), not the sum. As asked, the reviewer's equally spaced example (unverified) indicates the answer is no. Follow-up. Developed: thm:resonance-free-bandwidth answers the bandwidth question in the form that holds; the form asked is carried, with the example, by the new sub-idea.
  • The adaptive test vector aimed at \(\lambda_{\max}\) directly. It is not attempted. At the resonant bandwidth of the subset-sum family no test vector helps the off-axis part, because \(\lambda_{\max}(B)\le\|B\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\). At a resonance-free bandwidth a test vector could at most save the factor \((2n)^{3/2}\) lost in passing from the norm to \(\lambda_{\max}\) (many-pair norm bound, part 1); a factor polynomial in \(n\) moves the bound on \(\delta_*\) by \(O(\log n/\Lambda)\), which the conditional computation already allows for. Follow-up. Closed: superseded for the route's purpose by thm:resonance-free-bandwidth, limited at a single bandwidth by prop:subset-sum-resonance, and the residue it could still give is a factor \((2n)^{3/2}\) that does not change the exponent. Stakes. A yes (a test vector recovering the solitary bound at one bandwidth for all clusters) is excluded for the off-axis part alone by the proposition. A no leaves the route where the theorem puts it, with the factor \((2n)^{3/2}\) for \(\lambda_{\max}\) in place. Either way the open statement is not affected, because the missing premise is the same. Sources read. The closure relies on recorded results only: the two statements of this entry, part 1 of prop:many-pair-norm-bound and the inequality \(\lambda_{\max}\le\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\).

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • The range cannot be a constant multiple of \(\Lambda_1\): equal \(\delta\), heights \(jg\) for \(0\le j<N\), \(g=\pi/(2\Lambda_1)\). For simple points the Dirichlet kernel is bounded by \(\sqrt2\) on \([\Lambda_1,3\Lambda_1]\), giving \(\|B_\Lambda\|^2=O(\kappa^2)+O(N^2)\) against \(N\kappa^2\) throughout \([\Lambda_1+t,3\Lambda_1]\), \(t=2\ln N/\delta\); with Fejér multiplicities \(N-|j|\) the theorem's own conclusion fails there by a factor about \(N\), so \(H\) of order at least \(\pi/g\) is necessary. Both need \(\delta\Lambda_1\gg\ln N\) and \(\kappa\gg N^2\). Record. Searches for Hilbert inequality Montgomery Vaughan and almost periodic mean value average over bandwidth returned no record on these objects; thm:resonance-free-bandwidth is the statement it limits.
  • \(H=C/g\) suffices for equal \(\delta\), by the Montgomery–Vaughan mean value theorem \(\int_{x_0}^{x_0+H}|F|^2=\sum_jm_j^2(H+3\pi\theta_j/g)\), \(|\theta_j|\le1\): for \(H\ge6\pi/g\) the mean is at least half of \(\sum_jm_j^2\), which also improves \(m_*\) to \((\sum_jm_j^2)^{1/2}\). Record. Search for Hilbert inequality Montgomery Vaughan returned no record; the inequality is retained in the density import's Lean project in its weighted Hilbert form, not as a mean value theorem.
  • An elementary improvement within the present proof: ordering the heights, \(|\gamma_j-\gamma_l|\ge g|j-l|\), and \(m_jm_l\le(m_j^2+m_l^2)/2\) bound the off-diagonal total by \(4(1+\ln n)\sum_jm_j^2/g\), so \(H\ge8(1+\ln n)\sum_jm_j^2/(gm_*^2)\) suffices. Record. Same searches, no record; this would refine thm:resonance-free-bandwidth.
  • Unequal \(\delta\) at \(H=C/g\): a sketch only (discard pairs with \((\delta_*-\delta_j)\Lambda_1\) large, then a Hilbert-type inequality for the kernel \(1/(w_j+\bar w_l)\)), not verified by the reviewer. Record. Search for Riesz basis exponentials lower bound returned no record.

Suggestion check. The third suggestion's arithmetic is verified: with the heights ordered, \(\sum_{j\ne l}m_jm_l/|\gamma_j-\gamma_l|\le g^{-1}\sum_{j\ne l}(m_j^2+m_l^2)/(2|j-l|)=g^{-1}\sum_jm_j^2\sum_{l\ne j}1/|j-l|\le2(1+\ln n)g^{-1}\sum_jm_j^2\), and the proof's off-diagonal total is twice this. It is not adopted into the theorem in this entry, which keeps the reviewed statement; adopting it is part of the sub-idea. The other suggestions are not verified and nothing rests on them.

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: the second-moment identity, the real configuration bound and part 1 of the many-pair norm bound, from their full record. One fresh-context medium reviewer checked every step of the proposition and the theorem and found no mathematical error; it ran nothing and compared the quoted numbers with the stored output. Corrections, all incorporated: the proposition's ratio is an upper bound \(O_k(b^{-2k})\) with a constant growing in \(k\), and its conclusion is uniform in \(n\); the theorem's benchmark is the largest pair and not the sum over pairs, and it requires distinct heights; the conditional paragraph needed \(a\ge0\), bounded \(\delta_*\), a bound for the on-line part over the whole interval and the written upper direction; one rounding; notation reused for two objects (renamed). Unused or convenience hypotheses: \(m_j\ge1\) (only positivity is used), positivity of the \(q_i\), and \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\) for the constant only. The reviewer read the closure of the test-vector sub-idea and found its reason incomplete without the factor \((2n)^{3/2}\), now addressed there (reviewer's record). Process audit. Findings, all acted on: the title claimed a nearby bandwidth with no loss for every configuration (retitled); "removes the cluster obstruction", "clean equivalence" and "settled the cluster question" overstated the result (reworded); two benchmarks were used without saying so (now said); the scan never needed the range and its step undersamples resonances (now a paragraph of its own, with the maximizing bandwidths and steps read from the output); the range question was named as undecided while the item was marked Developed (the item is Continuing with that sub-idea); the second sub-idea was called answered in a stronger form when it is a different form (reworded); a count of failed scans had no source and was wrong, thirteen for eleven (corrected, with the archived output named). 11 October 2026 — What ties the seven-eighths source's scales to its boundary: the boundary is \(11/12\) minus a quarter of the prime-slot length, and the nearly tight inequality is its endpoint certificate, whose margin depends on the first stage's input

Source reading with computations from the source's displayed formulas, no new result; three statements read with their proofs in the retained TeX, the row counts behind them not read. Constraint reading. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Question. The missing implication for off-line exclusion is an estimate excluding a zero with \(\Re\rho>1/2\); the only mechanism in the workspace that claims any exclusion is the continuation criterion of the seven-eighths source (criterion reading). This cycle reads how that source's second stage ties its scales to the boundary \(7/8\), to decide from the text whether the stage could start again from its own conclusion with a lower boundary, or which inequality forbids it. At the goal's parameters a positive answer could give, conditionally on the unaudited source, a zero-free half-plane to the left of \(7/8\); a negative one names the estimate that would have to be replaced. The test ends with the inequalities collected and the nearly tight one identified, or with the finding that the text read does not determine it.

New mathematics. None. Relations among the source's parameters are computed from its displayed formulas and checked against both of its stages.

Goal progress. Unchanged. From the text: the boundary of a stage is where the Mellin exponent equals the low estimate's exponent, and with the source's relations among its scales this is \(\sigma_0=11/12-\ell/4\), \(\ell\) the total length of the prime slots (\(\ell=0\) in the first stage, \(1/6\) in the second). The low estimate alone would allow \(\ell\) up to nearly \(1/3\). What holds \(\ell\) at \(1/6\) is the high side: the source's endpoint certificate has a margin of about \(2.3\cdot10^{-4}\) at an interior point, and that margin uses the bound \(5/6\) inherited from the first stage's conclusion. With the bound \(3/4\) that the second stage's own conclusion would supply, the same expression has margin about \(2.3\cdot10^{-3}\). Whether this slack can be turned into a larger \(\ell\) depends on how the certificate's ingredients change with the scales, which the parts read do not show. Bearing. At the goal's parameters this gives nothing yet: it locates the inequality and shows it is sensitive to the input, and it neither proves nor refutes that the stage can be repeated. The cycle was spent on it because it is the one place in the workspace where an exclusion argument has an identifiable adjustable margin.

General statement. thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane is the registered statement this reading concerns; the relations below are stated for the source's whole two-parameter family of scales, on the assumption, marked where used, that its formulas keep their displayed form across that family.

What was read

In the retained TeX of the seven-eighths paper: the proposition "The compensated low estimate" with its proof; the lemma "A high exponent for one row bin" (statement and the defining formula of its exponent); the subsection "The endpoint inequality" with the lemma "Compensated endpoint certificate" and its proof, and the comparison that follows it. Not read: the row-count proposition that supplies the functions \(D_x,P_x\) and the cutoff used in the certificate, the moment estimates, the frequency ranges and the order of choices.

The boundary as a function of the scales

The source's second-stage geometry is \(b=1/8\), \(h=13/16\), \(\ell=1/6\), \(l_x=1+\ell-h=17/48\), \(l_y=l_x+b=23/48\), \(M=l_x+l_y\), \(M+\ell=1\). Its low estimate is \(|I(Z)|\ll Z^{l_x/2+b/12+\epsilon}\), and it records that the Mellin exponent of the principal signal is \(C(s)=l_x/2+s-1+h/6\). The boundary \(\sigma_0\) is where the two agree, \(C(\sigma_0)=l_x/2+b/12\): \[\sigma_0=1-{h\over6}+{b\over12}.\] The relations \(l_x=1+\ell-h\), \(l_y=l_x+b\), \(2l_x+b+\ell=1\) give \(l_x=(1-\ell-b)/2\), \(l_y=(1-\ell+b)/2\), \(h=1/2+3\ell/2+b/2\), and therefore \[\sigma_0={11\over12}-{\ell\over4},\] independent of \(b\). Check on the second stage: \(11/12-1/24=7/8\). Check on the first stage, which has both scales \(Z^{1/2}\) and no slots: \(\ell=b=0\), \(h=1/2\), \(l_x=1/2\) give \(C(s)=s-2/3\) and \(\sigma_0=11/12\), the source's values. So in this family the boundary is lowered only by lengthening the prime slots; \(b\) raises \(h\) without moving the boundary. That the two displayed formulas hold with these symbols for other values of \((\ell,b)\) is an extrapolation from the two stages; the source states them for its fixed geometry.

The low side

In the proof of the low estimate the Gram bound is used with \(P_a=Z^{b}\) up to constants and needs \(P_a^2/Y'\ll P_a^{1/6}\), which the source obtains from \(l_y-d-11b/6\ge1/12\) for every \(d\le\ell\). In the family this condition is \(l_y-\ell-11b/6>0\), that is \[{3\ell\over2}+{4b\over3}<{1\over2}.\] At the source's values the left side is \(5/12\), with the slack \(1/12\) the source displays. The other length conditions there (\(l_x-d\) and \(l_y-d\) bounded below) are weaker. So the low side alone admits every \(\ell<1/3\) with small \(b\), a boundary down to \(5/6\), and no further within this family: below \(5/6\) the low estimate itself, of the form square root of \(X'\) times \(P_a^{1/12}\), would have to change.

The high side and the certificate

The exponent of one row bin, relative to \(Z^{C(7/8)}\), is defined in the source by \[E(d)=a-{7\over8}+h\Bigl({17\over50}-{1\over6}\Bigr)-al_y-(1-a)\ell-\Bigl({\delta\over2}-q\Bigr)\ell+d\Bigl(R+{\delta\over2}-{17\over50}\Bigr),\] with \(\delta=2a-1\), \(q\in[0,\delta/2]\) and \(R\) the exponent of the row count; it then writes \(E(d)=C_0+\tfrac23\delta+q/6-h(1-R)+(d-h)(R+\delta/2-17/50)\) with \(C_0=-1/48\). I checked that the second form follows from the first with the geometry's numbers. Replacing \(7/8\) by \(\sigma_0\) and using the family's relations, the first form at \(d=h\) becomes \[E(h)=-{3\over4}-{\ell\over4}-{b\over3}+hR+\Bigl({1\over2}+\ell\Bigr)\delta+q\ell,\] which reproduces \(-5/6+hR+\tfrac23\delta+q/6\) at the source's values; this general form is again an extrapolation. At the upper endpoint the source takes \(R=1-\delta\) and \(q\le\delta/2\) and gets \(E(h)\le-1/48-\delta/16\); in the family this reads \(E(h)\le-1/4+5\ell/4+b/6-\delta b/2\).

The binding case is not that endpoint but the interior range, treated by the certificate. With \(\alpha=5/6\), \(y=1/2-x\in[0,1/2]\), \(D_x=(37+34y)/18\), \(P_x=(7+18y+8y^2)/9\) and \(\mathcal J=(\alpha-\delta)D_x+\delta P_x\), the source proves \[-E_*={1+(3+8y)\delta\over48}-{13\over32}{(\alpha-\delta)\delta P_x\over\mathcal J}\ \ge\ {49\over440640}\] for \(0\le\delta\le5/6\), by an explicit sum-of-squares identity, and then uses \(E_{\mathrm{actual}}(h)-\Delta\le-49/440640-(1-h/4)\Delta+\epsilon_{\mathrm{total}}\). The number \(5/6\) is the bound \(\kappa=2\beta_*-1\le5/6\) that the second stage takes from the first stage's conclusion \(\beta_*\le11/12\).

Evaluation. The script endpoint_margin.py evaluates the displayed expression in exact rationals on the grid \(\delta=\alpha i/400\), \(y=k/200\). With \(\alpha=5/6\) it confirms the source's polynomial identity at every grid point and finds the smallest value \(2.28\cdot10^{-4}\) at \(\delta=0.3875\), \(y=0\), above the source's floor \(1.11\cdot10^{-4}\) (the proof's square vanishes at \(\delta=79/204\approx0.387\) for \(y=0\), which is where the grid minimum sits). With \(\alpha\) replaced by \(3/4\), the value of \(2\beta-1\) at \(\beta=7/8\), and \(D_x,P_x\) left unchanged, the smallest value is \(2.29\cdot10^{-3}\) at \(\delta=0.349\), \(y=0\) (output). The first number is a check of my reading of the formula against the source's own bound; the second is a what-if on one formula and not a statement of the source, since \(D_x\), \(P_x\) and the cutoff come from a proposition I did not read and may themselves depend on \(\alpha\) and on the scales.

What this decides and what it leaves

From the text: the second stage's boundary is fixed by \(\ell=1/6\); the low side would allow more; the high side's certificate is nearly tight, with relative room of about \(2\cdot10^{-4}\) in an exponent, at an interior point with the main-slot mean at its largest. That is the inequality that forbids a lower boundary with the first stage's input. The source does not discuss repeating the stage. The evaluation shows the displayed margin grows tenfold when the input \(5/6\) is replaced by \(3/4\), so the question whether a third application with a slightly larger \(\ell\) closes is a real one, and its answer needs the general form of the row counts as functions of \((\ell,b,\alpha)\). If it closed, the iteration would have a fixed point where the margin vanishes with \(\alpha=2\sigma_0-1=5/6-\ell/2\); by the low side that fixed point cannot be below \(5/6\) in this family, so even the most favourable outcome moves the boundary by at most \(1/24\) and stays far from \(1/2\).

Consequence for the route. Both mechanisms now have a stated limit within their present form. The Gram route needs a premise equivalent in the model to the goal. The continuation criterion has an adjustable margin, but its probe's low estimate bounds the family's boundary below by \(5/6\). Reaching \(1/2\) by the criterion would need a probe whose low exponent \(C(\sigma_0)+\omega\) and high saving both hold at boundaries near \(1/2\), which is a different construction and not a tuning of this one. These are readings of the two imports and of the model, to be tested by the route review that follows this entry.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the source's row-count proposition and write \(D_x\), \(P_x\), the cutoff and the certificate as functions of \((\ell,b,\alpha)\); then decide whether \(\ell>1/6\) with \(\alpha=3/4\) keeps \(-E_*>0\) together with \(3\ell/2+4b/3<1/2\), and compute the fixed point of the iteration if it does.

Verification gate. No new lemma, theorem or encoding is given a status and no claim is registered, so no reviewer was used in this cycle; the route review that follows uses its reviewer for the correctness of this entry's derived relations as well. Checks made: the relations \(\sigma_0=1-h/6+b/12=11/12-\ell/4\) and the low-side condition reproduce the source's numbers at both stages; the general form of \(E(h)\) reproduces the source's second form and its endpoint value; the evaluation script confirms the source's polynomial identity and floor in exact arithmetic. Three steps are extrapolations from the source's fixed geometry and are marked as such: the two exponent formulas for general scales, the general form of \(E\), and the what-if with \(\alpha=3/4\).

Process assessment. The constraint system turned out to be readable from three statements because the source displays its exponents as formulas in the scales; the previous entry could have gone this far in the same cycle had it opened the low estimate's proof. Four research entries in a row now declare no bearing on the goal, which the start guidance flags; this one says in its Question what it could give. Prevention. No mistake of this cycle to prevent. For the run of entries without bearing the committed mechanism is the scheduled route review, which is the next entry, with the start guidance's count; it must decide whether either mechanism deserves further cycles.

11 October 2026 — Route review of the first six entries: two mechanisms, each a reformulation without an estimate at boundaries below seven-eighths; the next test is the bootstrap map of the seven-eighths source

Route review; assessment of the exclusion line only, no new result. Exclusion route review. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

New mathematics. None registered. One short argument is given as the test of a lead: in the complete-grid model a configuration with an off-axis pair has a negative eigenvalue.

Goal progress. Unchanged for off-line exclusion, as in all six entries. No statement about a zero of \(\zeta\) was proved or made conditional on a premise that is available.

General statement. The line's general claim rests on thm:resonance-free-bandwidth and lem:real-configuration-bound: in the flat-window complete-grid model, a second-moment bound for the Gram form over a long range of bandwidths holds when all points are real and, for configurations at distinct heights, bounds the largest distance of a point from the line. The second mechanism rests on the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its continuation criterion.

What the six entries did. Three developed a model of the density proof's Gram form (pair atom spectrum, many-pair bound, resonance-free bandwidth), with eight registered statements, each reviewed in its cycle (lem:pair-atom-spectrum, prop:solitary-amplification, prop:two-pair-cancellation, lem:gram-second-moment-identity, lem:real-configuration-bound, prop:many-pair-norm-bound, prop:subset-sum-resonance, thm:resonance-free-bandwidth). Three were readings of the two imports (bandwidth reading, criterion reading, constraint reading), without reviewer until this review.

What the goal needs. An estimate that excludes one hypothetical zero with \(1/2<\Re\rho\le7/8\) (or \(\le1\), if the unaudited import is not used), at every height. Density statements and statements about proportions do not supply it.

Falsification attempt. Two of the line's own conclusions were attacked. (1) "The Gram premise beyond bandwidth \(\log T\) is a consequence of the goal, so the route gives nothing": if the model let an off-axis pair hide from every one-sided test, the route would be even weaker than claimed; the first lead's test below shows the opposite, an off-axis pair always gives a negative eigenvalue on the complete grid, so the model's sign information is complete and the claim stands as stated, no stronger. (2) "The continuation probe's family is bounded below by \(5/6\)" (constraint reading): this does not survive as worded, in two ways. The condition \(3\ell/2+4b/3\le1/2\) is the regime in which the source's low estimate is stated; beyond it the row sum of that proof degenerates and what the family gives is unknown, not excluded. And the reading overlooked a consequence of its own general endpoint form, found by the reviewer: at \(\delta\to0\) the certificate's correction term vanishes and the condition is \(1/4-5\ell/4-b/6>0\), that is \(\ell<1/5-2b/15\), a boundary above \(13/15+b/30\). So under the reading's extrapolations it is the high side, not the low side, that bounds the family, and the most a repetition of the stage could gain is under \(1/120\), not \(1/24\). The living summary is corrected accordingly.

Corrections to the three readings, from stage two. They were committed without reviewer; the reviewer has now checked them against the entries and, for the third, against four ranges of the source. (a) Bandwidth reading: its bookkeeping is a fair computation with its hypothetical marked, but its title's "the single place" and the phrase "used in exactly one essential way" claim more than a reading of statements without proofs supports; it is the one place found. Its follow-up Developed rests on statements and docstrings, with proofs and the remaining error terms unread, and is to be read with that limit. (b) Criterion reading: the restatement and proof steps check; one step should be said, that the deleted Euler factors vanish only on \(\Re s=0\), so a zero of \(L\) is a zero of \(L^{\mathcal S}\); "close zeros cannot cancel" is right because the bounds are used as \(Z\to\infty\). (c) Constraint reading: the algebra is verified, including the family relations, the boundary formula, the low-side condition, the general \(E(h)\) and the grid minima. Its status line says three statements were read with their proofs, while the high-exponent lemma was read as statement and formula only. Its marked extrapolations are not the only ones: it also treats \(M+\ell=1\) as a structural constraint where the source displays fixed numbers, assumes that the constants \(11/6,1/6,1/12,17/50\) and the forms of the Gram bound and of \(R=1-\delta\) do not depend on the scales, and assumes that the \(7/8\) in \(E\) is the boundary. That \(\alpha=5/6\) is the bound on \(2\beta_*-1\) was read in the opening of the source's second part, outside the ranges the reviewer saw.

Assessment of this line. The line has produced an exact description of what second moments of the Gram form say about off-line points in a model, and a reading of where each import stops. It has produced no mechanism. The model results are sound as mathematics and their bearing on the goal is nil while the premise is unavailable; the last four entries (the two source readings, resonance-free bandwidth and the constraint reading) each declared no bearing, correctly. The readings found one adjustable quantity in the workspace: the margin of the seven-eighths source's endpoint certificate, which depends on the first stage's input. Evidence that the line can reach the goal: none. Evidence that it can move the imported boundary conditionally: a tenfold change of one margin under a what-if that leaves the row counts unexamined, which is weak. The reviewer, given only the imports, named the same two mechanisms and the same first question; since it was handed this notebook's own six inputs, that is agreement on what those inputs offer and not independent corroboration.

Review. The stand-in review after the fifth entry raised five objections. (1) A framework commit was made after a suite run of 16.0 s with three browser cases over their limit, and the cause was attributed to other sessions without measurement: accepted; the runner's CPU line does not separate other sessions' jobs in the shared slice from the suite's own, so the attribution was unsupported; a build item to find the cause is queued. (2) An overstatement caught only by the reviewer was reported in chat without a Prevention paragraph: accepted for the chat report; the entry itself has the paragraph. (3) The two reading entries were committed without reviewer although they steer the route: accepted; this review's stage two gives them and the third reading to the reviewer, and their conclusions are restated below only as corrected. (4) A script error was fixed without saying whether earlier output was affected: the failing run stopped before writing its output file, so no recorded number came from it; the scripts are one-off computations kept with their results under the rule for such jobs, not framework tools. (5) "What is missing is only the premise" overstated the fifth entry's result: accepted; the entry as committed says the premise is close to a restatement of the goal.

Obstacle

The line is stuck on one thing, stated twice. There is no estimate, in either mechanism, that holds at a boundary below \(7/8\).

In the Gram mechanism the needed input is a bound for the second moment of the zero sum \(\sum_\rho x^{\rho-1/2}\) for \(x\) up to \(T^\theta\), \(\theta>1\), better by a power than what the Montgomery–Vaughan inequality gives for the off-diagonal prime terms. At the Montgomery–Vaughan strength, in the model and if the source's remaining estimates extend past \(\theta=1\), the bound is \(T^{\theta}\) and constrains no zero at any bandwidth; a saving \(T^{-\varepsilon}\) gives \(\delta\le1/2-\varepsilon/(2\theta)\). In the model the full saving follows from the goal.

In the continuation mechanism the needed input is a probe \(J\) with a low bound \(Z^{C(\sigma_0)+\omega}\) and a high saving relative to \(Z^{C(\beta_*)}\) at a boundary \(\sigma_0<7/8\). As the independent reviewer notes, if \(\beta_*\le\sigma_0\) the Mellin integral itself is such a probe, so the criterion too is a reformulation; the content is a probe built without knowing the zeros. The source builds one from cubic theta sums, with boundary \(11/12-\ell/4\) in its family and a nearly tight certificate at \(\ell=1/6\).

So the obstacle is: a power saving that is not a consequence of already knowing where the zeros are, either in an off-diagonal prime sum beyond the diagonal range or in a probe's two estimates below \(7/8\).

Outside leads

  • Weil's positivity criterion (Weil 1952; Bombieri, Remarks on Weil's quadratic functional I, Theorem 1, read at statement level when the density result was imported). Target: off-line exclusion through the sign of the Gram form instead of its norm. In the model, let \(Z\) have off-axis pairs \(z_j,\bar z_j\) and real points; the real vectors \(v_\gamma\), \(x_j=\Re v_{z_j}\), \(y_j=\Im v_{z_j}\) are linearly independent, because a real relation among them is a complex relation among the functions \(\varphi(u)e^{-iuw}\) for distinct \(w\), which are independent. So there is \(u\) orthogonal to all \(v_\gamma\), all \(x_j\) and all \(y_j\) except \(y_1\), with \(u\cdot y_1\ne0\), and by the pair atom spectrum \(u^{\mathsf T}G_Zu=-m_1(\kappa_1-1)(u\cdot e_{y_1})^2<0\). Hence on the complete grid, which is infinite (all \(k\in\mathbb Z\), where the vectors correspond isometrically to the functions on the interval), \(G_Z\succeq0\) exactly when no point of the finite configuration is off the axis, with no exception for clusters. This is an unregistered sketch, not a claim of this notebook. On the import's cut grid the dimension is below the number of zeros in the window at bandwidth \(\log T\), the on-line atoms can fill the space, and no sign follows; the sketch needs more grid points than points, which means bandwidth beyond \(\log T\), the unavailable range. It would also break at the prime side: positivity of the form for long test functions is the statement to be proved, and only the trace and the second moment are controlled. Answers. It replaces the two-sided second moment, where close pairs cancel and the trivial bound sits exactly at \(\delta=1/2\), by a one-sided condition that a single off-line zero violates; the obstacle would be resolved by a lower bound for the smallest eigenvalue on the prime side at one bandwidth long enough to resolve the zero. The sketch is qualitative: for clusters \(u\cdot e_{y_1}\) can be exponentially small, so it gives no margin. Test. Passed as a sketch on the infinite grid; the reviewer, who did not have the model's conventions before it, checked it only conditionally and asked for the grid hypothesis now stated.
  • The Hermite–Biehler theorem and structure functions of de Branges type, \(E=\xi+\xi'\) in a suitable normalization (sources not read in this cycle; the theorem is used only in its polynomial form, which the test checks numerically). Target: off-line exclusion as a statement about one function on the real line. For a real polynomial \(p\), all zeros of \(p+ip'\) lie in the open lower half-plane exactly when the roots of \(p\) are real and simple; an off-axis pair puts a zero of \(p+ip'\) in the closed upper half-plane. The density import also bounds the zeros of \(\xi'\) on the line. It would break where the independent reviewer says: information about \(\xi'\) alone runs in the wrong direction, and the monotone-phase condition for \(E\) is again equivalent to the goal. Answers. It would turn the obstacle into a pointwise sign or monotonicity statement for the argument of \(E\) on the line, where a power saving is not what is asked; whether any such statement is reachable from the explicit formula is the open part. Test. Passed: for 200 random real-rooted polynomials of degree 3 to 8 all zeros of \(p+ip'\) are in the lower half-plane, and for the 200 obtained by moving two roots to a conjugate pair some zero is not (output).
  • Li's criterion (Li 1997; Bombieri–Lagarias 1999; not read in this cycle, used only through the elementary fact tested). Target: off-line exclusion as positivity of the numbers \(\lambda_n=\sum_\rho(1-(1-1/\rho)^n)\). For \(\Re\rho\ne1/2\) one of \(\rho\), \(1-\bar\rho\) has \(|1-1/\rho|>1\), since \(|1-1/\rho|^2=|\rho-1|^2/|\rho|^2\) exceeds one exactly when \(\Re\rho<1/2\); so an off-line pair contributes a term growing geometrically in \(n\), the discrete analogue of \(\kappa\approx e^{\delta\Lambda}\); since \(|1-1/\rho|-1\) is about \(\delta/\gamma^2\) at height \(\gamma\), such a zero shows only at \(n\) of order \(\gamma^2/\delta\). It would break at the arithmetic side, which involves prime sums whose length grows with \(n\), and at the same resonance among close zeros unless the sign condition is used over a range of \(n\). Answers. It gives the one-sided form of the obstacle with a discrete parameter; a lower bound for \(\lambda_n\) from the arithmetic side for all \(n\) would exclude every off-line zero. Test. Passed: the modulus computation above, an elementary fact that could not have failed; the pick is by the rule (three passed leads), not by strength.
  • Deligne's use of tensor powers (Weil I; the Rankin–Selberg squaring argument), which turns a weak uniform bound over a family closed under tensor powers into the optimal one. Target: sharpening \(7/8\) to \(1/2\) from uniformity over the Hecke family of the import. It breaks at once: the bound there is on Frobenius eigenvalues, which multiply under tensor products, while zeros of \(L\)-functions have no such multiplicative structure; in the model nothing relates the points of two configurations to those of a product. Answers. It would have removed the need for any saving beyond the imported one, by amplification. Test. Falsified: no operation on configurations in the model multiplies the quantities \(e^{\delta\Lambda}\) of two families into those of a third.
  • Large-value estimates for Dirichlet polynomials (Guth–Maynard, cited in the seven-eighths source's introduction as improving zero-density bounds; read only as that citation). Target: the aggregate form of the second moment at bandwidth \(\log T\). It breaks for this goal by its form: a density bound limits the number of off-line zeros and excludes none. Answers. It would bound how many clusters there can be, which the obstacle does not ask. Test. Falsified as an answer to the obstacle: by the Open statement, sparse exceptions must be excluded, and no density estimate does that.

Absurd bridges

  • Super-resolution of point sources (Candès and Fernandez-Granda; Batenkov, Goldman and Yomdin on near-colliding nodes; not read in this cycle). Translation: the points \(z_j\) are sources with complex frequencies, the bandwidth \(\Lambda\) is the cutoff, the second moment is one measurement; that theory states how the stability of recovering a cluster of \(n\) nodes degrades as a power of the ratio of cutoff resolution to node spacing. A theorem there would give the exact loss for clusters at one bandwidth and the least range of bandwidths that removes it, the open sub-idea of the queue's head. Smallest test: compare the model's proved two-point loss with a power law in the spacing over resolution. Answers. It answers only the cluster half of the obstacle, the dependence on bandwidth range; it does not supply a saving. Test. Passed as a consistency check: prop:two-pair-cancellation loses the factor \(3\pi^2/(4b^2)\), a power of \(b=\delta\Lambda\), and prop:subset-sum-resonance the power \(b^{-2k}\) for \(2^k\) nodes, the shape such a law has. This comparison could not have failed and the item, by its own answer, does not address the saving; it is picked by the rule as the only passed bridge.
  • Harmonic inversion of short signals in quantum chemistry (filter diagonalization, Wall–Neuhauser, Mandelshtam–Taylor; not read). Translation: \(S(s)=\sum_wm_we^{-isw}\) is a signal whose complex frequencies are the points; these methods extract frequencies from a time span far below the Fourier resolution by using the signal's exponential structure. A method that did so stably would resolve a cluster at one bandwidth. Smallest test: recover the \(\delta_j\) of a cluster by the matrix-pencil method from \(4n\) samples on a short span. Answers. It would have removed the need to vary the bandwidth. Test. Falsified: with spans 3.5 to 7.5 against Fourier resolutions 21 to 36, the largest error in \(\delta\) is \(6\cdot10^{-9}\), \(8\cdot10^{-4}\), \(5\cdot10^{-2}\) for \(n=2,3,4\) in double precision without noise, and \(9\cdot10^{-4}\), \(9\cdot10^{-2}\), \(2\cdot10^{-2}\) for \(n=2,3,4\) with relative noise \(10^{-9}\) (output): from three pairs on the recovery fails at the level of \(\delta\) itself even without noise; the noisy value at \(n=4\) is below that at \(n=3\), so the samples show failure for clusters and not a monotone law.
  • The Lee–Yang circle theorem, by way of spin-chain models whose partition functions are quotients of zeta values (Knauf's number-theoretical spin chain; not read). Translation: realize a function with the zeros of \(\zeta\) as a partition function of a ferromagnetic system, where zeros are confined to a line by the sign of the couplings. A theorem there would give off-line exclusion from a positivity of interactions. Smallest test: decide from the source whether the zeros of \(\zeta\) appear in such a model as zeros in the field variable, where the circle theorem applies, or in the temperature variable, where it does not. Answers. It would supply a structural reason for all zeros to lie on a line that is not a power saving. Test. Not run: the test is to read the source, which was not done in this cycle. The item is dropped: the reviewer recalls, also without reading, that in that chain the zeta quotient is a function of the inverse temperature, the variable in which the circle theorem does not apply.

Follow-ups

  • Read the source's row-count proposition and write \(D_x\), \(P_x\), the cutoff and the certificate as functions of \((\ell,b,\alpha)\); decide whether a third application closes and compute the fixed point. This is the decisive test below. Follow-up. Promoted: it is the one place where an exclusion argument in the workspace has an adjustable margin, and the independent reviewer names the same question first.

The earlier check listed by the criterion reading (collect the inequalities tying the scales to the boundary) was carried out by the constraint reading and is not promoted.

Architecture review

Necessary target. The weakest endpoint that suffices is: for every \(\sigma_0>1/2\), no zero of \(\zeta\) with \(\Re\rho>\sigma_0\), at every height; equivalently the exclusion of each single hypothetical zero. No uniformity over a family, no simplicity, no density and no asymptotic formula is required by the goal. Downstream. Evaluated through the proved statements: by thm:resonance-free-bandwidth with lem:real-configuration-bound, in the flat-window complete-grid model the target is implied by a second-moment bound with exponent tending to zero over a long range of bandwidths, and implies such bounds at every bandwidth; the consequence is the goal's analogue for finite configurations at distinct heights, weaker than the goal by the transfer from the model to the import's cut and smoothed form, which is unproved. Through the import thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane and its criterion the target at \(\sigma_0\) is implied by a probe with the two estimates at \(\sigma_0\); the available consequence is \(\sigma_0=7/8\), weaker than the goal and unaudited.

Extra requirements. The Gram mechanism asks for a two-sided norm bound where a one-sided sign suffices (first lead), for all bandwidths in a range where one long bandwidth suffices in the sign form, and for an asymptotic where an upper bound with a power saving suffices. The continuation mechanism asks for margins uniform over the whole Hecke family and a supremum over it, where the goal concerns \(\zeta\) alone; the source needs the family because its Poisson side produces twists, so this demand is forced by its construction and not by the criterion. Its second stage demands the first stage's conclusion as input, which is a strength: the requirement is relative to the current supremum and not absolute.

Independent alternative. The reviewer, given only the goal and six available results with the route withheld, found no architecture avoiding a link equivalent to the goal or to a zero-free strip, and ranked four candidates: descending the criterion's boundary, spectral positivity of the Gram form, pushing the density to one (closed: proportions cannot exclude one zero) and the zeros of \(\xi'\) (closed: wrong direction). The first two are the two mechanisms of this line; its first question is the bootstrap map (stage-one record). It also cautions that the seven-eighths half-plane and the real-zero gap are not accepted theorems in the literature it knows, so every use is conditional on an unaudited import. No materially different architecture is recorded, and none is invented here.

Rank normalization. The dimension counts of the density proof (rank against trace) are not used by this line, whose quantities are an eigenvalue, a norm and an exponent. The question behind the rule does apply to the continuation mechanism: its second stage measures its saving relative to the current supremum \(\beta_*\), not absolutely, and whether that relative contraction can be repeated is exactly the decisive test.

Decision. Test the continuation mechanism's bootstrap first, because it is the only place with an adjustable margin and a contraction relative to the current bound. The reason supports one bounded cycle and no more: no outcome reaches \(1/2\), and sharper constants for an unaudited import are off the critical path, so the test is confined to the fixed point's location and the question of what forces \(b\), and does not continue into tuning. Keep the Gram model as a recorded reformulation; of its open items only the sign form (first lead) could change what the route needs, and it is queued. The cluster sub-idea stays with the queue's head.

Decisive test. Write the second stage of the seven-eighths source as a map from an assumed bound \(\beta_{\mathrm{old}}\) (through \(\alpha=2\beta_{\mathrm{old}}-1\)) and scales \((\ell,b)\) to a boundary \(11/12-\ell/4\), using the general form of its row counts, and determine the least boundary for which its certificate and low-side condition hold when \(\beta_{\mathrm{old}}\) is that boundary. Outcomes: a fixed point at \(7/8\) closes the mechanism at that exponent in this family and names the row counts as the estimate to replace; a fixed point strictly below \(7/8\) gives a conditional improvement of the import and shows which of the certificate and the low estimate binds there; a failure to write the map from the text (row counts not given as functions of the scales) leaves the question open and says the source must be audited in full first. None of these reaches \(1/2\); each changes which estimate the route must replace. Power. Recorded limits on the test's object: under the constraint reading's extrapolations the endpoint form at \(\delta\to0\) requires \(\ell<1/5-2b/15\), so no boundary below \(13/15+b/30\) can come out and the largest possible gain is under \(1/120\); the low estimate is stated only for \(3\ell/2+4b/3\le1/2\); a first-order estimate by the reviewer with the row-count functions held fixed puts the fixed point near \(7/8-4.4\cdot10^{-4}\) (unverified). Both the low side and the endpoint constant prefer a smaller \(b\) than the source's \(1/8\), so what forces \(b\) is to be read first. The certificate's margin is \(2.3\cdot10^{-4}\) at the source's scales with \(\alpha=5/6\) and \(2.3\cdot10^{-3}\) with \(\alpha=3/4\) when the row-count functions are held fixed, so a change of \(\ell\) of order \(10^{-3}\) to \(10^{-2}\) is the size at stake; the outcomes differ in whether the margin survives the change of the row-count functions with \(\ell\), which the text decides or does not. It is a reading and an exact rational computation, with no size limit. Record search. 11 October 2026 — The seven-eighths source's second stage needs the earlier bound as a threshold and is not improved by a better one; under stated extrapolations its scales are within \(5\cdot10^{-5}\) of the optimum of their family: correction of the constraint reading and outcome of the review's test

Source reading with a correction and a computation under stated extrapolations, no new result; the row-count proposition read with its proof in the retained TeX. Family optimum. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Chosen. This entry takes the promoted check on the source's row counts ahead of the queue's head, because the route review made it the decisive test for off-line exclusion and the next research entry must perform it. At the goal's parameters it could have given, conditionally on the unaudited source, a zero-free boundary below \(7/8\); the review bounded that gain by \(1/120\) in advance and confined the test to one cycle.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding a zero with \(\Re\rho>1/2\). The test: write the source's second stage as a map from an assumed bound \(\beta_{\mathrm{old}}\) and scales \((\ell,b)\) to a boundary, and find its fixed point; read first what forces \(b\). It ends with a fixed point at \(7/8\), one below it, or the finding that the text does not give the row counts as functions of the scales.

New mathematics. No result is obtained. The entry contains a reading of the source's row counts, the correction of an earlier reading, and a numerical optimum of the source's system of inequalities over its two scales, valid only under the extrapolations listed with it.

Goal progress. Unchanged. Bearing. At the goal's parameters this gives nothing: under stated extrapolations it finds no room for a lower boundary by repeating the source's second stage or by retuning its two scales. The cycle was spent on it because the review named it as the one adjustable margin in the workspace.

General statement. thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane is the registered statement concerned; the computation below ranges over the source's two-parameter family of scales, under extrapolations listed with it.

Architecture test. Outcome of the test set by the route review: essentially its first outcome. (1) The second stage needs the earlier bound as a threshold: its bins have \(\delta\le\kappa=2\beta_*-1\le\alpha=5/6\), which is \(\beta_*\le11/12\), and the long count, the positivity of \(\mathcal J\) and the certificate's range all use \(\delta\le\alpha\). A better earlier bound does not improve it: the certificate's worst case is at \(\delta\approx0.39\), in the interior, and in the computation below every maximizing \(\delta\) is at most \(0.52\), below \(3/4\), so shrinking the range of \(\delta\) changes nothing. The boundary does feed back into the estimates, through \(\kappa_0=2\sigma_0-1\) in one capacity; the computation solves that feedback self-consistently, and it is reached in one step from \(11/12\), not by iteration. (2) Under the extrapolations below, in the two-parameter family at \(d=h\), the largest admissible slot length is \(\ell=0.166838\) at \(b=0.1225\), against the source's \(\ell=1/6=0.166667\), \(b=1/8\): a boundary of \(0.874957\), below \(7/8\) by \(4.3\cdot10^{-5}\), where the margin is zero. The source's scales are within that distance of the family's optimum. Whether the difference is a real conditional improvement or an artefact of the extrapolated forms is not claimed either way. (3) The binding inequality is the endpoint certificate at \(\delta\approx0.39\) with the main-slot mean at its largest (\(x=1/2\)). Improving the row counts alone cannot gain much: with the ideal count \(R=1-\delta\) the certificate reduces to \(\ell<1/5-2b/15\), so even perfect row counts move the boundary by at most \(1/120\) in this family; the remaining part of the exponent comes from the principal terms (the Mellin exponent, the low estimate, the slot coefficients).

Correction of the constraint reading

The constraint reading said that the number \(5/6\) in the endpoint certificate is the bound \(\kappa=2\beta_*-1\le5/6\) inherited from the first stage, and evaluated the certificate with \(3/4\) in its place. That is wrong. The source's subsection on selected prime slots sets \(\alpha:=5/6\) and says: the parameter \(\kappa\) remains dynamic, and \(\alpha\) is the fixed slope from sixth-power amplification. The two numbers coincide at the upper end of the second stage's range and are different objects: \(\alpha\) comes from the exponent \((1+5r)/6\) of the amplified inverse moment and does not change with the bound on the zeros. Withdrawn from that entry: the title's clause that the certificate's margin depends on the first stage's input; in Goal progress, that the margin uses the bound inherited from the first stage and is sensitive to the input, with the figure \(2.3\cdot10^{-3}\); in its Evaluation paragraph, the what-if with \(\alpha=3/4\); in "What this decides", that the certificate forbids a lower boundary with the first stage's input, and the fixed-point sentence with \(\alpha=2\sigma_0-1=5/6-\ell/2\) (the quantity \(5/6-\ell/2\) is \(\kappa_0\), which enters through a capacity, not \(\alpha\)); in "Consequence for the route", that the criterion has an adjustable margin; and the sub-idea's wording with \((\ell,b,\alpha)\) and \(\alpha=3/4\). The route review's Power paragraph and its reviewer's first-order estimate of a fixed point near \(7/8-4.4\cdot10^{-4}\), which used that reading, are superseded by the computation below. That entry's other relations (boundary formula, low-side condition, general form of \(E\)) stand as checked in the review.

How the bound on the zeros enters, from the text

Read in the subsection "Selected prime slots and the row counts", from its opening through the proposition "Row counts from the witnesses", its proof and the remark after it. The plain moment estimate has zero-loss capacity \(z_P(m)=(1-2m)/(6\kappa)\) with the dynamic \(\kappa=3/4+2\Delta\); the proof replaces it by its value at \(\Delta=0\), \(2(1-2m)/9\), and bounds the cost of the replacement by \(\Delta/4\) in the count exponent. The inverse estimate has capacity \(z_M(r)=(1-r)/2\) and, without selected primes, the amplified exponent \(e(r)=\max\{1,(1+5r)/6\}\). With \(x=q/\delta\) the two counts are \(A_I(r)=1-\delta\{x+(1-x)r\}\) and \(S_t(r)=1-\delta\{4x/9+(2-8x/9)(t-r)\}\); they cross at \(r_*(t)\), their weighted average is \(R_{\mathrm{short}}(t)=1-\delta+\delta P_x(3/2-t)/D_x\) with \(D_x=3-17x/9\), \(P_x=(2-8x/9)(1-x)\), and the long count is \(L(t)=1-\delta+(\alpha-\delta)(t-1)\). The count is \(U^{\max\{R_{\mathrm{short}},L\}+\Delta/4+\epsilon}\). The bound on the zeros enters in two ways: as the threshold \(\delta\le\kappa\le\alpha\), and through \(\kappa\) in the plain capacity at the cost \(\Delta/4\), which the final comparison \(E_{\mathrm{actual}}(h)-\Delta\le-49/440640-(1-h/4)\Delta+\epsilon\) absorbs for every \(\Delta>0\) in the range.

The numbers \(4/9\), \(8/9\), \(17/9\) are \(c\), \(2c\), \(1+2c\) with \(c=1/(3\kappa_0)\) at \(\kappa_0=3/4\), the value of \(2\sigma_0-1\) at the boundary \(\sigma_0=7/8\): the plain exponent is \(1-2\delta m-2q(1-2m)/(6\kappa_0)=1-\delta\{cx+(2-2cx)m\}\) with \(m\ge t-r\). For a general boundary in the family this gives \(D_x=3-(1+2c)x\), \(P_x=(2-2cx)(1-x)\), and the weighted average keeps the form above with the same \(3/2\) for every \(c\), since \(D_x-x(2-2cx)-cx(1-x)=3(1-x)(1-cx)=\tfrac32P_x\).

The optimum of the family

Extrapolations. The computation assumes, beyond the text: (i) that the family relations of the constraint reading hold for all \((\ell,b)\); the family has two parameters only because \(M+\ell=1\) and \(l_y=l_x+b\) are imposed; (ii) that the quantity which must be negative for \(0\le\delta\le5/6\), \(0\le x\le1/2\) is \(E_*=-3/4-\ell/4-b/3+hR_*+(1/2+\ell)\delta+x\delta\ell\) with \(R_*=1-\delta+(\alpha-\delta)\delta P_x/(2\mathcal J)\), \(\mathcal J=(\alpha-\delta)D_x+\delta P_x\), \(c=1/(3\kappa_0)\), \(\kappa_0=5/6-\ell/2\); only \(d=h\) is evaluated, and the source's treatment of other \(d\) is not examined; (iii) that the inverse capacity \((1-r)/2\), the form of \(A_I\), the amplified exponent \(e(r)\) and the slope \(\alpha\) do not depend on the scales or on \(\kappa_0\); the lemmas behind them were not read; (iv) that the constants of the low estimate and the contour value \(17/50\) (which cancels at \(d=h\)) do not depend on the scales, that the low side may be imposed without the source's fixed margin, and that the \(\Delta/4\) cost and its absorption generalize; (v) that the proposition's internal constants, which do change with \(c\) (the bounds \(23/37\) and \(1/3\), the margin \(9/37\), and the supply threshold, which is \((2-c)/(2(5-2c))\) and equals \(7/37\) only at \(c=4/9\)), stay admissible; (vi) that no other constraint of the source binds first (frequency ranges, the order of choices, the detector's conditions). The extrapolated forms could err in either direction; only the unread constraints are one-sided. The outcome is a statement about this system of inequalities, not about the source's method.

Computation and its checks. The script family_optimum.py first checks, in exact rationals on a grid of \(201\times51\) points, that the general \(E_*\) at \((\ell,b)=(1/6,1/8)\) equals the source's closed form \(-(1+(3+8y)\delta)/48+\tfrac{13}{32}(5/6-\delta)\delta P_x/\mathcal J\) and stays below \(-49/440640\), and that the floating-point function used afterwards agrees with it there. This tests the form at the source's point only; the dependence on \((\ell,b)\) rests on the derivation above and in the constraint reading, not on this check. Then, for each \(b=k/400\), \(0\le k\le120\), it finds by bisection the largest \(\ell\) with \(\max E_*\le0\) on a grid of \(1201\times201\) points and with the low-side condition; the bisection is justified because \(E_*\) increases with \(\ell\) pointwise (its derivative is at least \(5/4-\delta/2\ge5/6\), the dependence through \(c\) contributing about \(-0.003\)). Result (output): at \(b=1/8\) the largest \(\ell\) is \(0.166838\), with the maximum of \(E_*\) at \(\delta=0.387\), \(x=1/2\); over all \(b\) the largest is the same to six digits, at \(b=0.1225\), with boundary \(0.874957=7/8-4.3\cdot10^{-5}\); the certificate binds there, not the low side; over all 121 values of \(b\) the maximizing \(\delta\) lies between \(0.15\) and \(0.52\); the ratio \(\ell/h\) at the optimum is \(0.2056\), above \(7/37\approx0.189\), the supply threshold at \(c=4/9\), which changes negligibly at the optimum. The run could have returned a clearly larger \(\ell\) (up to \(1/5-2b/15\)) and did not. A grid maximum is at most the true maximum, so the true largest \(\ell\) is at most the value found; the difference is of order \(10^{-6}\). As a consistency check, the source's margin \(2.28\cdot10^{-4}\) divided by the derivative \(\partial E_*/\partial\ell\approx1.33\) gives \(1.7\cdot10^{-4}=\ell_{\max}-1/6\).

What forces \(b\). In \(E_*\) the scale \(b\) enters as \(-b/3+(b/2)R_*\): it helps where the row count is small (\(R_*<2/3\)) and hurts where it is large. At the binding point \(\delta\approx0.39\), \(x=1/2\), the source's values give \(R_*=0.668\), so the derivative in \(b\) is \(+6.7\cdot10^{-4}\), nearly zero: \(b=1/8\) is where the certificate's worst case is nearly stationary in \(b\), and the optimum sits just below it. This is the trade the review's reviewer asked about; it is a property of the certificate and not of the low side.

What this decides

In this two-parameter family at \(d=h\), under the extrapolations, the source's construction has no slack to convert: a better earlier bound does not improve the stage, and its scales are within \(5\cdot10^{-5}\) of the optimum. Perfect row counts would gain at most \(1/120\). A boundary substantially below \(7/8\) therefore needs a different probe, with different principal exponents, and not better counting inside this one. The route review's decision allowed one cycle for this test; it is done, and further work on these constants would be tuning of an unaudited import off the critical path. The next step returns to the one-sided form of the obstacle queued by the review.

Follow-up. Developed: the check asked to read the row-count proposition, write \(D_x\), \(P_x\), the cutoff and the certificate as functions of the scales, and decide whether a third application closes. The functions are written above from the text, conditionally on the extrapolations; a third application gains nothing, since a better earlier bound does not enter the binding case; the family's optimum is computed in research/results/rh_bootstrap_08/family_optimum.json. Sources read. Read in the retained TeX of the seven-eighths paper: the subsection "Selected prime slots and the row counts" from its opening through the proposition "Row counts from the witnesses" with its complete proof (including the no-slot count with \(e(r)\)) and the remark after it; previously, the low estimate, the high-exponent lemma's statement and formula, and the endpoint certificate with its proof. Not read, and therefore listed under the extrapolations: the two moment lemmas and the amplification lemma that supply the capacities and the slope; also unread are the detector, the frequency ranges and the order of choices.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Sensitivities at the binding point: the principal exponents enter \(E\) with coefficient of order one, the row-count inputs with coefficient about \(0.02\); per unit logarithmic change, the slope \(\alpha\) moves \(E\) by \(+0.021\), the inverse capacity by \(-0.026\), the plain capacity by \(-0.0024\). So the slope and the inverse capacity matter about equally, the plain capacity ten times less, and the feedback through \(\kappa_0\) is nearly inert. Record. Searches for seven-eighths, zero-free, continuation and sixth-power amplification returned the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane and thm:openai-eleven-twelfths-half-plane only; no record of these sensitivities.
  • The row counts together are capped: with \(R=1-\delta\) the certificate becomes \(\ell\le1/5-2b/15\), a boundary at least \(7/8-1/120\) in the family. Record. The same bound is in the route review's Suggestion check, from the constraint reading's endpoint form; verified below.

Suggestion check. The cap is verified: with \(R=1-\delta\) and \(q\le\delta/2\) the general form gives \(E(h)\le-1/4+5\ell/4+b/6-\delta b/2\), whose supremum over \(\delta\ge0\) is at \(\delta\to0\), so negativity for all \(\delta\) is \(\ell\le1/5-2b/15\) and the boundary \(11/12-\ell/4\ge13/15+b/30\ge7/8-1/120\). The sensitivity table is not verified and nothing rests on it beyond the statement, which follows from the cap, that row counts alone cannot move the boundary far.

The reading check tests the form at one point and on a formula typed twice (now said, and the floating-point function is asserted against the exact one).
