11 October 2026 — Power sums over consecutive exponents: an interpolation bound proved and applied to the off-axis sum of a cluster of \(n\) pairs over \(2n\) steps, with a loss depending on the gaps between heights; Turán's theorem itself not found in a readable source

Working proof of an elementary lower bound for power sums and its application to a model sum at lattice points; the theorem the item names is unread. Interpolation power-sum bound. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Chosen. This entry takes the queued Turán item ahead of the queue's head: it is the second of the items the second review left without a development cycle, the previous two entries developed two other items, and it bears on off-line exclusion through the cluster half of the obstacle, which is also the head item's subject.

Best outcome. Written before the work: a lower bound for the contribution of a cluster of off-line zeros over a short range of bandwidths, with a loss that is a fixed power of \(T\) at most, would settle the cluster half of the obstacle at the goal's parameters: several off-line zeros near one height could then be treated as one. It would still leave the first half, the estimate itself, untouched.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. For the sum \(S(s)=\sum_jm_je^{-isw_j}\) over a conjugation-closed cluster of \(n\) off-axis pairs, what lower bound holds at some point of a short range of \(s\), and how does it compare with thm:resonance-free-bandwidth?

New mathematics. An elementary bound by Lagrange interpolation: for points \(z_1,\dots,z_N\) with \(z_k\) different from the others, among any \(N\) consecutive power sums one is at least \(|b_k||z_k|^{m+1}\prod_{j\ne k}|z_k-z_j|/(1+|z_j|)\). Applied to a cluster of \(n\) pairs with heights in an interval of length \(R\ge1\): among the \(2n\) points \(s=\nu\pi/R\), \(m<\nu\le m+2n\), \(m\ge0\), one has \(|S(s)|\ge m_1e^{\delta_1s_1}\sinh(\pi\delta_1/R)\prod_{j\ge2}(0.34\,|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|/R)^2\), with \(s_1=(m+1)\pi/R\) the first point of the range. The interpolation argument is standard (I recall it as the first step of the power-sum method; no source read); no novelty is claimed.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The off-axis sum of a cluster is a power sum with \(2n\) terms, so the count of all zeros does not enter the lemma. Its loss is a product over the distances of the other heights from the leading one. For heights spread evenly over the interval the product is of the form \(c^n\), which for \(n\) of order \(\log T\) is a fixed power of \(T\), the threshold named under Best outcome; for close heights it is small without bound, and that, not the number of pairs, is where this lemma fails. The bound is pointwise at a lattice point, carries \(e^{\delta_1s}\) at the first point of the range and not at the point found, and is not connected to the Gram form's norm. So the best outcome is not reached, and I had first concluded, wrongly, that the loss is beyond any power of \(T\) for \(\log T\) pairs: an arithmetic slip the reviewer caught. The reviewer sketches a gap-free bound provable in a few lines, which would give a power-of-\(T\) loss for any cluster of \(O(\log T)\) pairs at lattice points; it is unverified and is the next attempt. Bearing. At the goal's parameters nothing is proved about the form; the cycle was spent on it because the item was owed a development cycle and the head item asks the same question.

General statement. lem:interpolation-power-sum-bound, for every \(N\ge1\), all complex \(z_1,\dots,z_N\) with \(z_k\ne z_j\) for \(j\ne k\), all complex coefficients and every integer \(m\ge0\); its application for every cluster as described.

Statement and proof

Lemma (interpolation power-sum bound). lem:interpolation-power-sum-bound. Let \(z_1,\dots,z_N\) be complex numbers, \(k\) an index with \(z_k\ne z_j\) for all \(j\ne k\), \(b_1,\dots,b_N\) complex, and \(s_\nu=\sum_jb_jz_j^{\nu}\). Then for every integer \(m\ge0\), \[\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\ \ge\ |b_k|\,|z_k|^{m+1}\prod_{j\ne k}{|z_k-z_j|\over1+|z_j|}.\]

Proof. Let \(P(z)=\prod_{j\ne k}(z-z_j)/(z_k-z_j)=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}a_iz^i\), so \(P(z_k)=1\) and \(P(z_j)=0\) for \(j\ne k\). Then \(\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}a_is_{m+1+i}=\sum_jb_jz_j^{m+1}P(z_j)=b_kz_k^{m+1}\), so \(|b_k||z_k|^{m+1}\le\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\cdot\sum_i|a_i|\). The coefficients of \(\prod_{j\ne k}(z-z_j)\) have absolute values summing to at most \(\prod_{j\ne k}(1+|z_j|)\), hence \(\sum_i|a_i|\le\prod_{j\ne k}(1+|z_j|)/|z_k-z_j|\). \(\square\)

Application to a cluster. Let \(w_j^{\pm}=\gamma_j\pm i\delta_j\), \(j=1,\dots,n\), with real \(\gamma_j\) in an interval of length \(R\ge1\), \(\gamma_j\ne\gamma_1\) for \(j\ge2\), \(0<\delta_j<1/2\), \(\delta_1=\max_j\delta_j\), and multiplicities \(m_j\ge1\). Let \(S(s)=\sum_jm_j(e^{-isw_j^+}+e^{-isw_j^-})\). Then for every integer \(m\ge0\) there is an integer \(\nu\) with \(m<\nu\le m+2n\) and \[\bigl|S(\nu\pi/R)\bigr|\ \ge\ m_1\,e^{(m+1)\pi\delta_1/R}\,\sinh(\pi\delta_1/R)\,\prod_{j\ge2}\Bigl({2|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|\over(1+e^{\pi/2})R}\Bigr)^{2}.\] If all the \(|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|\) are at least \(g\), the product is at least \((2g/((1+e^{\pi/2})R))^{2n-2}\). As \(|S(-s)|=|S(s)|\), the same holds for negative multiples.

Proof. Put \(h=\pi/R\) and \(\zeta_j^{\pm}=e^{-ihw_j^{\pm}}\), of modulus \(e^{\pm h\delta_j}\) and argument \(-h\gamma_j\). \(S(\nu h)\) is the power sum \(s_\nu\) of these \(2n\) numbers with coefficients \(m_j\). Apply the lemma with \(z_k=\zeta_1^{+}\), of modulus \(E=e^{h\delta_1}\le e^{\pi/2}\); it differs from \(\zeta_1^-\) in modulus and from the others in argument, since \(0<h|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|\le\pi\). The partner contributes \(|\zeta_1^+-\zeta_1^-|/(1+|\zeta_1^-|)=2\sinh(h\delta_1)/(1+e^{-h\delta_1})\ge\sinh(h\delta_1)\). For \(j\ge2\), with \(|r_1e^{i\theta}-r_2|^2=(r_1-r_2)^2+4r_1r_2\sin^2(\theta/2)\), \(r_1=E\), \(r_2=e^{\pm h\delta_j}\ge1/E\) (as \(\delta_j\le\delta_1\)), and \(\theta=h(\gamma_1-\gamma_j)\), \(|\theta|\le\pi\): \(|\zeta_1^+-\zeta_j^{\pm}|\ge2|\sin(\theta/2)|\ge2|\theta|/\pi=2|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|/R\), and \(1+|\zeta_j^{\pm}|\le1+E\le1+e^{\pi/2}\). Each \(j\ge2\) gives two such factors. \(\square\)

Degenerate cases. \(N=1\): the lemma is the identity \(|s_{m+1}|=|b||z|^{m+1}\). \(z_k=0\) gives the trivial bound \(0\). A point coinciding with \(z_k\) is excluded, and the bound degenerates as a point approaches \(z_k\); the other points may coincide with each other. Two points \(\pm1\) with unit coefficients: the bound is \(1\) and the true maximum over two consecutive exponents is \(2\). In the application, \(n=1\) gives \(m_1e^{(m+1)h\delta}\sinh(h\delta)\) against the true \(2m_1\cosh(\nu h\delta)\); a height equal to \(\gamma_1\) is excluded; \(R\ge1\) and \(\delta_j<1/2\) are used only for the constant. The bound ignores the positivity of the multiplicities: for two simple pairs at heights \(\pm g/2\) with equal \(\delta\), \(S(s)=4\cosh(s\delta)\cos(sg/2)\), which is not small for small \(g\) at moderate \(s\), while the bound's factor is of order \(g^2\).

Nonvacuity. The lemma's hypotheses hold, for instance, for \(z=(2,1,1/2)\), \(b=(1,1,1)\), \(m=0\), \(k=1\): the bound is \(2\cdot\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac{1.5}{1.5}=1\) and \(s_1=3.5\). The application's hypotheses hold for \(n=2\), \(\gamma=(0,1)\), \(R=1\), \(\delta=(0.3,0.2)\), unit multiplicities, \(m=0\): the bound is \(e^{0.3\pi}\sinh(0.3\pi)(2/(1+e^{\pi/2}))^2\), about \(0.33\).

What it says

The loss, in the goal's parameter. The factor lost against \(m_1e^{\delta_1s_1}\) is \(\sinh(\pi\delta_1/R)\prod_{j\ge2}(0.34|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|/R)^2\). For \(n\) heights spread evenly over the interval, \(|\gamma_1-\gamma_j|/R\) runs over multiples of \(1/(n-1)\) and the product is at least \(((n-1)!/(n-1)^{n-1})^2\,0.34^{2n-2}\), which is \(c^n\) with an absolute \(c\); for \(n\) of order \(\log T\) this is a fixed power of \(T\). With only a least gap \(g\) the bound \((0.34g/R)^{2n-2}\) can be as small as \(n^{-2n}\) when \(g/R\) is about \(1/n\), which for \(n\) of order \(\log T\) is \(T^{-O(\log\log T)}\). For close heights the factor is small without bound. A further factor is hidden in the exponent: the point found may be as late as \(s_1+(2n-1)\pi/R\), so against \(e^{\delta_1s}\) at that point the bound loses up to \(e^{-(2n-1)\pi\delta_1/R}\).

Comparison with the recorded theorem. thm:resonance-free-bandwidth, as registered: for the flat window on the complete grid, off-axis points at pairwise distinct heights with least gap \(g\), total multiplicity \(M\) and largest distance \(\delta_*\) of multiplicity \(m_*\); for \(\Lambda_1\ge1/\delta_*\) and \(H\ge4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) some \(\Lambda\in[\Lambda_1,\Lambda_1+H]\) has \(\|B_\Lambda\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\ge\tfrac{\sqrt2}4m_*e^{\delta_*\Lambda}/(\delta_*\Lambda)-M\). I compared with this registered summary and did not re-read the theorem's full record in this cycle. The two statements concern different quantities, a norm of the off-axis part of the form there and \(|S|\) at one lattice point here; with that said, the theorem has no multiplicative loss and a range that grows like \(1/g\), and the lemma has a range \(2n\pi/R\) independent of the gaps and a loss that depends on them. prop:subset-sum-resonance shows that at a single bandwidth a real loss of order \(b^{-2k}\) occurs for \(2^k\) points at resonant heights; the lemma says that over \(2n\) consecutive lattice points such a loss cannot persist beyond the stated factor, and I do not compare constants.

Not connected to the form's norm. The Gram model's second moment is a mean square of \(S\); passing from one lattice point to it needs a bound for the derivative of \(S\) near the point, which is not carried out here.

The theorem the item names. I searched arXiv for a statement of Turán's second main theorem; two papers that cite it were fetched as TeX sources (math/0607238 and math/0609271) and searched for the phrase, and neither states it (the first cites it to the 1984 book, Chapter 8). A request for the book is filed. The reviewer's recollection of the statement is listed below; nothing here rests on it.

Sub-ideas

  • Prove the reviewer's gap-free bound: for points of modulus at least one, interpolate \(z^{-(m+1)}\) in Newton form, bound the coefficient sum by \(\sum_{k<N}\binom{m+k}{k}2^k\le(2e(m+N)/N)^N\), and deduce \(\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\ge(N/(2e(m+N)))^N|\sum_jb_j|\); then the cluster consequence after rescaling by \(e^{h\delta_1}\), with the extra factor \(e^{-2\delta_1s}\) it costs, and the range of \(\delta_1s\) in which that is a power of \(T\).
  • Pass from a lattice point to the mean square: bound \(|S'|\) by the multiplicities and \(e^{\delta_1s}\), deduce a lower bound for the mean square of \(S\) over a short range, and state the corollary for the off-axis part's norm beside thm:resonance-free-bandwidth, re-reading that theorem's full record.
  • When the book's statement is available, restate the application with Turán's second main theorem, whose normalization by the largest modulus avoids the extra factor.

Follow-up. Continuing: the item asked to read the theorem in a source and to apply it to the off-line zeros alone, comparing with the recorded theorem. The application and comparison are made with a proved substitute; the theorem's own statement is unread. The next attempt on this item needs no source: the first sub-idea. Record search. The registry was searched for power sum lower bound Lagrange interpolation consecutive exponents Vandermonde, and for Turan power sum lower bound window (in the review's cycle): no record on these objects; the registered results on this question are thm:resonance-free-bandwidth and prop:subset-sum-resonance.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Turán's second main theorem, from the reviewer's memory: for \(1=|z_1|\ge\dots\ge|z_n|\), \(\max_{m<\nu\le m+n}|s_\nu|\ge2(n/(8e(m+n)))^n\min_j|b_1+\dots+b_j|\), partial sums in order of decreasing modulus, no gaps needed; constant improved to \(4e\) by later authors. Record. Searched Turan power sum lower bound window: no match; third sub-idea.
  • Its consequence for the cluster: positive coefficients make the least partial sum at least \(\min m_j\), the step is free, and some \(s\in(s_0,2s_0]\) has \(|S(s)|\ge2(16e)^{-2n}\min_jm_j\,e^{\delta_1s}\), a power-of-\(T\) loss for \(n=O(\log T)\). Record. The same search: no match; third sub-idea.
  • A gap-free bound in a few lines for the first theorem's normalization, by Newton interpolation of \(z^{-(m+1)}\) with divided differences given by complete homogeneous polynomials (checked by the reviewer for one small case only). Record. Searched power sum lower bound Lagrange interpolation consecutive exponents Vandermonde: no match; first sub-idea.
  • A recalled theorem of Cassels: for pure power sums, a maximum over \(2N-1\) exponents with no loss. Record. The same search: no match; not pursued in this entry.

Suggestion check. Verified: the two-pair example, \(S(s)=2\cosh(s\delta)(e^{-isg/2}+e^{isg/2})=4\cosh(s\delta)\cos(sg/2)\), now in the degenerate cases; and the evenly spaced product, \(\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(j/(n-1))^2=((n-1)!/(n-1)^{n-1})^2\ge e^{-2(n-1)}\), when the leading height is at an end of the interval (in the middle the distances are at least half as large in pairs, which changes \(c\) only). The recalled theorems and the Newton-form bound are not verified.

Verification gate. No dependency's proof is used; the comparison quotes thm:resonance-free-bandwidth and prop:subset-sum-resonance from their registered summaries, not their full records, which is said in the text. One fresh-context medium reviewer checked the lemma and the application and found both correct, and found the interpretation wrong in three places, all corrected: the loss \(c^N\) for \(N\) of order \(\log T\) is a power of \(T\), not beyond every power, so "boundedly many pairs only" and "neither bound gives a polynomial loss" were false; the least gap had been substituted for the actual distances, hiding that evenly spaced clusters lose only \(c^n\) (the application is restated with the product); the bound carries the exponential at the first point of the range, not at the point found. Also corrected: the hypotheses were stronger than used (only \(z_k\) must differ from the others); the summary's \(s_0\) was ambiguous; the comparison dropped the theorem's subtracted term and its condition on \(\Lambda_1\) (now quoted); "separates without difficulty" claimed more than shown. Process assessment. The lemma is correct and small. I judged its use by an arithmetic slip, treating \(c^{\log T}\) as smaller than every power of \(T\), and by replacing the distances with the least gap, and on that basis wrote that the method treats bounded clusters only; the reviewer showed the opposite for spaced clusters and pointed to a few-line route to a gap-free bound. Prevention. The class is a loss judged without converting it to the goal's parameter. The entry now has a paragraph that does the conversion, and the Best outcome paragraph, which the finisher requires, names the threshold to convert against; what failed is that I did not compare the two. 11 October 2026 — A gap-free bound for power sums of points of modulus at least one, proved by Newton interpolation; for a cluster it bounds the off-axis sum below only by \(e^{-\delta_1s}\), so it cannot detect an off-line cluster; the conversion to the goal's parameter is written out for this bound and, conditionally, for two others

Working proof of an elementary lower bound for power sums; its consequence for the model sum is too weak to use. Gap-free power-sum bound. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Chosen. This entry continues the queued Turán item ahead of the queue's head, as the previous entry's next step named (the queue tool's status: one entry, three sub-ideas open); it bears on off-line exclusion through the cluster half of the obstacle.

Owed attempt. The previous entry on this item named the gap-free bound by Newton interpolation as the next attempt. It is made here: the bound is proved, and its consequence for the cluster is worked out and found too weak.

Best outcome. Written before the work: a lower bound for the off-axis sum of a cluster of \(n\) pairs, at some point of a short range of \(s\), of size at least \(e^{\delta_1s}\) times a loss that is small against the gain \(e^{\delta_1s}\) itself for \(n\) up to a multiple of \(\log T\); at the goal's parameters that would let a cluster of off-line zeros near one height be treated as its leading pair.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. Does the reviewer's few-line route give a gap-free lower bound for power sums, and what does it give for the cluster when the loss is converted to the goal's parameter?

New mathematics. One lemma: for complex \(z_1,\dots,z_N\) of modulus at least one (not necessarily distinct), complex \(b_j\) and \(m\ge0\), \(\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\ge|\sum_jb_j|/\sum_{k<N}\binom{m+k}{k}2^k\). The reviewer recalls this as Turán's first main theorem with a constant due to Makai and de Bruijn; I have read neither, and claim no novelty.

Goal progress. Unchanged. For the cluster the lemma needs all moduli at least one, which is arranged by dividing by the smallest modulus, and the bound it returns is \(|S(s)|\ge2Me^{-\delta_1s}/A\): below the benchmark \(e^{\delta_1s}\) by the square of the gain, before the factor \(A\). Converted. In the goal's parameter: the gain of the leading pair at bandwidth \(s=\theta\log T\) is \(e^{\delta_1s}=T^{\delta_1\theta}\), at most \(T^{\theta/2}\); this lemma's bound is \(2MT^{-\delta_1\theta}/A\), which is below \(2M\), the upper bound for the same sum when every \(\delta_j=0\); it is a true statement of non-cancellation and holds equally for points on the axis, so it cannot separate an off-axis cluster from an on-axis one and is useless for detection. The previous entry's bound keeps the exponential, at the first point of its range, and loses \(c^{2n}\) for evenly spread heights; Turán's second theorem, as the reviewers recall it and with a constant \(c\) that is absolute only when the starting exponent is of the order of the number of points and the multiplicities are positive, would do so for arbitrary heights. For such a bound, a loss below the gain \(T^{\delta_1\theta}\) needs \(2n\log(1/c)<\delta_1\theta\log T\), that is fewer than \(\epsilon\log T\) pairs with \(\epsilon=\delta_1\theta/(2\log(1/c))\); this is a necessary condition for use, not a sufficient one, since nothing connects the lattice point to the form's norm yet. So the best outcome is not reached here; for the proved interpolation bound it could be reached only for evenly spread clusters below a small multiple of \(\log T\) pairs, and the statement about the recalled theorem is conditional on the recollection. Whether a loss \(c^{2n}\) is forced for clusters with bounded multiplicities is not shown. Bearing. At the goal's parameters this gives nothing. The cycle was spent on it because the attempt was owed and its outcome decides whether the item waits on the book.

General statement. lem:gap-free-power-sum-bound, for every \(N\ge1\), all complex \(z_j\) with \(|z_j|\ge1\), all complex \(b_j\) and every integer \(m\ge0\).

(4) "No arXiv source states the theorem" rested on one search and two fetched papers: accepted; the committed entry says exactly that and no more, and the chat line overstated it. (5) The independent-check safeguard could itself be a rule: fixed; the computation rules now require of a numerical control one check that does not share the method under test.

Statement and proof

Lemma (gap-free power-sum bound). lem:gap-free-power-sum-bound. Let \(z_1,\dots,z_N\) be complex numbers with \(|z_j|\ge1\), \(b_1,\dots,b_N\) complex, \(s_\nu=\sum_jb_jz_j^{\nu}\), and \(m\ge0\) an integer. Then \[\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\ \ge\ {|b_1+\dots+b_N|\over A(m,N)},\qquad A(m,N)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\binom{m+k}{k}2^k\ \le\ 2^{N-1}\binom{m+N}{N-1}.\] For \(m\ge N-1\), \(A(m,N)\le(2e(m+N)/N)^N\).

Proof. First let the \(z_j\) be distinct. Let \(f(z)=z^{-(m+1)}\) and let \(P\) be the polynomial of degree less than \(N\) with \(P(z_j)=f(z_j)\) for all \(j\). Writing \(P(z)=\sum_{i<N}a_iz^i\), \(\sum_ia_is_{m+1+i}=\sum_jb_jz_j^{m+1}P(z_j)=\sum_jb_j\), so \(|\sum_jb_j|\le\max_{m<\nu\le m+N}|s_\nu|\sum_i|a_i|\). Newton's form is \(P(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}f[z_1,\dots,z_{k+1}]\prod_{j\le k}(z-z_j)\), and the coefficients of \(\prod_{j\le k}(z-z_j)\) have absolute sum at most \(\prod_{j\le k}(1+|z_j|)\). The divided differences: for \(g_t(z)=1/(z-t)\), \(g_t[z_1,\dots,z_{k+1}]=(-1)^k/\prod_{j\le k+1}(z_j-t)\), by induction on \(k\) from \((1/(a-t)-1/(b-t))/(a-b)=-1/((a-t)(b-t))\) and the recursion for divided differences. For \(|t|<1\le|z_j|\), \(g_t(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}t^mz^{-(m+1)}\) and \(\prod_j(z_j-t)^{-1}=\prod_jz_j^{-1}\sum_{m\ge0}h_m(1/z_1,\dots,1/z_{k+1})t^m\), with \(h_m\) the complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial; divided differences being finite linear combinations of values, comparing coefficients of \(t^m\) gives \[f[z_1,\dots,z_{k+1}]=(-1)^k\prod_{j\le k+1}z_j^{-1}\;h_m(1/z_1,\dots,1/z_{k+1}).\] \(h_m\) in \(k+1\) variables has \(\binom{m+k}{k}\) monomials, each of modulus at most one here, so \(|f[z_1,\dots,z_{k+1}]|\le\binom{m+k}{k}\prod_{j\le k+1}|z_j|^{-1}\). Hence the \(k\)-th Newton term contributes at most \(\binom{m+k}{k}|z_{k+1}|^{-1}\prod_{j\le k}(1+|z_j|)/|z_j|\le\binom{m+k}{k}2^k\) to \(\sum_i|a_i|\), which gives \(\sum_i|a_i|\le A(m,N)\). If some points coincide, merge them, adding their coefficients: the power sums and \(\sum b_j\) are unchanged, the number of points drops to some \(N'\le N\), and the bound for \(N'\) implies the one for \(N\), since the maximum is over a larger set of exponents and \(A(m,N')\le A(m,N)\). The bounds for \(A\): \(2^k\le2^{N-1}\) and \(\sum_{k<N}\binom{m+k}{k}=\binom{m+N}{N-1}\); and \(\binom{m+N}{N-1}=\binom{m+N}{N}\tfrac N{m+1}\le(e(m+N)/N)^N\) when \(m+1\ge N\). \(\square\)

Degenerate cases. \(N=1\): \(A=1\) and the bound is \(|b||z|^{\nu}\ge|b|\), true as \(|z|\ge1\). \(\sum b_j=0\): the bound is trivial. All points equal: merged to one, bound \(|\sum b_j|\). A point of modulus below one is outside the statement, and the bound can fail there (one point \(z=1/2\), \(b=1\): \(s_\nu=2^{-\nu}<1\)). \(z=(1,-1)\), \(b=(1,1)\), \(m=0\): \(A=1+2=3\), bound \(2/3\), true maximum over \(\nu=1,2\) is \(2\).

Nonvacuity. \(z=(2,1,-1)\), \(b=(1,1,1)\), \(m=0\), \(N=3\): \(A=1+2+4=7\), bound \(3/7\); \(s_1=2\). All moduli are at least one.

The cluster, and the conversion

Consequence for the cluster. With the previous entry's notation, \(S(\nu h)=\sum_jm_j((\zeta_j^+)^\nu+(\zeta_j^-)^\nu)\), \(|\zeta_j^{\pm}|=e^{\pm h\delta_j}\), for any step \(h>0\) and with no condition on the heights. The numbers \(E\zeta_j^{\pm}\), \(E=e^{h\delta_1}\), have modulus at least one, and their power sums are \(E^{\nu}S(\nu h)\) with coefficient sum \(2M\), \(M=\sum_jm_j\). The lemma gives, for every \(m\ge0\), some \(\nu\in(m,m+2n]\) with \[|S(\nu h)|\ \ge\ {2M\,e^{-\nu h\delta_1}\over A(m,2n)}.\] The exponential has the wrong sign: the normalization by the smallest modulus is what the lemma's hypothesis forces, and the bound then only propagates the value \(S(0)=2M\) forward against the decay of the small terms. Against the benchmark \(m_1e^{\delta_1s}\) it loses \(e^{-2\delta_1s}\) besides \(A\). The application uses neither the positivity of the multiplicities (only \(M\ne0\)) nor \(\delta_j<1/2\), and the pairing only to identify the smallest modulus. \(A(m,2n)\) is a power of \(T\) when \(n\log((m+2n)/n)=O(\log T)\), which is moot given the sign of the exponential.

What a usable bound must have. The conversion in the Goal progress paragraph shows two requirements: the normalization must be by the largest modulus, so that the bound carries \(e^{+\delta_1s}\) (the previous entry's interpolation bound has this, at the cost of the gaps; Turán's second theorem as recalled has it without that cost); and the loss \(c^{2n}\) must be below the gain \(e^{\delta_1s}\), which for \(s=\theta\log T\) restricts \(n\) to less than \(\delta_1\theta\log T/(2\log(1/c))\). A cluster of more pairs than that, within one window, is beyond these three bounds; whether a loss \(c^{2n}\) is forced for such clusters is not shown. The reviewer's example below shows it is forced when multiplicities are unrestricted, and that the example needs total multiplicity \(2^n\), more than a window can hold.

Decide whether, for a cluster with multiplicities bounded by \(K\), a loss \(c^{n}\) over a range of proportional length is forced (an example) or can be beaten; test the recalled theorem of Borwein and Erdélyi on polynomials with bounded coefficients on arcs, first in the case of equal \(\delta_j\) and heights on a lattice, where \(S\) is a polynomial in \(e^{-is\eta}\) times \(2\cosh(s\delta)\). Compare \(\epsilon\log T\) with an explicit bound for the number of zeros in a window.

11 October 2026 — The backward-flowed function as a series over \(n\): in this form the weights \(e^{t\log^2n/4}\) are not multiplicative and each term's factor is shifted by \(it\log n\); two of the three recorded prime-side premises rest on properties of coefficients that the Euler product supplies, and the filter is not shown to remove any of the three

Working proof of an elementary rewriting of a series stated in a read source; a classification of three recorded premises by their definitions; no result about zeta zeros. Flowed series expansion. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Chosen. This entry takes the heat-flow item ahead of the queue's head, as the previous entry's next step named: the Turán item has had two entries in a row without bearing (the queue tool's guidance at this cycle's start) and the rule asks for a different item. It bears on off-line exclusion by asking which property of \(\xi\) a proof must use.

Owed attempt. It is made here for the definitions; the polynomial-model test of a certificate under backward flow, the sub-idea's other half, is not.

Best outcome. Written before the work: at the goal's own parameters this excludes no zero. Its best outcome is to find a recorded premise that uses only the sum over \(n\), which the filter would then remove from the route, or to show that all three use multiplicativity, which says the route's premises are at least of the kind a proof must use.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. What series over \(n\) does the flowed function keep, in what sense is it not multiplicative, and through which coefficients are the three recorded prime-side premises defined?

New mathematics. A rewriting of the source's series: for \(t<0\) and all complex \(z\), \(H_t(z)=\tfrac12\sum_{n\ge1}n^{-1/2-iz/2}\,e^{t\log^2n/4}\,F_t(z+it\log n)\), with \(F_t(w)=\int_{\mathbb R}e^{tv^2}\varphi_1(v)e^{iwv}dv\) and \(\varphi_1\) the first term of \(\Phi\). At \(t=0\) and \(\Im z<-1\) this is \(\zeta(s)F_0(z)/2\) with \(s=1/2+iz/2\), which is checked to equal \(\xi(s)/8\). The splitting of each term into a weight and a shifted factor is a property of this way of writing the series and not of \(H_t\): the factor's growth cancels the weight. The source has the series in another form (its display for \(H_t\) as a sum over \(n\) of oscillatory integrals); the rewriting is mine and elementary.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The flowed function is a sum over \(n\) whose weights \(e^{t\log^2n/4}\) are not multiplicative for \(t\ne0\) and whose \(n\)-th term depends on \(z\) also through the shift \(it\log n\), so it is not a Dirichlet series times a fixed factor in this form. Two of the three recorded prime-side premises use special properties of the coefficients \(\Lambda(n)\) of \(-\zeta'/\zeta\), their support on prime powers and their sign, which the Euler product supplies (a reciprocal or logarithmic derivative of a Dirichlet series is always a Dirichlet series; the product gives these properties). For the third, the continuation criterion, I did not determine in this cycle which property of the coefficients of \(1/L\) its premise uses. No analogue of any of the three for the flowed function is exhibited or excluded, so the filter is not shown to remove any of them. Converted. No threshold in the goal's parameter is involved: the outcome is a classification by definition. Bearing. At the goal's parameters this gives nothing, as stated before the work.

General statement. lem:flowed-series-expansion, for every real \(t<0\) and every complex \(z\); and for \(t=0\), every \(z\) with \(\Im z<-1\).

What the source states

Read in the TeX source of arXiv 1801.05914, beyond the lines read in the item's previous entry: \(H_0(z)=\tfrac18\xi(\tfrac12+\tfrac{iz}2)\); \(H_0(z)=\int_0^\infty\Phi(u)\cos(zu)\,du\) with \(\Phi(u)=\sum_{n\ge1}(2\pi^2n^4e^{9u}-3\pi n^2e^{5u})\exp(-\pi n^2e^{4u})\), the sum converging absolutely for negative \(u\) also, and \(\Phi\) even by Poisson summation; and, in the section on asymptotics, "as \(\Phi\) is even" \(H_t(z)=\tfrac12\int_{\mathbb R}e^{tu^2}\Phi(u)e^{izu}du\) and, by Fubini's theorem "which can be justified when \(t<0\)", a display of \(H_t\) as half a sum over \(n\) of two oscillatory integrals. I read these lines directly and not the proofs around them.

Statement and proof

Lemma (flowed series expansion). lem:flowed-series-expansion. Let \(\varphi_n(u)=(2\pi^2n^4e^{9u}-3\pi n^2e^{5u})\exp(-\pi n^2e^{4u})\), so \(\Phi=\sum_n\varphi_n\), and for real \(t\le0\) let \(K_t(z)=\int_{\mathbb R}e^{tu^2}\Phi(u)e^{izu}\,du\) and \(F_t(w)=\int_{\mathbb R}e^{tv^2}\varphi_1(v)e^{iwv}\,dv\), which is entire for \(t<0\) and defined for \(\Im w<5\) when \(t=0\). Then: (1) \(\varphi_n(u)=n^{-1/2}\varphi_1(u+\tfrac12\log n)\). (2) For \(t<0\) and every complex \(z\), and for \(t=0\) and \(\Im z<-1\), \[K_t(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}n^{-1/2-iz/2}\,e^{t\log^2n/4}\,F_t(z+it\log n),\] the series converging absolutely. (3) For \(t\ne0\) the weights \(a_t(n)=e^{t\log^2n/4}\) are not multiplicative: \(a_t(6)\ne a_t(2)a_t(3)\).

Proof. (1) With \(v=u+\tfrac12\log n\): \(e^{9v}=n^{9/2}e^{9u}\), \(e^{5v}=n^{5/2}e^{5u}\), \(e^{4v}=n^2e^{4u}\), so \(\varphi_1(v)=n^{1/2}\varphi_n(u)\). (2) Absolute convergence. \(|\varphi_n(u)|\le(2\pi^2n^4e^{9u}+3\pi n^2e^{5u})\exp(-\pi n^2e^{4u})\), and \(\sum_nn^4e^{-\pi n^2x}\le C(1+x^{-5/2})\), \(\sum_nn^2e^{-\pi n^2x}\le C(1+x^{-3/2})\) for \(x>0\), so \(\sum_n|\varphi_n(u)|\le C'e^{9|u|}(1+e^{-10u})\), at most exponential in \(|u|\); for \(t<0\) the factor \(e^{tu^2}\) makes \(\sum_n\int e^{tu^2}|\varphi_n(u)|e^{|u||\Im z|}du\) finite for every \(z\). For \(t=0\) and \(c=-\Im z>1\), \(\int|\varphi_n(u)|e^{cu}du=n^{-1/2-c/2}\int|\varphi_1(v)|e^{cv}dv\) by (1), finite because \(\varphi_1\) decays like \(e^{5v}\) at \(-\infty\) and faster than any exponential at \(+\infty\), and \(\sum_nn^{-(1+c)/2}\) converges. So the sum and the integral may be exchanged. For the \(n\)-th term put \(u=v-\tfrac12L\), \(L=\log n\): \(\varphi_n(u)=n^{-1/2}\varphi_1(v)\), \(e^{tu^2}=e^{tv^2}e^{-tLv}e^{tL^2/4}\), \(e^{izu}=e^{izv}e^{-izL/2}\), and \(e^{-tLv}e^{izv}=e^{i(z+itL)v}\); hence the term equals \(n^{-1/2-iz/2}e^{tL^2/4}F_t(z+itL)\). (3) \(\log^26-\log^22-\log^23=2\log2\log3\ne0\). \(\square\)

Consequence, with the source's evenness. As the source states \(\Phi\) even, \(H_t=\tfrac12K_t\), which gives the formula under New mathematics. At \(t=0\), \(K_0(z)=\zeta(s)F_0(z)\) for \(\Im z<-1\), \(s=\tfrac12+\tfrac{iz}2\). Check against \(H_0=\xi(s)/8\): with \(x=\pi e^{4v}\) and \(e^{izv}=e^{(2s-1)v}\), \(F_0(z)=\tfrac12\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2+2)-\tfrac34\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2+1)=\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2)\tfrac s2(\tfrac s4-\tfrac14)=\tfrac{s(s-1)}8\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2)\), so \(K_0/2=\zeta(s)\tfrac{s(s-1)}{16}\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2)=\xi(s)/8\), with the source's \(\xi(s)=\tfrac{s(s-1)}2\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(\tfrac s2)\zeta(s)\). This is an independent check of the lemma and of the factor \(\tfrac12\).

The form is not canonical. The \(n\)-th factor \(F_t(z+it\log n)\) depends on \(n\), and the maximum over \(v\) of \(e^{tv^2+|t|Lv}\) is \(e^{|t|L^2/4}\), which cancels the weight \(e^{tL^2/4}\); so the non-multiplicativity of the weights is a feature of this way of writing the series and by itself says nothing about \(H_t\). The reviewer's observation.

Degenerate cases. \(t=0\) and \(-1\le\Im z<5\): the series diverges, as it must, being \(\zeta\) to the left of its abscissa; for \(\Im z\ge5\) the terms are undefined. \(n=1\): the term is \(F_t(z)\). \(t>0\) is outside the statement: the convergence argument uses the Gaussian decay of \(e^{tu^2}\) for \(t<0\), and the source justifies its own exchange for \(t<0\) only. Part 3 fails at \(t=0\), where the weights are all \(1\).

Nonvacuity. Every \(t<0\) and every \(z\) satisfy the hypotheses, for instance \(t=-1\), \(z=0\); at \(t=0\), \(z=-2i\) gives \(s=3/2\).

The three premises, by their definitions

  • The Gram form's prime side, read in the density project's definitions: \(P_X(\tau)=-\tfrac1\pi\sum_{n\le X}\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}\cos(\tau\log n)\), with \(\Lambda\) the von Mangoldt function. These are the Dirichlet coefficients of \(-\zeta'/\zeta\); they are supported on prime powers and nonnegative because \(\zeta\) is an Euler product. Uses multiplicativity.
  • The continuation criterion of the seven-eighths source, as recorded in this notebook's criterion reading: a Mellin integral of the reciprocal \(L\)-function. The reciprocal's coefficients are the Möbius function by the Euler product. Which property of them the criterion's premise uses (boundedness, multiplicativity, or only that the reciprocal is a Dirichlet series) is not determined in this cycle: I rely on the recorded reading's summary and did not re-read the record or the source.
  • The certificate's sign condition \(g(\log n)\ge0\) at prime powers (second review): it controls the sign of \(\sum_n\Lambda(n)n^{-1/2}g(\log n)\), again through \(\Lambda\ge0\). Uses multiplicativity.

What is and is not concluded. The first and third premises use properties of \(\Lambda\) (support on prime powers, nonnegativity) that the Euler product supplies; the second is undetermined. The flowed function, by the lemma, is a series over \(n\) which in this form has non-multiplicative weights and shifted factors, a form that is not canonical. I do not prove that its logarithmic derivative or reciprocal has no expansion that could play the same role: that would need a zero-free half-plane for it and the structure of \(F_t\), which are not examined; the reviewer's heuristic, listed below, is that the series is dominated by its first term only in a region that widens logarithmically and not in a half-plane. So the filter is not shown to remove any of the three. The Best outcome paragraph, written before the work, said that the premises would then be "of the kind a proof must use"; that goes beyond the filter, which says only that a proof must use some property failing for the backward-flowed functions, of which multiplicativity is a candidate and not shown to be the one.

Follow-up. Continuing: the item asked which recorded premise makes sense for the flowed function and whether it survives a backward flow. Answered so far: the flowed function's series, and that the three prime-side premises are defined through Euler-product coefficients. Open: the test of a certificate's zero-side inequality under backward flow in the polynomial model, and the absorption rate with the theorem of Ki, Kim and Lee. The next attempt on this item, after the route review that is now due and after another item has had its turn (this is the item's second entry in a row without bearing), is the polynomial-model test, which needs no source; it was not attempted here because the series and the classification filled the cycle. Record search. The registry was searched for heat flow xi series over n Gaussian weight non-multiplicative coefficients Dirichlet, and for de Bruijn-Newman zero-free region flowed function: no record on these objects. The item's sub-ideas:

11 October 2026 — Third route review, goal-level: six entries limited three methods and proved no premise; a theorem of Turán read in a supplied source is a lossy one-directional implication from prime sums to a zero-free segment, and the next test is to find where its proof loses

Goal-level route review; no result about zeta zeros. Third exclusion review. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

New mathematics. None. Statements read in four sources supplied by the user during the previous cycle are quoted below with what was and was not read.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The six research entries since the last review each reported the obligation unchanged, and the turn guidance at this cycle's start counts sixteen research entries in a row declaring no bearing at the goal's parameters. Bearing. This review gives nothing at the goal's parameters; it selects the next test.

General statement. The line's claim is the open statement itself, off-line exclusion. No registered conjecture stands between the proved statements and it.

Review. No stand-in review ran at the previous entry's checkpoint; the five objections of the one before are answered in the gap-free power-sum entry.

What the six entries did. Two entries on the last review's picked lead: a test function nonnegative on the whole strip, or relaxed in a window with one count, can certify the absence of a zero at a point only where a weighted count of actual zeros near its height is below \(4/\cos(\pi\delta)\) (strip positivity obstruction, relaxed). Two on the heat flow: a strip contraction for polynomials, and the flowed function as a series over \(n\); the filter says a property shared with a backward-flowed function cannot be the only one a proof uses, and is not shown to remove any recorded premise (contraction, series). Two on power sums: an interpolation bound whose loss depends on the gaps, and a gap-free bound that loses the exponential (interpolation, gap-free). By the Verification gates of the entries concerned, drafted disposals of queue items were not upheld by their reviewers (the relaxed-positivity and heat-flow entries in this period, two more before it), and each entry's summary was trimmed in review.

What the goal needs. An inequality that a hypothetical zero at \(\beta_0+i\gamma_0\), \(\beta_0>1/2\), violates, proved without knowledge of the other zeros, at every height.

Falsification attempt. Against the last review's reading that the obstacle is "a power saving in a prime sum of length about \(T^{\theta}\) at height \(T\)": Turán's Theorem 33.2, read in this cycle, is consistent with it and gives it a published one-directional form. It does not falsify the reading, and shows that the conversion from saving to region loses: an exponent \(b\) of saving on sums of length at least \(\tau_0^{a}\) gives the region \(\sigma\ge1-b^3/a^2\), so square-root cancellation at length \(\tau_0\) (\(a=1\), \(b=1/2\)) gives \(7/8\) and not the line, and, on the reviewer's recalled bound for the sums (a heuristic, not a proof), no pair gives better than \(3/4\) from a premise true under the hypothesis. Against the notebook's habit of working in models: none of the sixteen entries without bearing touched a statement about primes; the one theorem in hand that links a statement about primes to a zero at a point was in a book the notebook had named and not read.

Assessment of this line. Weak, with one change in kind. The six entries limited methods and proved nothing toward a premise. The change is the reading: a published theorem gives a lossy one-directional implication from a bound on the sums \(\sum_{N_1\le n\le N_2}\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau}\) to a zero-free segment at that height, an object about primes in place of the models of criteria on which the route spent its cycles. As stated it does not reach the goal. I give no odds. The honest reading of the record is that the notebook has no approach.

Obstacle

The line is stuck on an unconditional bound, with a power saving in the height, for the exponential sum over primes \(Z(\tau,N_1,N_2)=\sum_{N_1\le n\le N_2}\Lambda(n)e^{-i\tau\log n}\) on ranges \(N\ge\tau^{a}\). By the read theorem a saving \(\tau^{-b}\) there excludes zeros at height \(\tau\) with \(\sigma\ge1-b^3/a^2\); what is missing is any pair \((a,b)\) with \(b^3/a^2\) bounded below, and, for the goal, a route that does not lose in the conversion.

Outside leads

  • The proof mechanism of Turán's local implication from prime exponential sums to a zero-free segment. Named source: P. Turán, On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications (1984), Section 33, Theorems 33.1 and 33.2 and their two corollaries, read as page images from the supplied scan. Theorem 33.2: if for a real \(\tau_0\) and constants \(a>0\), \(0<b\le1\), \(|Z(\tau_0,N_1,N_2)|<cN\log^{100}N/\tau_0^{\,b}\) for all \(\tau_0^{a}\le N\le N_1<N_2\le2N\), then \(\zeta(s)\ne0\) on the segment \(\sigma\ge1-b^3/a^2\), \(t=\tau_0\), for \(\tau_0\) large. Its second corollary: the same for all large \(\tau_0\) gives the half-plane, which the book calls a quasi-Riemann conjecture. Theorem 33.1 is a statement in the other direction, from a zero-free rectangle near height \(T\) to a bound for \(Z\); its exponent is not legible to me in the scan and is not quoted. Target: off-line exclusion at a point, from a statement about primes at that height. Answers. Its mechanism turns an inequality about primes, local in the height, into the exclusion of a zero at that height whatever the other zeros are; a version without its loss would be the second half of the missing implication. Break point. The translation breaks if no pair \((a,b)\) with \(b^3/a^2\) bounded below is available, and for the goal because the conversion is lossy: the line needs \(b^3/a^2\ge1/2\), beyond square-root cancellation at length \(\tau_0\). What the test found there: I know of no unconditional pair with fixed \(b>0\) and state none; and the stage-one reviewer's arithmetic, checked under Independent alternative, gives a ceiling resting on a recalled bound: fed a bound \(N^{\theta}\) the theorem returns \(1-(1-\theta)^2\), so \(3/4\) at best if the sums are never smaller than about \(N^{1/2}\), which is recalled and not proved here. The stated theorem, aimed at the open statement, is falsified on that recollection; the lead is its proof mechanism; what survives the break point is its form, a local one-directional implication from primes to a zero at a point, and the question where its proof loses. Test. Passed with that limit: the statement is read, the translation is exact, and the loss is located in the conversion, not in the form.
  • Ki's theorem on zeros of the sum functions. Named source: H. Ki, On a theorem of Levinson, J. Number Theory 107 (2004), its main theorem, read in the supplied copy: for \(\lambda>0\) there is \(\kappa>0\) such that all zeros of \(F_\lambda\) in a region \(R_\lambda(\kappa)\) are real, and for \(\lambda\ge1/2\) all zeros are real; the paper asks how zeros of \(\zeta\) to the right of \(1/2+\lambda\) force non-real zeros of \(F_\lambda\). I read the statement and the surrounding paragraphs and not the definition of the region. Target: the consequence side of the Hermite–Biehler lead. Answers. It would say where the consequence side is known unconditionally, which the structure-functions entry left open. The definition was then read: \(R_\lambda(\kappa)\) consists of the \(z\) with \(|\Re z|\) at least the height of the first zero of \(\zeta\) to the right of \(1/2+\lambda\) and at distance more than \(\kappa\) from the height of every such zero. So non-real zeros of \(F_\lambda\) occur only within \(\kappa\) of heights of zeros of \(\zeta\) beyond \(1/2+\lambda\). Test. Falsified for this formulation: the theorem goes from the location of zeros of \(\zeta\) to the reality of zeros of \(F_\lambda\), the direction that supplies no premise; the direction the paper leaves as a question, whether a zero beyond \(1/2+\lambda\) forces a non-real zero of \(F_\lambda\), and the proof are not examined.
  • The growth criterion of Bombieri and Lagarias. Named source: E. Bombieri and J. C. Lagarias, Complements to Li's criterion, J. Number Theory 77 (1999), Theorem 1, read in the supplied copy: for a multiset with their convergence condition, all real parts at most \(1/2\) if and only if the sums \(\sum_\rho\Re[1-(1-1/\rho)^{-n}]\) are nonnegative, if and only if for every \(\epsilon>0\) they are at least \(-c(\epsilon)e^{\epsilon n}\). Theorem 2, an arithmetic formula for the coefficients, is located and not read in detail. Target: a one-sided bound as premise. Answers. It would replace positivity by a growth bound. Test. Falsified as a weaker premise, for Theorem 1 only (Theorem 2 is unread in detail): by prop:li-coefficient-threshold an off-line pair at height \(\gamma\) and distance \(\delta\) grows like \(e^{n\delta/\gamma^2}\), so the bound for one \(\epsilon\) concerns only zeros with \(\delta/\gamma^2\) above about \(\epsilon\), a bounded range of heights, and the bound for every \(\epsilon\) is the statement at all heights; no part of it is weaker than a zero-free statement at the corresponding heights.
  • The structure function of Lagarias. Named source: J. C. Lagarias, Hilbert spaces of entire functions and Dirichlet L-functions (the supplied copy; first two pages read as images): \(E(z)=\xi(\tfrac12-iz)+\xi'(\tfrac12-iz)\) is the structure function of a de Branges space if and only if the hypothesis holds, and the paper says the general theorems of de Branges fail to apply to \(\xi(1-iz)\) by a result of Conrey and Li. Answers. It would give an operator whose positivity is the goal. Test. Falsified for this formulation only, on two pages read (the paper's operator and its coefficient functions are unread): the defining inequality for this \(E\) is \(\Re\xi'/\xi>0\) on the half-plane, by the computation verified in the structure-functions entry, which is the half-plane sign form already recorded as the zero-free statement.

Architecture review

Necessary target. For each height \(\tau_0\) and each \(\sigma_0\in(1/2,1)\): an unconditional inequality that a zero at \(\sigma_0+i\tau_0\) violates. Turán's form is a sufficient condition, not a necessary one: a bound for \(Z(\tau_0,N_1,N_2)\) on all dyadic ranges \(N\ge\tau_0^{a}\) with saving \(\tau_0^{-b}\), \(1-b^3/a^2\le\sigma_0\); on the recalled ceiling no pair satisfies it for \(\sigma_0\le3/4\). Downstream. Through the proved statements nothing changes: prop:shifted-structure-criterion and prop:log-derivative-sign-criteria make the half-plane forms equivalent to zero-freeness, and lem:strip-positivity-obstruction limits certificates; through the read theorem the target for a pair \((a,b)\) gives the segment \(\sigma\ge1-b^3/a^2\) at that height, which is weaker than the goal for every pair: by the ceiling under Independent alternative, which rests on a recalled bound, the stated theorem returns at best \(3/4\).

Extra requirements. The route asked for criteria equivalent to the goal and for methods with no hypothesis on the other zeros; both are more than the target. Turán's theorem needs neither: it is one-directional, local in the height, and its premise is a bound on sums over primes, on every dyadic range above a power of the height. What it adds as a requirement is the range of lengths, all \(N\ge\tau_0^{a}\), and the lossy conversion.

Independent alternative. Before the route was revealed, the review's medium reviewer was given the goal, the two imports, the six read statements and the four groups of proved results, with no registered open conjecture on the route to list, and asked for a different architecture (brief and reply). Its verdict: none found that is both new and able to reach the line. Its main point is a ceiling for the read theorem, which I checked as arithmetic: if the sums satisfy \(|Z|\ll N^{\theta}\) up to logarithms (what a zero-free region \(\sigma>\theta\) near the height gives, by the explicit formula as the reviewer recalls it; not read here), the hypothesis holds exactly for \(b=a(1-\theta)\), \(b\le1\), and then \(b^3/a^2=a(1-\theta)^3\le(1-\theta)^2\). So the theorem returns \(\sigma\ge1-(1-\theta)^2\): from \(\theta=1/2\) it returns \(3/4\), from \(7/8\) it returns \(63/64\). As stated it cannot exclude a zero with \(1/2<\sigma\le3/4\) even from the strongest premise that is true under the hypothesis, the recalled reason being that zeros on the line make \(Z\) of size \(N^{1/2}\). Its two proposals: a sharpened local converse with exponent \(b/a\) in place of \(b^3/a^2\), which it expects to need a power-sum bound without the cluster loss; and a statement that no collision of zeros occurs in a short forward time of the heat flow at height \(T\), which needs lower bounds for gaps. Its reason none goes further: every listed tool is linear in the zeros through the explicit formula. Compared with the route: the first proposal is the picked lead with its loss named, and it sends the decisive test to the proof of the theorem rather than to a table of exponents.

Rank normalization. Not a dimension count. The analogous question is whether the premise is measured in the same normalization as the conclusion: Turán's saving is relative to the trivial bound \(N\) on each dyadic range, and the conclusion is a region, so the comparison is relative by construction.

Decision. Core. The open statement stays the one worked on; what changes is the scale on which work is measured. The next cycles place the recorded mechanisms and imports on the exponents \((a,b)\) of the read theorem, starting with the picked lead.

Decisive test. Read the proof of Theorem 33.2 in the supplied book (Sections 33.5 to 33.10) and the stronger forms it announces in Sections 34 to 36, and answer: at which step does the exponent \(b^3/a^2\) arise, and is that step the second main theorem applied to the zeros near the height, so that the loss is the cluster loss already recorded, or something else? Outcomes: if the loss is the power-sum loss over about \(\log\tau_0\) zeros, the sharpened converse reduces to the open cluster question of the power-sum item and the two items merge into one named statement; if it arises elsewhere (the range of lengths, the smoothing), that step is the new object and the entry states what a lossless version would need; if the loss comes from the worst-case count of zeros in a short window, the missing input is a local count and the entry says which; if a later section already has exponent \(b/a\) or reaches the line from square-root cancellation, the route's premise becomes a bound on \(Z\). The parameter choice at the end of the proof is read first. Trivial cases. The quantity compared is the exponent of the region as a function of \((a,b)\). It is unchanged by the power of the logarithm in the hypothesis. The trivial instances \(b\to0\), and \(a\to\infty\) with \(b\) fixed, give no region in every version, so no trivial instance produces any of the outcomes; they are distinguished by the proof's steps and not by a value. Power. Recorded limits on the test's object: lem:prime-side-form-bound gives only the trivial bound for the prime sum by elementary means; the bandwidth reading records that a saving \(T^{-\varepsilon}\) in a second moment would move the Gram mechanism; neither is on Turán's scale yet. The test is a reading with arithmetic and no computation; its outcomes differ in what the next entries measure. Record search. The earlier reviews' tests (the second stage as a map of the assumed bound; the Harnack obstruction) are answered by the family-optimum and strip-positivity entries and are different objects.

11 October 2026 — Where Turán's local implication loses: its proof is read; of its four constraints three are linear and come from the choice of a small disk, one of them giving width at most \(1/1600\) for every pair of exponents, and the fourth is the second main theorem applied to Jensen's count of zeros in that disk; the reviewer's unverified derivation is that the linear constant and the count trade against each other

A published theorem and its proof read as page images, with the two sources of loss located; arithmetic of its constants; no result about zeta zeros. Turán implication loss. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its hard limit; the reading is recorded in Working mathematical context

Chosen. This entry takes the picked lead of the third review ahead of the queue's head, because the turn guidance requires the review's decisive test in the next research cycle; it bears on off-line exclusion as the one read implication from a statement about primes to the absence of a zero at a point.

Best outcome. Written before the reading: at the goal's own parameters the stated theorem gives nothing (the review's ceiling \(3/4\), which rests on a recollection; the reading below replaces it by a read constraint). The best outcome of this cycle is to name the step of the proof that loses, so that the missing implication's second half becomes one stated inequality; a later section with a lossless form would be more, and would turn the route's premise into a bound for the prime sums.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. In the proof of the local implication, at which step is the region reduced below what the attenuation of a single zero would allow?

Architecture test. The third review's test asked where the exponent arises, with four outcomes. Outcome: two kinds of step. The first kind is linear, \(1-\beta^*\le b/(1600a)\), and comes from keeping \(\mu^*\) small in the estimates of the Dirichlet-series side. The second is the review's first and fourth outcomes together: the second main theorem is applied to all zeros in a disk of radius \(8\mu^*\) about \(s_0=1+\mu^*+i\tau_0\), whose number is bounded by Jensen's formula from the growth of the function, about \(100A_1(a/b)(1-\beta^*)\log\tau_0\); its loss must stay below the saving, which gives \((1-\beta^*)\log\frac{b}{A_1(1-\beta^*)}\lesssim b^2/(300A_1a)\). The later sections are not read in this cycle. So the sharpened converse does reduce to a cluster question, but about the zeros near the line \(\sigma=1\) inside one small disk, and in addition it needs the linear constraint with a constant near one. By the reviewer's derivation, listed below and not verified, the two are not independent: the constant can approach one only with a large \(\mu^*\), hence a large disk and a stronger hypothesis on the other zeros.

New mathematics. None of mine beyond arithmetic. Read: the statement and the proof of the theorem for a class of Dirichlet series (the book's Theorem 33.3, of which the review's theorem is the case of \(\zeta\)), registered as thm:turan-class-local-implication with status established, read and not audited.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The second half of the missing implication now has a stated form: from a saving \(\tau_0^{-b}\) for the prime sums on lengths at least \(\tau_0^{a}\), exclude a zero at \(\beta^*+i\tau_0\) when \(1-\beta^*<c\,b/a\), with \(c\) near one, without the loss from the other zeros in the disk. Neither part is proved, and the first half, the saving itself, is untouched. Converted. No recollection is needed: the last constraint with \(A_2\ge A_3\) gives \(A_4\le1/1600\) for every pair of exponents and every growth exponent. So the theorem as proved never gives a region wider than \(\sigma>1-1/1600\): nothing at the goal's parameters. (As an illustration only, with the values \(A_1=1/2\), \(A_2=2\), \(A_3=1\), the four constraints in the theorem's order allow about \(0.00039\), \(e^{-4.5}/8\), \(9/500\) and \(1/3200\); the last binds.) Bearing. None at the goal's parameters; the cycle was spent on it because the review's test required it and it replaces a guessed location of the loss by a read one.

General statement. thm:turan-class-local-implication, for every function of the class with constants \(A_1,A_2,A_3\) and every large \(\tau_0\); a published statement, not a result of this notebook.

The statement read

P. Turán, On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications (1984), Section 33.6, read as page images from the copy supplied by the user. The class \(\Delta(A_1,A_2,A_3)\): (I) \(f(s)=1+\sum_{n\ge2}a_nn^{-s}\) converges for \(\sigma>1\) and satisfies \(|f(s)|\ge\log^{-10}(3+|t|)\) there; (II) \(f\) is meromorphic in \(\sigma\ge1/2\) and \(|f(s)|\le|t|^{A_1(1-\sigma)}\log^{10}|t|\) for \(\sigma\ge1/2\), \(|t|\ge3\) (so the scan reads; taken literally for all \(\sigma\ge1/2\) the class would be empty, the bound tending to zero at \(\sigma=2\), so the intended range is \(1/2\le\sigma\le1\), where alone the proof uses it); (III) with \(f'/f(s)=\sum b_nn^{-s}\), \(|b_n|\le c\log n\). Theorem (thm:turan-class-local-implication). If \(\big|\sum_{N_1\le n\le N_2}b_ne^{-i\tau_0\log n}\big|<cN\log^{100}N/|\tau_0|^{A_3}\) for all \(\tau_0^{A_2}\le N\le N_1<N_2\le2N\), with \(\tau_0\) larger than a constant depending on \(A_1,A_2,A_3\), then \(f(s)\ne0\) on \(\sigma>1-A_4\), \(t=\tau_0\), where \(0<A_4<1/2\) and, with \(A_2\ge A_3\) assumed without loss of generality, \(A_4\) is the greatest \(x\) satisfying \[x\log\frac{A_3}{A_1x}\le\frac1{300A_1}\frac{A_3^2}{A_2},\quad x\le\frac{A_3}{4A_2}\exp\Big(-\frac{4A_2+A_3}{A_2}\Big),\quad x\le\frac{9}{1000}\frac{A_3}{A_1},\quad x\le\frac1{1600}\frac{A_3}{A_2}.\] The scan is small in places; the constants above are as I read them, and the exponent of the logarithm in (I) and (II) is the least certain reading.

The proof, as read

Suppose a zero \(s^*=\beta^*+i\tau_0\) with \(\beta^*>1-A_4\). Put \(s_0=1+\mu^*+i\tau_0\) with \(\mu^*=\max(\log^{-1/2}\tau_0,\,4(A_2/A_3)(1-\beta^*))\), and take an order \(\nu\) of differentiation with \(A_2\log\tau_0\le\nu\le2A_2\log\tau_0\). (i) From the growth condition and a lemma of the book's appendix, the \(\nu\)-th derivative of \(f'/f\) at \(s_0\) is, up to sign and \(\nu!\), the sum of \((s_0-s_j)^{-\nu-1}\) over the zeros \(s_j\) within \(8\mu^*\) of \(s_0\), with an error \(c(\nu+1)!(8\mu^*)^{-\nu-1}\log\tau_0\). (ii) From the Dirichlet series, the same derivative is \(\sum b_n\log^{\nu}n\cdot n^{-s_0}\); the part \(n\le\tau_0^{A_2}\) is bounded trivially, and the part above by partial summation on dyadic ranges using the hypothesis, where the lower bound on \(\nu\) puts the weight on \(n\ge\tau_0^{A_2}\). (iii) Together, after multiplying by \(|s^*-s_0|^{\nu+1}=(1+\mu^*-\beta^*)^{\nu+1}\): \(\big|\sum_j\big(\frac{s^*-s_0}{s_j-s_0}\big)^{\nu+1}\big|<c\log^{151}\tau_0\cdot\tau_0^{-A_3}(1+\frac{1-\beta^*}{\mu^*})^{\nu+1}\le c\log^{151}\tau_0\cdot\tau_0^{-A_3/2}\), using \((1+\frac{A_3}{4A_2})^{2A_2\log\tau_0}\le\tau_0^{A_3/2}\). (iv) The left side is a power sum of the fixed numbers \((s_0-s^*)/(s_0-s_j)\), the largest of modulus at least one (the zero \(s^*\) itself gives one). The second main theorem with \(m=A_2\log\tau_0\) and \(N\) the number of zeros in the disk gives some \(\nu+1\in(m,m+N]\) with left side at least \((N/(8e(m+N)))^N\). (v) \(N\) is bounded by Jensen's formula on radius \(16\mu^*\), using the growth condition down to \(\sigma=1-15\mu^*\) and the lower bound of condition (I) at \(s_0\): \(N\le100A_1(A_2/A_3)(1-\beta^*)\log\tau_0+c\sqrt{\log\tau_0}\). Then the left side exceeds \(\tau_0^{-A_5}\) up to a factor \(\exp(-c\log^{3/4}\tau_0)\), with \(A_5=100\frac{A_1A_2}{A_3}(1-\beta^*)\log\big(\frac{A_3}{A_1}\frac1{1-\beta^*}\big)\), and the first constraint gives \(A_5\le A_3/3\): a contradiction with (iii) for large \(\tau_0\) (the book's last page of the proof, read).

Where the region is lost

  • The three linear constraints. As the reviewer attributed them from the scan, and as the pages I read show at the cited steps: the factor \(4\) in \(\mu^*\) comes from step (iii), so that \((1+(1-\beta^*)/\mu^*)^{\nu+1}\) costs at most half the saving. The second constraint makes \(\mu^*<\exp(-(4A_2+A_3)/A_2)\), which makes the trivial part \(n\le\tau_0^{A_2}\) of step (ii) negligible. The fourth makes \(\mu^*\le1/400\), used in the tail of the part above \(\tau_0^{A_2}\). The third, \(x\le9A_3/(1000A_1)\), keeps \(N\le A_2\log\tau_0\) so that \(\nu\) stays in its range. The radius \(8\) serves the error of step (i): \(8^{-A_2\log\tau_0}<\tau_0^{-A_3}\). The second constraint is always implied by the fourth, since \(e^{-5}/4>1/1600\). So \(1-\beta^*\le A_3/(1600A_2)\) is a consequence of choosing \(\mu^*\) tiny, which the proof does to keep the disk small.
  • The other zeros in the disk, quadratic. Step (iv) pays \((N/(8e(m+N)))^N\) for all \(N\) zeros in the disk, and step (v) knows \(N\) only from the growth of \(f\). This is the first constraint. Arithmetic of mine: if the disk were known to hold at most \(K=\kappa\log\tau_0\) zeros, the factor would be at least \(\tau_0^{-\kappa\log(16eA_2/\kappa)}\) for \(\kappa\le A_2\), whose exponent tends to zero with \(\kappa\); so with a local count \(o(\log\tau_0)\), in a disk whose radius is never below \(8\log^{-1/2}\tau_0\), the first and third constraints would disappear and only the other linear ones would remain. I have not redone the proof under that hypothesis.
  • Which zeros. The disk about \(1+\mu^*+i\tau_0\) of radius \(8\mu^*\) reaches only \(\sigma\ge1-7\mu^*\). The zeros that cost are therefore zeros far from the critical line and near the hypothetical one, at heights within \(8\mu^*\): a cluster of off-line zeros in the sense of this notebook's open cluster case, not the zeros on the line. No unconditional bound for their number in one disk better than Jensen's is known to me; density estimates count such zeros on average over heights and are not read here.

Degenerate cases. \(f\equiv1\): every \(b_n=0\), the hypothesis holds and the conclusion is true and empty. \(A_3\to0\): every constraint forces \(A_4\to0\), no region. A zero alone in its disk (\(N=1\)): step (iv) loses only \(1/(8e(m+1))\), a power of \(\log\tau_0\), and the first constraint is not needed; the linear ones remain.

Nonvacuity. The book states (Section 33.6) that the theorem is proved in order to obtain its Theorem 33.2 for \(\zeta\), so \(\zeta\) is in the class for suitable constants, with condition (II) read on \(1/2\le\sigma\le1\); this rests on the book's announcement alone, and I did not verify the three conditions for \(\zeta\).

Sub-ideas

  • Redo the proof under a local count: assuming at most \(K\) zeros of \(f\) in the disk of radius \(8\mu^*\) about \(s_0\), track every constant and state the resulting region; decide the best constant \(c\) in \(1-\beta^*<c\,b/a\) that the method allows as a function of \(\mu^*\) (with the radius and the range of \(\nu\)), verifying or refuting the reviewer's derivation that \(c\to1\) only as \(\mu^*\to\infty\), and state the trade between the constant and the size of the disk in which the count is needed as one statement.
  • Read Sections 34 to 36 of the book for the stronger forms it announces, in particular whether the count of zeros in the disk is handled differently there.
  • Read the book's Theorem 33.1 and its proof (Section 33.14) for the exact bound a zero-free rectangle gives for the prime sums, to replace the review's recalled ceiling by a read statement.

Follow-up. Continuing: the lead's question, where the proof loses, is answered by reading; its application to the open statement remains, as the first sub-idea, which needs no further source and is the next attempt on this item. Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • The constant can approach one only by giving up locality: with \(s_0=1+\mu\), \(\nu=\lambda\log\tau_0\), \(r=x/\mu\), \(x=1-\beta^*\), the zero's term against the saving needs \(\lambda\log(1+r)<b\), the trivial part is a lower Gamma tail needing \(\log(1+r)<t-1-\log t\) with \(t=a\mu/\lambda<1\), so \(x=(b/a)\,t\,r/\log(1+r)\); \(c\to1\) as \(r\to0\), and \(c\approx0.33\) at \(r=1\). The cost is \(\mu=x/r\to\infty\): the zero must be nearly the rightmost in a window of heights of order \(\mu\). Record. 11 October 2026 — The local implication redone with explicit parameters: an inequality between the zeros in a disk and the sums of the coefficients of \(f'/f\) at one height; under a count \(O(\log\tau_0)\) of zeros and a growth bound in a fixed disk, a saving \(\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}\) on all lengths at least \(\tau_0^{a}\), for \(a\) large, excludes zeros at that height with \(1-\beta<\vartheta\), for an abstract function; an example shows the constant is sharp at \(\vartheta=1\); for \(\zeta\) the premise as stated is false because of the pole, and the repair is not made

Working proof of an inequality, relying on one published theorem read and not audited; a conditional corollary; no result about zeta zeros. Long-sum local converse. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its hard limit; the result is recorded in Working mathematical context

Chosen. This entry continues the picked lead of the third review ahead of the queue's head: its previous entry named a next attempt that needs no source, and it bears on off-line exclusion as the second half of the missing implication.

Owed attempt. The item's previous entry, Turán implication loss, named its first sub-idea: redo the proof under a count of zeros in the disk, track constants, and decide the constant in \(1-\beta^*<c\,b/a\). Made here, with the outcome below.

Best outcome. Written before the work: at the goal's own parameters, the best outcome is an implication reaching the line, from a bound for the prime sums at one height with exponents \(b/a\) near \(1/2\) to the absence of zeros at that height to the right of the line, with hypotheses on the other zeros that are known or stated. It would leave the bound itself, the first half, untouched.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. With the parameters of the proof left free, what inequality does the argument give, how does the loss from the other zeros in the disk depend on them, and what is the best constant?

New mathematics. lem:local-zero-sum-inequality: an explicit inequality, for any function analytic in a disk about \(s_0=1+\mu+i\tau_0\) whose logarithmic derivative is a Dirichlet series, between a power sum over its zeros in the half disk and the sums of the coefficients at height \(\tau_0\). cor:long-sum-local-converse: under a count \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) of zeros and a growth bound \(M_0\log\tau_0\) in a fixed disk, the saving \(\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}\) on lengths at least \(\tau_0^{a}\) excludes a zero at \(1-x+i\tau_0\) for every \(x<\vartheta\), once \(a\) is large; the loss from the other zeros is of order \(\kappa\log a\) against margins of order \(a\). An example shows that at \(\vartheta=1\) the range \(x<\vartheta\) cannot be enlarged; for \(\vartheta<1\) sharpness is not shown. Both statements are about an abstract function satisfying the hypotheses; \(\zeta\) does not satisfy the corollary's premise as stated (below).

Goal progress. Unchanged. The corollary is proved for an abstract function. It does not apply to \(\zeta\) as stated, a point my draft missed and the reviewer found: the pole at \(s=1\) gives \(\sum_{y<n\le y'}\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau_0}\) the main term \((y'^{\,1-i\tau_0}-y^{1-i\tau_0})/(1-i\tau_0)\), of modulus about \(2y/\tau_0\) at \(y'=y(1+\pi/\tau_0)\), so, granted that the remainder is smaller for very long sums (the prime number theorem, recalled and not read here), no saving beyond \(\tau_0^{-1}\) is possible there and (H) with \(S=C\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}\) fails once \(\vartheta a>1\), while the corollary needs \(a\) large. This agrees with the published theorem, which is stated for \(0<b\le1\). The reviewer's repair, to remove the pole's term before applying the lemma, is not made here. The two local hypotheses for \(\zeta\) (a count of zeros and a growth bound in a fixed disk) are untested hypotheses in this entry, as is my expectation that a repaired premise is of the same strength as the conclusion. Converted. Withdrawn before commit: the draft converted the corollary to prime sums with \(\vartheta=1/2-\epsilon\); for \(\Lambda(n)\) that premise is false. The Best outcome is not reached. Bearing.
