General statement. lem:local-zero-sum-inequality, for all parameters in its statement; cor:long-sum-local-converse, for all \(0<x<\vartheta\le1\) and all sufficiently large \(a\) and \(\tau_0\).

Dependencies

One published theorem, read as page images in the user's supplied scan and not audited: Turán's second main theorem (On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications, 1984, Section 8.1): for complex \(z_j\) with \(1=|z_1|\ge\dots\ge|z_n|\), complex \(b_j\) and an integer \(m\ge0\), \(\max_{m+1\le k\le m+n}|\sum_jb_jz_j^{k}|\ge2\big(\frac{n}{8e(m+n)}\big)^{n}\min_j|b_1+\dots+b_j|\). It is used with all \(b_j=1\), where the minimum is one. Everything else is proved below.

Statement

Lemma (local zero-sum inequality). lem:local-zero-sum-inequality. Let \(\tau_0\) be real, \(\mu>0\), \(s_0=1+\mu+i\tau_0\), \(R>0\), \(M\ge0\), \(B,S,A\ge0\), \(X\ge2\). Assume: (D) \(|b_n|\le B\log n\) for \(n\ge2\) and \(F(s)=\sum_{n\ge2}b_nn^{-s}\) for \(\sigma>1\); (Z) \(f\) is analytic on the closed disk \(|s-s_0|\le R\), \(f(s_0)\ne0\), \(|f(s)|\le e^{M}|f(s_0)|\) on it, and \(f'/f=F\) near \(s_0\); its zeros in \(|s-s_0|\le R/2\), with multiplicity, are \(\rho_1,\dots,\rho_K\), \(K\ge1\), and \(d=\min_j|s_0-\rho_j|\); (H) \(\big|\sum_{y<n\le y'}b_nn^{-i\tau_0}\big|\le Sy\log^{A}y\) for all \(X\le y<y'\le2y\). Let \(m\ge1\) be an integer with \(\mu\log X\le m+1\). Then there is an integer \(k\) with \(m<k\le m+K\) and \[2\Big(\frac{K}{8e(m+K)}\Big)^{K}d^{-k}\le\frac{E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}}{(k-1)!}+2Mk\Big(\frac2R\Big)^{k},\] \[E_{\mathrm{low}}=B\Big[(\log X)^{k+1}X^{-\mu}+\Big(\frac{k}{e(1+\mu)}\Big)^{k}\Big],\qquad E_{\mathrm{up}}=3S\,\Gamma(k+A)\,\mu^{-k-A}\Big(\frac1{\log2}+3\mu\Big).\]

Corollary (long-sum local converse). cor:long-sum-local-converse. Let \(0<x<\vartheta\le1\), and \(B,C,A\ge0\). There are \(r>0\) and \(t\in(0,1)\), depending only on \(x/\vartheta\), such that with \(\mu=x/r\) and any \(\kappa>0\), \(M_0\ge0\) the following holds for all \(a\ge a_0(x,\vartheta,\kappa)\) and all \(\tau_0\ge\tau_*(x,\vartheta,\kappa,M_0,a,A,B,C)\). If (D) holds, (Z) holds with \(R=4(\mu+x)\), \(M\le M_0\log\tau_0\) and \(K\le\kappa\log\tau_0\), and (H) holds with \(X=\tau_0^{a}\) and \(S=C\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}\), then \(f(1-x+i\tau_0)\ne0\).

Proof of the lemma

The zeros. Put \(g(s)=f(s)\prod_j(s-\rho_j)^{-1}\), analytic on the disk. On \(|s-s_0|=R\), \(|s-\rho_j|\ge R/2\ge|s_0-\rho_j|\), so \(|g(s)/g(s_0)|=|f(s)/f(s_0)|\prod_j|s_0-\rho_j|/|s-\rho_j|\le e^{M}\), and by the maximum principle this holds on the whole disk. \(g\) has no zero in \(|s-s_0|\le R/2\), so \(G=\log(g/g(s_0))\) is analytic in \(|s-s_0|<R/2\), \(G(s_0)=0\), \(\Re G\le M\). Write \(G=\sum_{j\ge1}c_j(s-s_0)^j\). For \(0<\varrho<R/2\) and \(j\ge1\), \(c_j\varrho^{j}=\frac1\pi\int_0^{2\pi}\Re G(s_0+\varrho e^{i\phi})e^{-ij\phi}d\phi=-\frac1\pi\int_0^{2\pi}(M-\Re G)e^{-ij\phi}d\phi\), and \(\frac1\pi\int(M-\Re G)=2M\) because the mean of \(\Re G\) is \(\Re G(s_0)=0\); so \(|c_j|\le2M\varrho^{-j}\), and letting \(\varrho\to R/2\), \(|c_j|\le2M(2/R)^{j}\). Since \(G'=f'/f-\sum_j(s-\rho_j)^{-1}\) and \(G^{(k)}(s_0)=k!\,c_k\), for \(k\ge2\): \[\Big|F^{(k-1)}(s_0)-(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}\Big|\le2M\,k!\,(2/R)^{k}.\]

The power sum. The numbers \(z_j=d/(s_0-\rho_j)\) have moduli at most one, with equality for a nearest zero. By the second main theorem with \(b_j=1\) there is \(k\in(m,m+K]\) with \(|\sum_jz_j^{k}|\ge2(K/(8e(m+K)))^{K}\), that is \(|\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}|\ge2(K/(8e(m+K)))^{K}d^{-k}\). As \(m\ge1\), \(k\ge2\).

The Dirichlet series. \(F^{(k-1)}(s_0)=(-1)^{k-1}\sum_nb_n\log^{k-1}n\cdot n^{-s_0}\). For \(n\le X\): the modulus of that part is at most \(B\sum_{2\le n\le X}\varphi_1(n)\), \(\varphi_1(y)=\log^{k}y\cdot y^{-1-\mu}\). \(\varphi_1(e^{u})=u^{k}e^{-(1+\mu)u}\) increases and then decreases, so the sum is at most \(\int_1^{X}\varphi_1+\max\varphi_1\). \(\int_1^{X}\varphi_1(y)dy=\int_0^{\log X}u^{k}e^{-\mu u}du\), whose integrand increases up to \(u=k/\mu\ge\log X\) (as \(\mu\log X\le m+1\le k\)), so it is at most \((\log X)^{k+1}X^{-\mu}\); and \(\max\varphi_1=(k/(e(1+\mu)))^{k}\). This is \(E_{\mathrm{low}}\). For \(n>X\): on a block \((y,2y]\), \(y=2^{i}X\), with \(\varphi(v)=\log^{k-1}v\cdot v^{-1-\mu}\) and \(C(v)=\sum_{y<n\le v}b_nn^{-i\tau_0}\), partial summation gives \(\sum_{y<n\le2y}b_nn^{-i\tau_0}\varphi(n)=C(2y)\varphi(2y)-\int_y^{2y}C\varphi'\), of modulus at most \(Sy\log^{A}y\,(\varphi(2y)+\int_y^{2y}|\varphi'|)\le3Sy\log^{A}y\sup_{[y,2y]}\varphi\), since \(\varphi\) is unimodal. For \(v\in[y,2y]\), \(y\log^{A}y\,\varphi(v)\le v\log^{A}v\,\varphi(v)=\Psi(\log v)\), \(\Psi(u)=u^{k-1+A}e^{-\mu u}\). So the part \(n>X\) is at most \(3S\sum_{i\ge0}\sup_{I_i}\Psi\) over consecutive intervals \(I_i\) of length \(\log2\). \(\Psi\) is unimodal: an interval on which \(\Psi\) increases and whose successor is also in the increasing range has \(\sup_{I_i}\Psi\le\frac1{\log2}\int_{I_{i+1}}\Psi\); likewise on the decreasing side with the predecessor; the targets are distinct and at most three intervals are left over. Hence the sum is at most \(\frac1{\log2}\int_0^\infty\Psi+3\sup\Psi\), with \(\int_0^\infty\Psi=\Gamma(k+A)\mu^{-k-A}\) and \(\sup\Psi=((k-1+A)/(e\mu))^{k-1+A}\le\Gamma(k+A)\mu^{-k+1-A}\). This is \(E_{\mathrm{up}}\).

Conclusion. \((k-1)!\,|\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}|\le|F^{(k-1)}(s_0)|+2Mk!(2/R)^{k}\le E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}+2Mk!(2/R)^{k}\); divide by \((k-1)!\) and use the power-sum bound. \(\square\)

Proof of the corollary

Let \(h(t)=t-1-\log t\), positive and decreasing on \((0,1)\). As \(r\to0\) and \(t\to1\), \(tr/\log(1+r)\to1\); choose \(t\in(0,1)\) with \(t>x/\vartheta\), then \(r>0\) so small that \(q:=\frac{x}{\vartheta}\cdot\frac{\log(1+r)}{tr}<1\), \(\log(1+r)<h(t)\), and \(r<1/\mu\), which with \(\mu=x/r\) is \(x<1\). Put \(\lambda=a\mu/t\), \(L=\log\tau_0\), \(m=\lceil\lambda L\rceil\); then \(\mu\log X=\mu aL=t\lambda L\le m+1\). Suppose \(s^*=1-x+i\tau_0\) is a zero. It lies in the half disk, \(|s_0-s^*|=\mu+x\le R/2\), so \(d\le\mu+x=\mu(1+r)\), and the lemma applies with some \(k\in(m,m+K]\), so \(\lambda L\le k\le(\lambda+\kappa)L+1\). Multiply the lemma's inequality by \(\mu^{k}(1+r)^{k}\). Left side: at least \(2(K/(8e(m+K)))^{K}\); \(K\log(8e(m+K)/K)\) increases with \(K\), so this is at least \(\tau_0^{-\kappa\log(8e(\lambda+\kappa+1/L)/\kappa)}\). Right side, term by term, using \((k-1)!\ge(k/e)^{k}/k\) and \((1+r)^{k}\le(1+r)\tau_0^{(\lambda+\kappa)\log(1+r)}\):

  • \(E_{\mathrm{up}}\mu^{k}/(k-1)!\le3C\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}(k+A)^{A}\mu^{-A}(1/\log2+3\mu)\): a power of \(L\) times \(\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}\).
  • The first part of \(E_{\mathrm{low}}\): \(B(\mu\log X)^{k}\log X\cdot X^{-\mu}/(k-1)!\le Bk\log X\exp(-k\,h(t_k))\) with \(t_k=\mu\log X/k\le t\), and \(h(t_k)\ge h(t)\), \(k\ge\lambda L\): at most a power of \(L\) times \(\tau_0^{-\lambda h(t)}\).
  • The second part: \(B(k\mu/(e(1+\mu)))^{k}/(k-1)!\le Bk(1+1/\mu)^{-k}\le Bk\,\tau_0^{-\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)}\).
  • The last term: \(2Mk(2\mu(1+r)/R)^{k}=2Mk\,2^{-k}\), since \(R=4\mu(1+r)\): at most a power of \(L\) times \(\tau_0^{-\lambda\log2}\); this one is not multiplied by a growing power of \(1+r\) beyond what is written.

So a zero forces, up to powers of \(L\), \(\tau_0^{-\kappa\log(8e(\lambda+\kappa+1)/\kappa)}\le\tau_0^{(\lambda+\kappa)\log(1+r)}\big(\tau_0^{-\vartheta a}+\tau_0^{-\lambda h(t)}+\tau_0^{-\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)}\big)+\tau_0^{-\lambda\log2}\). Now \(\lambda\log(1+r)=a\frac{x}{rt}\log(1+r)=q\vartheta a\) with \(q<1\); \(\lambda h(t)-\lambda\log(1+r)\) and \(\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)-\lambda\log(1+r)\) are positive multiples of \(a\); and \(\kappa\log(1+r)+\kappa\log(8e(\lambda+\kappa+1)/\kappa)=O_\kappa(\log a)\) for fixed \(x,\vartheta\). For \(a\ge a_0\) every exponent on the right is smaller than the one on the left by a fixed positive amount, and the inequality fails for large \(\tau_0\). \(\square\)

The constant cannot exceed one. \(f(s)=1-2^{-s}\) is entire with zeros \(2\pi ij/\log2\), and \(f'/f=\log2\sum_{j\ge1}2^{-js}\): \(b_{2^{j}}=\log2\), other \(b_n=0\), so (D) holds with \(B=1\). At \(\tau_0=2\pi j_0/\log2\) every \(2^{-ij\tau_0}=1\), and a block \((y,2y]\) holds one power of two, so the sums in (H) are at most \(\log2\), which is \(Sy\) for all \(y\ge X=\tau_0^{a}\) with \(S=\log2\cdot\tau_0^{-a}\): \(\vartheta=1\). The zeros in any fixed disk are boundedly many and the growth is bounded. The corollary gives no zero with \(x<1\) at these heights; there is one with \(x=1\). So at \(\vartheta=1\) the range cannot be enlarged. For \(\vartheta<1\) the example gives only that the constant is at most \(1/\vartheta\); a shifted example \(1-2^{1-\vartheta-s}\) violates (D), and no sharpness example for \(\vartheta<1\) is given. Unused hypotheses: (H) is used only at \(y=2^{i}X\), and \(\vartheta\le1\) only to have \(x<1\).

Degenerate cases. \(K=0\) is excluded by the lemma's hypothesis; with no zero in the half disk there is nothing to bound. \(M=0\): then \(G\equiv0\) and the last term vanishes, consistent. \(x\ge\vartheta\): the corollary says nothing, and the example shows it cannot. \(x\to0\): \(\mu=x/r\to0\) for fixed \(r\), the regime of the published proof. \(S=0\) (all tail sums vanish): the lemma still bounds the zeros through \(E_{\mathrm{low}}\) alone.

Nonvacuity. The example above satisfies all hypotheses of the lemma with \(B=1\), \(A=0\), \(X=\tau_0^{a}\), \(S=\log2\cdot\tau_0^{-a}\), any \(\mu,R\), \(K\) the number of zeros in the half disk (at least one when \(R/2\ge1+\mu\)), and of the corollary with \(\vartheta=1\), every \(x<1\); there the conclusion is true and the bound \(x<\vartheta\) is attained in the limit.

Where the published loss went

For an abstract function only; see Goal progress for \(\zeta\). In the published proof, as read in the previous entry, \(\mu^*\) is tiny, the order of differentiation is about \(a\log\tau_0\) with \(a\) fixed, and the count of zeros is taken from Jensen's formula, proportional to \(\mu^*\log\tau_0\); the loss \(\kappa\log(\lambda/\kappa)\) is then compared with a margin of order \(b\). Here the margins are multiples of \(a\) and the loss grows like \(\kappa\log a\): long sums pay for the other zeros. The price is in the premise, which must hold with the same relative exponent on all lengths above \(\tau_0^{a}\) for a large \(a\), and in the disk, which is of fixed size \(4(\mu+x)\) with \(\mu=x/r\) large when the constant is near one. For \(x<1/2\) the half disk about \(1+\mu+i\tau_0\) of radius \(2(\mu+x)\) contains zeros on the critical line; they are among the \(K\) zeros and cost nothing beyond the count.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the book's converse (its Theorem 33.1, Section 33.14) and decide whether the premise of the corollary for all large \(a\) is equivalent to a zero-free region at that height, with which exponents; then state the equivalence or the gap.
  • Repair the premise for \(\zeta\): remove the pole's contribution (the reviewer's proposal: state (H) for \(\Lambda(n)-1\) and bound the extra term \(\zeta^{(k-1)}(s_0)\) by Cauchy's inequality, or cap (H) at a largest length and bound the tail), prove the repaired corollary, and only then check the two local hypotheses for \(\zeta\) from a read source. Decide whether the repaired premise is local in the height: the reviewer's heuristic is that it constrains zeros at all heights up to about \(\tau_0^{\vartheta a}\).

11 October 2026 — How the imported zero-free half-plane gets its power saving, read in the outline of its eleven-twelfths preprint: a premise on Möbius sums at every scale with no uniformity in the height, a family of sextic twists in which the sum recurs at sixth powers of primes, and a mean square over the family; under a hypothetical mean square the recurrence step would give exponent \(1-1/(2k)\) for symbols of order \(k\), and a \(2m\)-th moment would give \(1/2+(1-1/k)/(2m)\)

Source reading of an unaudited import's outline, with arithmetic of mine on one step; no new result and no result about zeta zeros. Family extraction reading. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its hard limit; the reading is recorded in Working mathematical context

Its best outcome is to find the device by which the source saves a power without knowing the zeros, and to say whether that device can be aimed at the premise this route has reduced to, or what in it is tied to the exponent.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. Earlier entries read the source's exclusion step and its scales but not where its power saving comes from. Which sums does it bound, at what lengths, and by what device?

New mathematics. None. One piece of arithmetic of mine on the source's extraction step, below.

Goal progress. Unchanged. Three things are learned about the route, none a result. First, the source's premise asks for no uniformity in the height: a power saving for smoothed twisted Möbius sums at every scale \(D\) and every cutoff \(W\) gives the half-plane, because a zero at any height is a pole of the reciprocal of the \(L\)-function. As the reviewer read in the deducing section, the cutoff used against a zero \(\varrho\) is \(W(y)=y^{-\varrho}\phi(y)\), so the sum is one of \(\mu(n)\nu(n)n^{-i\tau}\) at the zero's height with a constant depending on \(W\): fixed height, \(D\to\infty\). This differs from the route's one-height premise since the third review, which asked for a saving measured in the height. Second, the saving does not come from bounding one sum: the sum is placed in a family indexed by a second variable, it recurs in the family at sixth powers of primes, and a mean square over the whole family at square-root strength forces it to be small. Third, the recurrence step from a second moment fixes an exponent \(1-1/(2k)\) for symbols of order \(k\), which is \(11/12\) for \(k=6\); this is a property of that extraction from that moment, not of every family, as the reviewer showed: a \(2m\)-th moment of the same strength would give \(1/2+(1-1/k)/(2m)\). Converted. At the goal's parameters: the source's extraction from its second moment cannot go below \(11/12\) with sextic symbols; an exponent near the line would need, for instance, high moments of the family at square-root strength, which nothing read here supplies. Bearing. None; the cycle was spent on it because the user asked and because this source claims the kind of saving the route lacks.

General statement. None of mine. The statement read about is the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane and its intermediate half-plane \(\Re s>11/12\), for every finite-order Hecke \(L\)-function over \(\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})\); unaudited, and two reviewers of this notebook note that they do not know it from the published literature.

What was read

The preprint of 5 October 2026 in the retained snapshot (its TeX, lines 52 to 480: the introduction, and the outline's Steps 1 to 4 and the start of Step 5). It proves the half-plane \(11/12\) and says the stronger \(7/8\) is established in a companion paper, of which this is an isolated intermediate step. I read the outline, which the preprint says suppresses coprimality conditions, local factors and smoothing; the sections with the precise statements and proofs are not read.

The method, as the outline states it

  • The premise. For a finite-order Hecke character \(\nu\) of \(K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})\): \(A_1(D)=\sum_{\mathfrak n}\mu(\mathfrak n)\nu(\mathfrak n)W(N\mathfrak n/D)\ll D^{1-\delta+\epsilon}\) for every smooth cutoff \(W\) gives a zero-free half-plane; the outline calls this the smoothed Hecke version of the classical relation between Möbius sums and zero-free regions and cites Littlewood for the equivalence of the Riemann hypothesis with \(\sum_{n\le x}\mu(n)\ll x^{1/2+\epsilon}\).
  • The family. \(A_u(D)=\sum_{\mathfrak n}\mu(\mathfrak n)\nu(\mathfrak n)\chi_{\mathfrak n}(u)W(N\mathfrak n/D)\), with \(\chi_n(u)=(u/n)_6\) the sextic residue symbol and \(u\) an Eisenstein integer. The stated key estimate: \(\sum_{0<N(u)\le H}|A_u(D)|^2\ll D^{1+\epsilon}H\) for \(H=D^{1+\vartheta}\), \(0<\vartheta\le1/10\); square-root cancellation on average over \(u\).
  • The recurrence. For a prime \(p\) with \(Y/2<N(p)\le Y\), \(\chi_n(p^6)\) is \(1\) unless \(p\mid n\), so \(A_{p^6}(D)=A_1(D)+O(D/Y)\). With \(Y=H^{1/6}\) there are about \(Y/\log Y\) such rows, and the mean square gives \(|A_1(D)|^2\ll D^{1+\epsilon}H^{5/6}+D^2H^{-1/3}\), hence \(A_1(D)\ll D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\epsilon}\).
  • The mean square. Poisson summation in \(u\) produces sextic Gauss sums; an identity the preprint derives following Hasse and Heath-Brown, for squarefree primary \(n\) away from a fixed set of excluded primes, \(\mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\), absorbs the Möbius factor and leaves cubic Gauss sums, which are Fourier coefficients of Kubota's cubic theta function (Patterson's formula). The automorphy of the theta function transforms the completed column sums into dual sums twisted by a quadratic character, to which a quadratic large sieve over number fields applies with the factor \(M+L\); the cube factors introduced by the completion are removed by Möbius inversion with a cutoff; the outline's treatment of the part beyond the cutoff lies after the lines I read (the organization paragraph speaks of a recursive inequality).

Arithmetic on the recurrence step

Mine, and only about the step as the outline states it. Suppose a family built with symbols of order \(k\), so that \(A_{p^k}(D)=A_1(D)+O(D/Y)\) for primes of norm about \(Y=H^{1/k}\), and suppose the mean square \(\sum_{N(u)\le H}|A_u|^2\ll D^{1+\epsilon}H\) at \(H\) about \(D\). Then \(|A_1|^2\cdot H^{1/k}\ll D^{1+\epsilon}H+D^2H^{-1/k}\), so \(|A_1|^2\ll D^{2-1/k+\epsilon}\) and the exponent is \(1-1/(2k)\): \(11/12\) for \(k=6\), in agreement with the outline, \(5/6\) for \(k=3\), \(3/4\) for \(k=2\). Both the choice \(H\) about \(D\) (optimal under a bound of the shape \((D+H)D\), since below \(D\) it gives \(D^2H^{-1/k}\) and above \(DH^{1-1/k}\)) and every \(k\ne6\) are hypothetical: the source has only \(H=D^{1+\vartheta}\), \(\vartheta>0\), and sextic symbols. The exponent \(1-1/(2k)\) is what this extraction gives from a second moment, under four assumptions the reviewer listed: recurrence only at \(k\)-th powers with \(k\ge2\); a second moment only; square-root strength only at \(H\ge D\); and extraction by positivity, dropping all other rows. It is not a limit for every family. With the same recurrence and a \(2m\)-th moment \(\sum_u|A_u|^{2m}\ll D^{m+\epsilon}H\), the same step gives \(|A_1|^{2m}H^{1/k}\ll D^{m}H\), exponent \(1/2+(1-1/k)/(2m)\) at \(H=D\): \(5/8\) for \(k=2\), \(m=2\), and tending to \(1/2\) as \(m\) grows (the reviewer's observation; I checked the arithmetic). Using all \(k\)-th powers \(u=m^k\) gains only a logarithm, and those rows give coprimality-restricted sums, not \(A_1+O(D/Y)\); rows \(p^kv\) recover \(A_v\), not \(A_1\). The companion paper's \(7/8\) is below \(11/12\) by a second stage read in the family-optimum entry. On imprimitive rows, from the reviewer's reading of the deducing section: the key proposition sums over all \(0<N(u)\le D^{1+\vartheta}\) with no squarefree condition, so it contains the rows \(p^6\) and hence the saving itself; no general large sieve can hold with such rows (coefficients one give \(D^2H^{1/2}\)); the source applies the large sieve to the dual variables after Poisson summation, not to \(u\); and its deduction uses only sixth powers of primes outside a fixed set.

Against this route's record

  • No uniformity in the height. The source's premise is at a fixed height with the scale \(D\) going to infinity and a constant depending on the cutoff. The long-sum converse of this notebook works at one height and, for \(\zeta\), has a premise that the pole falsifies as stated. For a half-plane the height-free premise on Möbius sums is the natural one, and the open statement in that form is the classical one the outline cites: \(\sum_{n\le x}\mu(n)\ll x^{1/2+\epsilon}\). I state it as cited by the source, not as read in Littlewood.
  • Where the saving comes from. Both reviews recorded that every mechanism examined lacks a power saving that does not presuppose the zeros. The source's answer is a second variable: twist the sum, average over the twists, and use structure of the transformed coefficients (here automorphy of a theta function) that the original sum does not show. None of this notebook's mechanisms has a family in this sense; the Gram form averages over heights, which is the variable the zeros live in.
  • What is tied to the exponent. The order of the symbol and the order of the moment, through the recurrence step. The source's second moment with sextic symbols stops at \(11/12\) in this step. A higher moment at square-root strength, a mean square restricted to rows with small \(k\)-free part, or a square-root mean square at \(H<D\) would each lower it (the reviewer's list, unverified beyond the first); none is implied by the full mean square. Why the sextic case is the one where the Möbius factor is absorbed is the source's identity; for the quadratic symbol the reviewer's remark, unverified, is that the Gauss sums are explicit and nothing absorbs it.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the preprint's section deducing the zero-free region from the mean square (its Section 3) and the precise form of the key estimate, in particular how rows that are not squarefree are treated, and whether the recurrence is used only at sixth powers of primes or at all sixth powers.
  • High moments of the family: state as a lemma the extraction from a \(2m\)-th moment, with its hypotheses, and decide what a fourth moment \(\sum_u|A_u(D)|^4\) of the sextic family would require after Poisson summation in \(u\) (which dual sums appear, whether the source's Gauss-sum identity still absorbs the Möbius factors), and whether the known obstruction to large sieves with imprimitive rows applies to it. Also the reviewer's two other ways: a mean square over rows with small \(k\)-free part, and square-root strength at \(H<D\).
  • Restate the open statement's premise in the height-free form for \(\zeta\) and place the route's recorded mechanisms against it: which of them is a statement at every scale and which is tied to a height.

Follow-up. Continuing: the device is identified from the outline; whether another extraction exists, the item's bearing on the open statement, is the second sub-idea and the next attempt, and needs no source. The registry was searched for Mobius sum power saving sextic twist family mean square cubic Gauss sums theta, and for large sieve imprimitive rows sixth powers extraction exponent: no record on these objects beyond the imported theorem.

Searched Mobius sum power saving sextic twist family mean square cubic Gauss sums theta: no match; the second sub-idea.

Suggestion check. Checked: the arithmetic of the \(2m\)-th moment, \(|A_1|^{2m}H^{1/k}\ll D^{m}H\) giving \(1/2+(1-1/k)/(2m)\) at \(H=D\). The rest is not verified.

Verification gate. One fresh-context medium reviewer read the same lines of the preprint and its deducing section. It found the reading faithful and the arithmetic right, and required corrections, all incorporated: my conclusion that the extraction cannot go below \(3/4\) "whatever the family" was unjustified, resting on four unstated assumptions, and a higher moment beats it (withdrawn and restated with the assumptions); "no height" overstated a premise that is at a fixed height without uniformity (reworded in the title and twice in the body); the citation of Littlewood was attached to the wrong statement; a recursion was attributed to lines that do not contain it; the identity lacked its qualifiers; the hypothetical status of \(H=D\) and of other orders was not said. Process audit. 11 October 2026 — Recovering one sum from a family through a \(2m\)-th moment: with \(D^{\beta}\) rows the exponent is \(\tfrac12+\beta(1-\tfrac1k)/(2m)\) plus a defect; for \(\beta\ge m\) it is at best \(1-1/(2k)\), and for \(\beta<m\) the moment hypothesis by itself already gives every member of the family the exponent \(\tfrac12+\beta/(2m)\), so the extraction only improves a saving the hypothesis contains

Working proof of an elementary implication and arithmetic of its exponents; a reading of one proposition of an unaudited import; no result about zeta zeros. Moment extraction exponents. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its hard limit; the result is recorded in Working mathematical context

Chosen. This entry continues the item queued from the user's message and picked in the previous entry, ahead of the queue's head; it bears on off-line exclusion through the height-free premise on Möbius sums.

Owed attempt. The item's previous entry, family extraction reading, named its second sub-idea: the extraction from a \(2m\)-th moment as a lemma with its hypotheses, and what a fourth moment of the sextic family would require. The first part is made here. The second is not made: the one-row consequence below shows that a fourth moment at fewer than \(D^{2}\) rows would by itself be a stronger theorem than the source's, so reading the source's Poisson step for it comes after this computation and is kept as a sub-idea.

Best outcome. Written before the work: at the goal's own parameters the best outcome is an extraction whose exponent tends to \(1/2\) under a hypothesis weaker than the conclusion for the whole family, which would make high moments of a twisted family a premise for the line. If the hypothesis turns out to be of the conclusion's strength, the outcome is that this door is closed for exact recurrence.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero, in the height-free form a power saving for Möbius sums. The previous entry's reviewer observed that a \(2m\)-th moment would lower the exponent of the source's extraction. With all terms kept, what exponent does a \(2m\)-th moment give, for how many rows must it hold, and what does the hypothesis amount to?

New mathematics. prop:moment-extraction-exponents, an elementary implication with its exponent arithmetic: with \(H=D^{\beta}\) rows, recurrence at \(H^{1/k}\) of them up to a defect \(E\), and a \(2m\)-th moment of size \(D^{m}H\), the sum is at most \(D^{1/2+\beta(1-1/k)/(2m)}+E\). Consequences are computed. The defect, which the reviewer's formula omitted, is \(D^{1-\beta/k}\) when bounded trivially; for Möbius coefficients it is a short sum of sums of the same kind at smaller scales with the same excluded set, so an induction over scales removes it. And, the reviewer's observation in this cycle, keeping a single row of the moment gives every member the exponent \(\tfrac12+\beta/(2m)\) with no recurrence at all.

Goal progress. Unchanged. For \(\beta\ge m\), the only range in which a square-root bound for the \(2m\)-th moment can hold for arbitrary coefficients, the extraction gives at best \(1-1/(2k)\), the second moment's exponent. For \(\beta<m\) the exponent falls below that, but a moment bound \(\sum_{N(u)\le D^{\beta}}|A_u|^{2m}\le CD^{m+\epsilon}H\) then gives, from one row and positivity, \(|A_u|\le C^{1/(2m)}D^{1/2+\beta/(2m)+\epsilon/(2m)}\) for every row, the target row included: a power saving for every member with no recurrence. The extraction improves \(\beta/(2m)\) to \(\beta(1-1/k)/(2m)\) and nothing more. So in the sense of the Best outcome the door is closed for this hypothesis: a moment bound below the large-sieve range is not weaker than a quasi-Riemann hypothesis for the whole family with nearly the same exponent; it contains one. What remains open is whether such a bound can be proved for some family, which would be a result in itself and not a premise. Converted. At the goal's parameters: exponent \(1/2+\epsilon\) from this extraction needs \(\beta(1-1/k)/(2m)\le\epsilon\), and the hypothesis used then already gives \(1/2+\epsilon k/(k-1)\) for every member. Bearing. None; the cycle was spent on it because the reviewer's observation, if it had held with an accessible hypothesis, would have been a premise for the line, and it had to be computed with all its terms.

General statement. prop:moment-extraction-exponents, for all integers \(k,m\ge1\), all \(\beta>0\) and all families of complex numbers satisfying its hypotheses.

Statement and proof

Proposition (moment extraction exponents). prop:moment-extraction-exponents. Let \(k,m\ge1\) be integers, \(\beta>0\), \(\epsilon\ge0\), \(C\ge1\), \(D\ge2\), \(H=D^{\beta}\). Let \(A\) and \(A_u\), \(u\in P\), be complex numbers, \(P\) a finite set with \(|P|\ge C^{-1}H^{1/k}D^{-\epsilon}\), such that \(|A-A_u|\le E\) for every \(u\in P\) and \(\sum_{u\in P}|A_u|^{2m}\le CD^{m+\epsilon}H\). Then \[|A|\le C^{1/m}D^{\frac12+\frac{\beta(1-1/k)}{2m}+\frac{\epsilon}{m}}+E.\]

Proof. For each \(u\in P\), \(|A|-E\le|A_u|\); so \((|A|-E)_+^{2m}|P|\le\sum_{u\in P}|A_u|^{2m}\le CD^{m+\epsilon}H\), and \((|A|-E)_+\le(C^2D^{m+2\epsilon}H^{1-1/k})^{1/(2m)}=C^{1/m}D^{1/2+\epsilon/m}H^{(1-1/k)/(2m)}\). \(\square\)

Consequences (arithmetic; \(e_1=\tfrac12+\beta(1-\tfrac1k)/(2m)\)).

  • Trivial defect. If \(E\le CD^{1-\beta/k}\), the exponent is \(\max(e_1,1-\beta/k)\). Over \(\beta\) it is least at \(\beta^*=mk/(k-1+2m)\), where both equal \((k-1+m)/(k-1+2m)\). At \(\beta=1\) it is \(\max(\tfrac12+(1-\tfrac1k)/(2m),\,1-\tfrac1k)\): for \(k=6\), \(m=1\) this is \(11/12\), the source's exponent; for \(k=6\) and every \(m\ge2\) it is \(5/6\), the defect; for \(k=2\), \(m=2\) it is \(5/8\), the reviewer's number.
  • Large-sieve range. If the moment bound is available only for \(\beta\ge m\), then \(e_1\ge\tfrac12+(1-\tfrac1k)/2=1-\tfrac1{2k}\) for every \(m\), with equality at \(\beta=m\), where the defect exponent \(1-m/k\) is smaller. So in that range every moment gives the second moment's exponent.
  • Defect of the same kind. If \(E\le CD^{\theta(1-\beta/k)}\) for some \(\theta\le1\), the exponent is \(\max(e_1,\theta(1-\beta/k))\), below \(\theta\) whenever \(e_1<\theta\). For the source's coefficients this is the natural form of the defect: for a prime \(p\) outside the excluded set \(S\), \(A_1(D)-A_{p^k}(D)=\sum_{p\mid n}\mu(n)\nu(n)W(N(n)/D)=-\nu(p)\sum_{(n',S\cup\{p\})=1}\mu(n')\nu(n')W\big(N(n')\big/(D/N(p))\big)\), a sum of the same form at scale \(D/N(p)\) with \(p\) added to the excluded set. The excluded set does not have to grow (the reviewer's correction of my draft): with \(B(D')\) the sum over \(n'\) prime to \(S\cup\{p\}\), splitting \(A_1^{S}(D')\) by whether \(p\mid n\) gives \(B(D')=A_1^{S}(D')+\nu(p)B(D'/N(p))\), hence \(B(D')=\sum_{j\ge0}\nu(p)^jA_1^{S}(D'/N(p)^{j})\), a sum of at most \(k/\beta+1\) nonzero terms when \(N(p)\) is about \(D^{\beta/k}\) and \(W\) has compact support. So if \(|A_1^{S}(D')|\le KD'^{\theta}\) for all \(D'<D\), then \(E\le K(k/\beta+1)D^{\theta(1-\beta/k)}\), and with the moment hypothesis at every scale an induction on \(D\) gives \(A_1(D)\ll D^{e_1+\epsilon'}\): the defect is not a barrier. For instance \(k=6\), \(m=2\), \(\beta=1\) would give \(17/24\) under the hypothesis.
  • One row. Under \(\sum_{u\in U}|A_u|^{2m}\le CD^{m+\epsilon}H\), every \(u\in U\) has \(|A_u|\le C^{1/(2m)}D^{1/2+\beta/(2m)+\epsilon/(2m)}\), by dropping the other terms. This is better than the trivial bound \(D\) exactly when \(\beta<m\) (up to \(\epsilon\)). If the row \(u=1\) is in \(U\) and the hypothesis holds at every large \(D\), this is a power saving for \(A_1\) itself with exponent \(\tfrac12+\beta/(2m)\), and the extraction's \(e_1\) differs from it by the factor \(1-1/k\) in the second term.

What the hypothesis amounts to. \(\sum_u|A_u|^{2m}\) is the mean square of the \(m\)-fold product sums, of length \(D^{m}\). For arbitrary coefficients of modulus one a bound \(\sum_{u\in U}|\sum_{n}a_n\chi_n(u)|^2\le X\cdot L\) over sums of length \(L\) forces \(X\ge L\): take \(a_n=\overline{\chi_n(u_0)}\) for one row \(u_0\) at which the symbols do not vanish, so that this row alone contributes \(L^2\). So square-root strength \(D^{m}H\) for arbitrary coefficients of the product sums needs \(H\ge D^{m}\); this is necessary, and I do not claim it is sufficient for symbols of any order. For \(H<D^{m}\) the hypothesis is about the particular coefficients, and the one-row consequence above says what it contains. In the limit: if the hypothesis holds for every \(m\), with constants allowed to depend on \(m\), at a fixed \(\beta\) and every large \(D\), then every member satisfies \(|A_u|\le D^{1/2+\delta}\) for each \(\delta>0\) and large \(D\). Unused in the proposition: \(D\ge2\) and \(C\ge1\) only fix conventions.

Degenerate cases. \(k=1\): the sum recurs on all rows, \(e_1=1/2\), and the hypothesis is the conclusion on average, a tautology. \(m\to\infty\): \((\sum_P|A_u|^{2m}/|P|)^{1/(2m)}\to\max_P|A_u|\), and the proposition becomes \(|A|\le\max_P|A_u|+E\). \(E=0\) and all \(A_u=A\): equality up to the constants. \(P\) empty is excluded by the lower bound on \(|P|\).

Nonvacuity. With \(D=2^{k}\), \(\beta=1\), so that \(H^{1/k}=2\): the constant family \(A_u=A=D^{1/2}\) on \(|P|=2\) rows, \(E=0\), \(C=1\), \(\epsilon=0\) satisfies the hypotheses, since \(\sum_P|A_u|^{2m}=D^{m}H^{1/k}\le D^{m}H\). The source's family at \(k=6\), \(m=1\), \(\beta=1+\vartheta\) satisfies them if its proposition holds, with \(E\ll D/Y\).

The source's key proposition, read

Lines 660 to 772 of the preprint's TeX, read in this cycle. The proposition: for every fixed \(0<\vartheta\le1/10\) and \(\epsilon>0\) there is an integer \(j_0\) such that for all smooth \(W\) supported in a compact interval and \(D\ge2\), \(\sum_{0<N(u)\le D^{1+\vartheta}}|A_u(D)|^2\ll(\max_{0\le j\le j_0}\|W^{(j)}\|_\infty)^2D^{2+\vartheta+\epsilon}\), the constant depending on the character, the excluded set \(S\), the interval, \(\vartheta\) and \(\epsilon\). The sum is over all \(u\) in the range, with no squarefree condition. The deduction uses the rows \(p^6\) for primes \(p\notin S\) of norm in \((Y/2,Y]\), \(Y=H^{1/6}\), the bound \(|A_1-A_{p^6}|\ll D/Y\), and then the Mellin transform \(\int A_1(D)D^{-s}dD/D=\widehat W(s)/L^S(s,\nu)\) with \(W(y)=y^{-\varrho}\phi(y)\) against a hypothetical zero \(\varrho\). This matches the proposition above with \(k=6\), \(m=1\), \(\beta=1+\vartheta\). How the proof of the mean square treats the rows that are sixth powers is in sections not read.

11 October 2026 — The local implication for \(\zeta\) itself, with no repair of the pole: at the exponents the pole allows, if a saving \(\tau_0^{-1}\) on lengths at least \(\tau_0^{2}\) is assumed, then for large \(\tau_0\) a zero \(1-x+i\tau_0\) with \(x<1/2\) is excluded unless at least \(\kappa(x)\log\tau_0\) zeros, all off the critical line, lie in a fixed disk centred at \(1+\mu+i\tau_0\) with that zero near its left edge

Working proof of a lemma with two radii and a conditional corollary for the zeta function, relying on one published theorem read and not audited; the premise on prime sums is unproved. Sparse off-line exclusion. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its hard limit; the result is recorded in Working mathematical context

Chosen. This entry takes the picked lead of the third review ahead of the queue's head: the item developed in the last two entries has had two in a row declaring no bearing (the first and the second), and this lead's last entry left its corollary inapplicable to \(\zeta\). It bears on off-line exclusion directly.

Owed attempt. The item's previous entry, long-sum local converse, named the repair of its premise for \(\zeta\), whose pole forbids a saving beyond \(\tau_0^{-1}\) on long sums. Made here in a different way from the one proposed: instead of removing the pole's term, the corollary is proved at the exponents the pole allows, where the margins no longer grow and the count of zeros must be small.

Best outcome. Written before the work: at the goal's own parameters, an implication for \(\zeta\) itself from square-root cancellation of the prime sums at one height to the absence of a zero at that height anywhere to the right of the line, with hypotheses on the other zeros that are stated exactly. It would leave the cancellation itself unproved.

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. For \(\zeta\) the sums \(\sum\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau_0}\) cannot save more than \(\tau_0^{-1}\) on long ranges. With \(b=1\) and \(a=2\), where \(b/a=1/2\), what does the local inequality give, and what must be assumed about the other zeros?

New mathematics. lem:two-radius-zero-sum-inequality: the local inequality with the zeros taken in a disk of radius \(R_1\) and the growth bounded on a larger radius \(R_2\), not necessarily \(2R_1\), at the cost of \(K\log^+(R_1/(R_2-R_1))\) in the growth constant. cor:sparse-off-line-exclusion: for \(\zeta\) and each \(0<x<1/2\) there are \(\mu\), \(R_1<\mu+\tfrac12\) and \(\kappa>0\) such that, if at a large height \(\tau_0\) the prime sums satisfy \(|\sum_{y<n\le y'}\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau_0}|\le Cy\tau_0^{-1}\log^{A}y\) for all \(\tau_0^{2}\le y<y'\le2y\), and the disk of radius \(R_1\) about \(1+\mu+i\tau_0\) holds at most \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) zeros, then \(\zeta(1-x+i\tau_0)\ne0\). The growth hypothesis is proved for \(\zeta\) from an elementary formula; the disk does not meet the critical line.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The corollary is a conditional exclusion of an off-line zero for \(\zeta\) itself at every \(x<1/2\), but its premise on prime sums is unproved. From the reviewer's and my recollection of the explicit formula, unread and an untested hypothesis here: under the Riemann hypothesis the sums are the pole's term plus \(O(y^{1/2}\log^2(y\tau_0))\), so the premise holds with \(A=2\) from \(y\ge\tau_0^{2}\) on; conversely a zero \(\beta+i\gamma\) contributes about \(y^{\beta}/|\gamma-\tau_0|\) and, barring cancellation, violates it at \(y=\tau_0^{2}\) when \(|\gamma-\tau_0|<\tau_0^{2\beta-1}\). On that reading the premise speaks of off-line zeros in a window whose width is a power of \(\tau_0\), far wider than the disk, and the count hypothesis is the condition that the zeros near the height do not cancel. So this is a reduction to a statement of about the conclusion's strength. What it adds to the record is the exact shape of the exception: a cluster of at least \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) off-line zeros in one disk. Converted. At the goal's parameters: under the premise at height \(\tau_0\), a zero with real part \(1-x>1/2\) at that height must be accompanied by at least \(\kappa(x)\log\tau_0\) zeros with real part above \(\tfrac34-\tfrac x2\) and height within \(R_1\) of \(\tau_0\); \(\kappa(x)\to0\) as \(x\to1/2\). The constant is small: the reviewer's rough value at \(x=1/4\), \(r=0.03\) is \(\kappa\approx0.015\), unverified, so that even a single zero in the disk is admitted only for \(\tau_0\) beyond about \(e^{66}\). Goal instance. For \(\zeta\) the premise is not excluded by the pole: the pole's term in these sums is at most \(4y/\tau_0\) in modulus, within the premise for \(C\ge4\). Whether the premise holds is not known. The count hypothesis is about hypothetical zeros and cannot be checked. Bearing. Some: for \(\zeta\), a stated premise on primes at one height under which only clustered off-line zeros survive; the bearing is the proved implication, not the unread heuristics about the premise. Not covered. The premise; clusters of \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) or more off-line zeros in the disk; heights below the unspecified threshold \(\tau_*\).

General statement. lem:two-radius-zero-sum-inequality, for all parameters in its statement; cor:sparse-off-line-exclusion, for every \(0<x<1/2\) and all sufficiently large \(\tau_0\).

Dependencies

lem:local-zero-sum-inequality (recorded proof, re-read in this cycle): its hypotheses (D) and (H), its quantities \(E_{\mathrm{low}}\), \(E_{\mathrm{up}}\), and the two parts of its proof headed "The power sum" and "The Dirichlet series", which do not involve the radius; the first of these uses Turán's second main theorem, published, read and not audited. The part on the zeros is replaced below.

The lemma with two radii

Lemma (two-radius zero-sum inequality). lem:two-radius-zero-sum-inequality. Keep \(\tau_0,\mu,s_0,B,S,A,X\), (D) and (H) of the recorded lemma. Let \(0<R_1<R_2\), \(M\ge0\). Assume (Z′): \(f\) is analytic on \(|s-s_0|\le R_2\), \(f(s_0)\ne0\), \(|f(s)|\le e^{M}|f(s_0)|\) there, \(f'/f=F\) near \(s_0\); its zeros in \(|s-s_0|\le R_1\), with multiplicity, are \(\rho_1,\dots,\rho_K\), \(K\ge1\), \(d=\min_j|s_0-\rho_j|\). Put \(M'=M+K\log^+\frac{R_1}{R_2-R_1}\). Then for every integer \(m\ge1\) with \(\mu\log X\le m+1\) there is \(k\in(m,m+K]\) with \[2\Big(\frac{K}{8e(m+K)}\Big)^{K}d^{-k}\le\frac{E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}}{(k-1)!}+2M'k\,R_1^{-k}.\] With \(R_2=2R_1\) this is the recorded lemma.

Proof. Put \(g=f\prod_j(s-\rho_j)^{-1}\). On \(|s-s_0|=R_2\), \(|s-\rho_j|\ge R_2-R_1\) and \(|s_0-\rho_j|\le R_1\), so \(|g(s)/g(s_0)|\le e^{M}(R_1/(R_2-R_1))^{K}\le e^{M'}\), and by the maximum principle on the whole disk. \(g\) has no zero in \(|s-s_0|\le R_1\), so \(G=\log(g/g(s_0))\) is analytic in a neighbourhood of that disk, \(G(s_0)=0\), \(\Re G\le M'\). As in the recorded proof, with \(G=\sum_{j\ge1}c_j(s-s_0)^j\): \(c_j\varrho^{j}=-\frac1\pi\int_0^{2\pi}(M'-\Re G(s_0+\varrho e^{i\phi}))e^{-ij\phi}d\phi\) and \(\frac1\pi\int(M'-\Re G)=2M'\), so \(|c_j|\le2M'R_1^{-j}\). Hence \(|F^{(k-1)}(s_0)-(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}|\le2M'k!\,R_1^{-k}\). The power-sum and Dirichlet-series parts are those of the recorded proof, unchanged, and the conclusion follows as there. \(\square\)

Corollary in parameters. Let \(x,\mu,a,b>0\), \(r=x/\mu\), \(t\in(0,1)\), \(\lambda=a\mu/t\), \(R_1>\mu+x\), \(\theta=R_1/(\mu+x)\), \(R_2>R_1\), \(\kappa>0\), \(M_0\ge0\), \(h(t)=t-1-\log t\), \(\ell=\log(8e(\lambda+\kappa+1)/\kappa)\), and assume \[\kappa\ell+(\lambda+\kappa)\log(1+r)<\min\big(b,\ \lambda h(t),\ \lambda\log(1+1/\mu)\big),\qquad\kappa\ell<\lambda\log\theta.\] Then for all \(\tau_0\ge\tau_*\) (depending on these parameters and on \(A,B,C\)): if (D) and (Z′) hold with \(M\le M_0\log\tau_0\) and \(K\le\kappa\log\tau_0\), and (H) holds with \(X=\tau_0^{a}\), \(S=C\tau_0^{-b}\), then \(f(1-x+i\tau_0)\ne0\). Proof. As for the recorded corollary: \(L=\log\tau_0\ge1\), \(m=\lceil\lambda L\rceil\), so \(\mu\log X=t\lambda L\le m+1\); a zero \(s^*=1-x+i\tau_0\) lies in the disk of radius \(R_1\), \(d\le\mu(1+r)\), and \(\lambda L\le k\le(\lambda+\kappa)L+1\). Multiply the lemma's inequality by \(\mu^{k}(1+r)^{k}\). The left side is at least \(2\tau_0^{-\kappa\ell}\). The three Dirichlet terms are bounded as in the recorded bullets by powers of \(L\) times \(\tau_0^{-b}\), \(\tau_0^{-\lambda h(t)}\), \(\tau_0^{-\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)}\), each multiplied by \((1+r)^{k}\le(1+r)\tau_0^{(\lambda+\kappa)\log(1+r)}\). The last term is \(2M'k(\mu(1+r)/R_1)^{k}=2M'k\theta^{-k}\le2M'k\,\tau_0^{-\lambda\log\theta}\), with \(M'\le(M_0+\kappa\log^+\frac{R_1}{R_2-R_1})L\). By the two assumed inequalities every exponent on the right is below \(-\kappa\ell\) by a fixed amount, so the inequality fails for large \(\tau_0\). \(\square\)

The zeta function

Growth. For \(\sigma>1\), \(\zeta(s)=s\int_1^\infty\lfloor u\rfloor u^{-s-1}du=\frac{s}{s-1}-s\int_1^\infty\{u\}u^{-s-1}du\), and the right side is analytic for \(\sigma>0\), \(s\ne1\); so \(|\zeta(s)|\le|s|/|s-1|+|s|/\sigma\) there. For \(\sigma\ge1/4\) and \(|t|\ge1\) this is at most \(5|s|\). And \(|\zeta(s_0)|^{-1}=|\sum\mu(n)n^{-s_0}|\le\zeta(1+\mu)\). So on the disk of radius \(R_2=\mu+\tfrac34\) about \(s_0=1+\mu+i\tau_0\), which lies in \(\sigma\ge1/4\) and, for \(\tau_0\ge\mu+2\), in \(|t|\ge1\) and away from \(s=1\): \(|\zeta(s)/\zeta(s_0)|\le5(\tau_0+2\mu+2)\zeta(1+\mu)\), so \(M\le2\log\tau_0\) for large \(\tau_0\).

Corollary (sparse off-line exclusion). cor:sparse-off-line-exclusion. Let \(0<x<1/2\) and \(A,C\ge0\). Put \(t=\tfrac12+x\), choose \(r\in(0,1)\) with \(\log(1+r)<h(t)\), and put \(\mu=x/r\), \(R_1=\mu+\tfrac14+\tfrac x2\). There is \(\kappa=\kappa(x,r)>0\) such that for all \(\tau_0\ge\tau_*(x,r,A,C)\): if \[\Big|\sum_{y<n\le y'}\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau_0}\Big|\le C\,y\,\tau_0^{-1}\log^{A}y\quad\text{for all }\tau_0^{2}\le y<y'\le2y,\] and \(\zeta\) has at most \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) zeros, with multiplicity, in \(|s-(1+\mu+i\tau_0)|\le R_1\), then \(\zeta(1-x+i\tau_0)\ne0\). Every point of that disk has real part at least \(\tfrac34-\tfrac x2>\tfrac12\).

Proof. Apply the corollary in parameters to \(f=\zeta\), \(b_n=-\Lambda(n)\) (so \(B=1\) and \(f'/f=F\) for \(\sigma>1\)), \(a=2\), \(b=1\), \(R_2=\mu+\tfrac34\), \(M_0=2\). Here \(\mu+x<R_1<\mu+\tfrac12<R_2\) because \(x<\tfrac14+\tfrac x2<\tfrac12\), so \(\theta>1\), the disk of radius \(R_1\) has real parts at least \(1+\mu-R_1=\tfrac34-\tfrac x2\), and \(R_2-R_1=\tfrac12-\tfrac x2>\tfrac14\). The assumed inequalities at \(\kappa=0\): \(\lambda\log(1+r)=\frac{2x}{t}\cdot\frac{\log(1+r)}{r}<\frac{2x}{t}<1=b\), since \(t=\tfrac12+x>2x\); \(\log(1+r)<h(t)\) by the choice of \(r\); \(\log(1+r)<\log(1+1/\mu)\) because \(r<1/\mu=r/x\); and \(0<\lambda\log\theta\). As \(\kappa\to0\), \(\kappa\ell\to0\) and \(\kappa\log(1+r)\to0\), so the strict inequalities hold for some \(\kappa>0\). The growth hypothesis is the paragraph above. \(\square\)

Degenerate cases. \(x\to1/2\): \(t\to1\), \(h(t)\to0\), so \(r\to0\), \(\mu\to\infty\) and \(\kappa\to0\); the statement degenerates because the margin \(h(t)\) vanishes, not because of zeros on the line, which the disk never meets. \(x\to0\) with \(r\) fixed: \(\lambda=2x/(rt)\to0\) and \(\kappa\to0\) as well; near the line \(\sigma=1\) the statement is useful only with \(\mu\) kept fixed. No zero at all in the disk: the conclusion is trivially true. The zero alone in its disk (\(K=1\)): excluded for large \(\tau_0\) under the premise, whatever \(\kappa\). In the lemma, \(R_2\ge2R_1\) gives \(M'=M\).

Nonvacuity. For the lemma: \(f(s)=1-2^{-s}\) of the recorded example, with \(\tau_0=2\pi j_0/\log2\), \(\mu=1\), \(R_1=5/2\), \(R_2=3\ne2R_1\): the zero \(i\tau_0\) is at distance \(2\) from \(s_0=2+i\tau_0\), so \(K\ge1\), and (D), (H) hold as recorded. For the corollary's parameters: \(x=1/4\), \(t=3/4\), \(h(t)=0.0377\), \(r=0.03\) (\(\log1.03=0.0296\)), \(\mu=8.33\), \(R_1=8.71\) satisfy its conditions; whether any height satisfies the premise on prime sums is not known, and under the hypothesis the conclusion is true at every height.

What the exception is

The disk about \(1+\mu+i\tau_0\) of radius \(R_1=\mu+\tfrac14+\tfrac x2\) meets the strip in the region \(\sigma\ge1+\mu-\sqrt{R_1^2-(\gamma-\tau_0)^2}\): near the height \(\tau_0\) it reaches \(\sigma=\tfrac34-\tfrac x2\), halfway between the line and the hypothetical zero, and it closes at height distance about \(\sqrt{2\mu(\tfrac14+\tfrac x2)}\) from \(\tau_0\) for points with real part near one. So the zeros that can defeat the exclusion are off-line zeros to the right of a circular arc through the midpoint (a parabola to first order), within a bounded height distance; zeros on the critical line are outside the disk and play no part beyond the growth bound. This is the function-level form of the cluster case recorded for the Gram model in the resonance-free bandwidth entry, with a constant: fewer than \(\kappa(x)\log\tau_0\) of them cannot hide a zero from the prime sums at its height. Unused in the corollary: \(r<1\), and the particular \(R_2\), for which any value in \((R_1,\mu+\tfrac34]\) serves.

  • Give \(\kappa(x)\) explicitly for one value of \(x\) with the threshold \(e^{1/\kappa}\), and compare the exceptional cluster with what a zero-density estimate read from a source allows on average. Weaken the count to a thin shell (the reviewer's proposal): zeros with \(|s_0-\rho|\ge de^{\varepsilon}\) contribute at most \(K\tau_0^{-\varepsilon\lambda}\) after normalizing, so the second main theorem is needed only for zeros with \(d\le|s_0-\rho|\le de^{\varepsilon}\), the full count being \(O(\log\tau_0)\) by Jensen's formula from the growth bound; state and prove that version. Compare with the published theorem at \(a=2\), \(b=1\).

11 October 2026 — The count is needed only in a thin shell: under the assumed saving for the prime sums at height \(\tau_0\), a zero \(\tfrac34+i\tau_0\) of \(\zeta\) forces, for large \(\tau_0\), at least \(0.02\log\tau_0\) zeros whose distance from \(4.125+i\tau_0\) is within two percent of the least such distance; the proof's own threshold is beyond \(\tau_0=e^{460}\), so the statement is empty at every height one could name

New mathematics. lem:shell-zero-sum-inequality: in the two-radius inequality the second main theorem is applied only to the zeros whose distance from \(s_0\) is within a factor \(e^{\varepsilon}\) of the least distance \(d\); the others contribute at most \((K-K_s)e^{-\varepsilon k}\), and the total number \(K\) of zeros in the disk is at most \(M/\log(R_2/R_1)\), by a two-line argument with Blaschke factors. cor:shell-off-line-exclusion: the corollary for \(\zeta\) with the count hypothesis on the shell only, and an explicit instance at \(x=1/4\).

Goal progress. Unchanged. The exception of the conditional exclusion is narrowed and made explicit; the premise on prime sums is untouched and unproved. Converted. The threshold \(\tau_*\) is not computed, and it is large: the step that discards the zeros outside the shell needs \(19.65\log\tau_0\le\tau_0^{0.0198}\), that is \(\log\tau_0\) at least about \(460\) (the reviewer's figure, which a one-line solve of mine confirms), and the first inequality's margin \(0.0082\) against powers of \(\log\tau_0\) pushes it further. So it says nothing at any height one could name. Goal instance. General statement. lem:shell-zero-sum-inequality, for all parameters in its statement; cor:shell-off-line-exclusion, for the stated parameters at \(x=1/4\) and all sufficiently large \(\tau_0\), and in its parameter form for all parameters satisfying its three inequalities.

Dependencies

lem:two-radius-zero-sum-inequality and the growth bound for \(\zeta\) (recorded proofs, re-read): the bound \(|F^{(k-1)}(s_0)-(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}|\le2M'k!R_1^{-k}\), valid for every \(k\ge2\), and the corollary in parameters with its proof; lem:local-zero-sum-inequality (recorded proof): the bound \(|F^{(k-1)}(s_0)|\le E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}\) for every \(k\) with \(\mu\log X\le k\); Turán's second main theorem, published, read and not audited.

Statement and proof

Lemma (shell zero-sum inequality). lem:shell-zero-sum-inequality. Under the hypotheses of the two-radius lemma, let \(\varepsilon>0\) and let \(K_s\ge1\) be the number of zeros \(\rho_j\) with \(|s_0-\rho_j|\le e^{\varepsilon}d\). Then (1) \(K\le M/\log(R_2/R_1)\); (2) for every integer \(m\ge1\) with \(\mu\log X\le m+1\) there is \(k\in(m,m+K_s]\) with \[\Big[2\Big(\frac{K_s}{8e(m+K_s)}\Big)^{K_s}-(K-K_s)e^{-\varepsilon k}\Big]d^{-k}\le\frac{E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}}{(k-1)!}+2M'k\,R_1^{-k}.\]

Proof. (1) Put \(w_j=\rho_j-s_0\) and \(\tilde g(s)=f(s)\prod_j\frac{R_2^2-\overline{w_j}(s-s_0)}{R_2\,(s-\rho_j)}\), analytic on \(|s-s_0|\le R_2\). On the circle \(|s-s_0|=R_2\) each factor has modulus one, since \(|R_2^2-\overline{w}z|=|z|\,|\bar z-\bar w|=R_2|z-w|\) for \(|z|=R_2\). So \(|\tilde g|\le e^{M}|f(s_0)|\) on the circle, hence at \(s_0\): \(|f(s_0)|\prod_jR_2/|w_j|\le e^{M}|f(s_0)|\), and as \(|w_j|\le R_1\), \(K\log(R_2/R_1)\le M\). (2) Apply the second main theorem to the \(K_s\) numbers \(z_j=d/(s_0-\rho_j)\) of the shell, of modulus at most one with equality for a nearest zero, with unit coefficients: there is \(k\in(m,m+K_s]\) with \(|\sum_{\mathrm{shell}}z_j^{k}|\ge2(K_s/(8e(m+K_s)))^{K_s}\). The other \(K-K_s\) zeros have \(|z_j|<e^{-\varepsilon}\), so \(|\sum_jz_j^{k}|\ge2(K_s/(8e(m+K_s)))^{K_s}-(K-K_s)e^{-\varepsilon k}\). For this \(k\), which is at least \(2\) and satisfies \(\mu\log X\le k\), the two recorded bounds give \((k-1)!\,d^{-k}|\sum_jz_j^{k}|=(k-1)!|\sum_j(s_0-\rho_j)^{-k}|\le E_{\mathrm{low}}+E_{\mathrm{up}}+2M'k!R_1^{-k}\). \(\square\)

Corollary in parameters. With the notation of the recorded corollary in parameters, \(\kappa_s>0\) in place of \(\kappa\), \(\ell_s=\log(8e(\lambda+\kappa_s+1)/\kappa_s)\) and \(\varepsilon>0\), assume \[\kappa_s\ell_s+(\lambda+\kappa_s)\log(1+r)<\min\big(b,\lambda h(t),\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)\big),\qquad\kappa_s\ell_s<\lambda\log\theta,\qquad\kappa_s\ell_s<\varepsilon\lambda.\] Then for all large \(\tau_0\): if (D), (Z′) with \(M\le M_0\log\tau_0\), and (H) with \(X=\tau_0^{a}\), \(S=C\tau_0^{-b}\) hold, and \(f(1-x+i\tau_0)=0\), then more than \(\kappa_s\log\tau_0\) zeros lie in \(d\le|s-s_0|\le e^{\varepsilon}d\), where \(d\le\mu+x\) is the least distance from \(s_0\) to a zero. Proof. Suppose \(K_s\le\kappa_sL\), \(L=\log\tau_0\). By part (1), \(K\le M_0L/\log(R_2/R_1)\). In part (2) with \(m=\lceil\lambda L\rceil\), \(k>\lambda L\), the bracket is at least \(2\tau_0^{-\kappa_s\ell_s}-\frac{M_0L}{\log(R_2/R_1)}\tau_0^{-\varepsilon\lambda}\ge\tau_0^{-\kappa_s\ell_s}\) for large \(\tau_0\), by the third inequality. Multiply by \(\mu^{k}(1+r)^{k}\ge d^{k}\); the right side is bounded exactly as in the recorded proof, with \(k\le(\lambda+\kappa_s)L+1\) and \(M'\le(M_0+\frac{M_0}{\log(R_2/R_1)}\log^+\frac{R_1}{R_2-R_1})L\), and the first two inequalities give a contradiction for large \(\tau_0\). \(\square\)

Corollary (shell off-line exclusion). cor:shell-off-line-exclusion. Let \(A,C\ge0\). For all \(\tau_0\ge\tau_*(A,C)\): if \(|\sum_{y<n\le y'}\Lambda(n)n^{-i\tau_0}|\le Cy\tau_0^{-1}\log^{A}y\) for all \(\tau_0^{2}\le y<y'\le2y\) and \(\zeta(\tfrac34+i\tau_0)=0\), then, with \(s_0=4.125+i\tau_0\) and \(d\le3.375\) the least distance from \(s_0\) to a zero of \(\zeta\), more than \(0.02\log\tau_0\) zeros of \(\zeta\), with multiplicity, lie in \(d\le|s-s_0|\le e^{0.02}d\); all of them have real part at least \(0.625\). The whole disk \(|s-s_0|\le3.5\) holds at most \(19.65\log\tau_0\) zeros. Proof. The corollary in parameters for \(f=\zeta\), \(b_n=-\Lambda(n)\), \(a=2\), \(b=1\), \(x=1/4\), \(t=0.6\), \(r=0.08\), \(\mu=3.125\), \(R_1=3.5\), \(R_2=3.875\), \(M_0=2\) (the recorded growth bound, whose disk of radius \(R_2=\mu+\tfrac34\) lies in \(\sigma\ge1/4\)), \(\kappa_s=0.02\), \(\varepsilon=0.02\). The three inequalities, evaluated by a script with saved output: \(\lambda=10.4167\), \(\ell_s=9.4283\), \(\kappa_s\ell_s=0.18857\); the left side of the first is \(0.99178\), below \(b=1\), \(\lambda h(t)=1.15443\) and \(\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)=2.89200\); \(\lambda\log\theta=0.37883\); \(\varepsilon\lambda=0.20833\). The shell lies in the disk of radius \(e^{0.02}\cdot3.375<3.5\), whose points have real part at least \(1+\mu-R_1=0.625\). The count: \(M_0/\log(R_2/R_1)=19.6497\). \(\square\)

Degenerate cases. \(\varepsilon\) so large that the shell is the whole disk: \(K_s=K\), the correction vanishes and the lemma is the two-radius lemma. \(K_s=K=1\): the bracket is \(2/(8e(m+1))\). \(M=0\): part (1) gives \(K=0\), contradicting \(K\ge1\), so the hypotheses cannot hold with a zero in the disk, as they should not (\(f\) is then constant in modulus at most \(|f(s_0)|\), hence constant). In the corollary the margin of the first inequality is small, \(1-0.99178\): the parameters are near the edge of what this choice of \(t\) and \(r\) allows.

Nonvacuity. For the lemma: \(f(s)=1-2^{-s}\), \(\tau_0=2\pi j_0/\log2\), \(\mu=1\), \(R_1=9.5\), \(R_2=12\), \(\varepsilon=0.1\): the zero \(i\tau_0\) at distance \(2\) is the nearest, the next ones \(i\tau_0\pm2\pi i/\log2\) are at distance \(\sqrt{4+82.2}=9.28\le R_1\) and outside the shell, so \(K=3\), \(K_s=1\), and the term for the zeros outside the shell is present. For the corollary in parameters the script asserts the three inequalities at the stated values. Whether any height satisfies the premise on prime sums is not known.

What the exception is now

Only zeros nearly equidistant from \(s_0\) with the nearest one can defeat the exclusion: with \(\varepsilon=0.02\), those within two percent of the least distance. If \(\tfrac34+i\tau_0\) is itself the nearest, the shell is an annular sliver of width about \(0.07\) through it, curving to the right as the height leaves \(\tau_0\). The constant \(0.02\) comes from the logarithm \(\ell_s\approx9.4\) in the second main theorem's loss against margins of size about \(0.2\); it is the loss of the power-sum entries, now with its number. On whether \(0.02\) is near the best, from the reviewer's hand arithmetic, unverified: the condition \(h(t)>\log(1+r)\) forces \(\kappa_s\ell_s<1-2x\), approached only as \(r\to0\) where \(\ell_s\) grows; other parameters at \(x=1/4\) give up to about \(0.04\), a factor of two and no more; as \(x\to0\) with \(\mu\) fixed about \(0.13\) to \(0.15\); and \(1/\log(8e)\approx0.32\) is an absolute cap from the constant of the second main theorem. Unused in the corollary: the inequality with \(\lambda\log(1+1/\mu)\) never binds, and \(\varepsilon\le\log\theta\) is needed only for the statement on real parts.

11 October 2026 — Fourth route review, goal-level: the conditional exclusion proved for \(\zeta\) in this period is in substance a theorem in the supplied book, left unread for three entries; the import claims a zero-free half-plane for \(\zeta\) itself, and the next test reads its proof as a claim to be broken

Goal-level route review; no result about zeta zeros. Fourth exclusion review. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

New mathematics. None. Four statements of the supplied book are read and quoted under Falsification attempt and Outside leads; one of them shows that this period's corollaries for \(\zeta\) rediscover, in a weaker form, a published conditional theorem.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The six research entries since the last review each reported the obligation unchanged. Bearing. This review gives nothing at the goal's parameters; it selects the next test.

General statement. The line's claim is the open statement itself, off-line exclusion. No registered conjecture stands between the proved statements and it: the missing implication is unregistered, not absent. It is not registered as a conjecture because it is the goal itself, carried under its stable identifier in Open statements; the registry holds statements with evidence, and none exists for it.

Review. No stand-in review ran since the entry that answered the last one (family extraction reading). Of its five objections two remain open in fact: the suite is over its limit and undiagnosed (one run since then was taken on a saturated machine and measured nothing; the queued item suite-slow-kernel-tests is to be run when the shared slice is idle), and no queued check has been built.

What the six entries did. Two read sources: the proof of Turán's local implication, whose region is at most \(1/1600\) wide and whose loss is the second main theorem over a Jensen count (loss); and, at the user's suggestion, the outline of the import's eleven-twelfths preprint, whose saving comes from a second moment over a family of sextic twists in which the Möbius sum recurs (device). Four proved statements: the local inequality with free parameters (long sums, inapplicable to \(\zeta\) as first stated because of the pole); the arithmetic of extraction from moments (moments: at best \(1-1/(2k)\) in the large-sieve range, and below it a hypothesis that contains the conclusion); and for \(\zeta\) itself a conditional exclusion of an off-line zero from a saving \(\tau^{-1}\) in prime sums on lengths at least \(\tau^{2}\), with a cluster exception (disk, shell), empty below about \(e^{460}\). In every one of the six the reviewer withdrew or narrowed a conclusion of the draft.

What the goal needs. An unconditional inequality that a zero off the line violates. In the two forms now on record: a power saving at square-root strength for Möbius sums at every scale (height-free, from the import's first step, cited there to Littlewood and not read), or a saving \(\tau^{-1}\) for prime sums at one height on lengths at least \(\tau^{2}\) together with the absence of a dense off-line cluster.

Falsification attempt. Against the value of the one-height form: the book's converse, read in this cycle from page images and not checked, shows its premise is implied by a zero-free window, and the window is as wide as the height. Theorem 33.1's proof ends with \(|Z(\tau_0,1,N)|\le c(\delta)\,N\,T^{-B}\log^{2}T\log N\) for \(N\ge T^{B/(1-\Theta)}\), from \(\zeta\ne0\) in \(\sigma>\Theta\), \(|t-T|\le\tfrac\delta2T^{B}\), for \(|\tau_0-T|\le\tfrac{1-\delta}2T^{B}\), \(0<B\le1\), \(\tfrac12\le\Theta<1\). At \(B=1\), \(\Theta=\tfrac12\) that is the premise of the conditional exclusion (exponents \(a=2\), \(b=1\)), obtained from zero-freeness in a window \(|t-T|\le\tfrac\delta2T\): the dyadic differences cost a factor two, and \(\log^{2}T\log N\le\log^{3}N\) fits the premise's factor \(\log^{A}y\) for \(A\ge3\). So the premise is implied by the conclusion on a window of width proportional to the height, and no weaker source for it is known; this does not show the premise to be as strong as the conclusion, since the other direction is only the conditional exclusion with its cluster exception. The one-height form has content only if its premise is proved from the primes. The attempt leaves the implication standing and removes any appearance that its premise is local. Against the height-free form: the only device on record for it is the import's, unaudited, and by the moment arithmetic its extraction stops at \(1-1/(2k)\).

Assessment of this line. Weak. The period produced correct statements and no approach. For the function itself the route now has a proved implication from a statement about primes to the exclusion of an isolated off-line zero; that is the first statement in this notebook that is about \(\zeta\) and not a model. But it is in substance a published theorem: the book's Theorem 36.1, read in this cycle, obtains the same exponent \(b/a\) under the Lindelöf hypothesis, from prime sums on a short range of lengths only; this notebook's version replaces that hypothesis by a count of zeros in a shell, asks for all lengths, and has constants that put it beyond \(e^{460}\). Its premise is implied by the conclusion on a wide window and is not known from less. The import's device is the only thing seen that claims a saving without knowing the zeros, and what was learned about it is where it stops. I give no odds.

Obstacle

The line is stuck on a power saving, for sums of the Möbius or von Mangoldt function, that is proved from arithmetic and not from the location of zeros. Every premise on record is either implied by a zero-free region and known from nothing weaker (the converse read above) or, where a device is claimed (the import's second moment over a twisted family), is limited by the sparsity of the rows at which the sum recurs.

Outside leads

  • The converse in Turán's book and its remark on localization. Named source: P. Turán, On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications (1984), Sections 33.13 and 33.14, read as page images in this cycle, the thrice-deferred reading. Statement read: the display quoted under Falsification attempt; and the remark that in the direct theorem only the primes with \(\tau_0^{a}<p<\exp(\tau_0^{b/100})\) are used nontrivially, with a stronger localization promised in the next chapter. Target: the premise of the conditional exclusion. Answers. It would say whether the one-height premise is available from something weaker than the conclusion. Test. Falsified as a source of the premise: what is read gives the premise at exponents \((2,1)\) from zero-freeness in a window of width proportional to the height, and from nothing less.
  • A mean square restricted to rows near the recurrent ones. Named source: the import's key proposition (its Proposition 3.1, read) and prop:moment-extraction-exponents. The previous reviewer proposed restricting the mean square to rows with small sixth-power-free part, where the Möbius sum recurs more densely. Target: the height-free premise. Answers. It would raise the density of recurrence and lower the exponent below \(11/12\). Test. Not run for the translation. What the cycle has is two one-step objections: for fewer than \(D\) rows the one-row argument shows such a bound would be a stronger theorem than the import's, which says it is strong and not that it is false; and the stage-two reviewer's remark, unverified, that rows \(u=vw^6\) repeat the row \(v\), so that the restricted set is few distinct rows each repeated and the target recurs only at \(v=1\). Neither is a development of the lead; it is not picked and is recorded for the item queued from the user's suggestion.
  • The localized theorems of the same book, and the exact one under the Lindelöf hypothesis. Named source: the same book, Sections 34 to 36, statements read as page images in this cycle, proofs not read. Its Theorem 35.1, unconditional: if \(|Z(\tau_0,N_1,N_2)|<cN\log^{10}N/\tau_0^{\,b}\) for \(\tau_0^{D}\le N\le N_1<N_2\le\min(2N,\tau_0^{D(1+4b^{1/20})})\), with \(D\ge4\) and \(0<b\le\min(a_1,1/(64D^2))\), \(a_1\le3^{-20}\), then \(\zeta\ne0\) on \(\sigma\ge1-b^2\), \(t=\tau_0\). Its Theorem 36.1, under the Lindelöf hypothesis (\(|\zeta(s)|\le c(\epsilon)|t|^{\epsilon}\) for \(\sigma\ge1/2\)), with \(0<\delta<1/2\), \(6\delta^2\log\frac3\delta\le b\le1\), \(\delta\le b/a\le1/2\): if the same bound holds for \(\tau_0^{a}\le N\le N_1<N_2\le\min(2N,\tau_0^{a(1+2\delta)})\), then \(\zeta\ne0\) on \(\sigma\ge1-\frac ba(1-2\delta)\), \(t=\tau_0\), for \(\tau_0>c(\delta)\). Target: the premise of the one-height form. Answers. It needs prime sums on a short range of lengths only, and under one named hypothesis on \(\zeta\) it has the exact exponent \(b/a\) with no cluster exception stated; at \(b/a=1/2\) it reaches \(\sigma\ge\tfrac12+\delta\). Break point. The translation would break if the conditional theorem needed more than this notebook's count hypothesis, or less. What the test found: the exponent \(b/a\) is the one this notebook's corollaries reach, so they rediscover the theorem in a weaker form (all lengths, a count of zeros in a shell in place of the Lindelöf hypothesis, far worse constants); whether the count hypothesis follows from the Lindelöf hypothesis, as a theorem of Backlund recalled by me and unread would give, is not checked, and the proof of Theorem 36.1 is unread, so how it avoids the cluster loss is not known. Test. Passed: the statements are read and the translation is exact in its exponent; the comparison of hypotheses is the development.

Absurd bridges

  • Detection of a planted signal on a sparse set of coordinates. Translation: the family \((A_u)\) is a vector whose entries at the recurrent rows equal the target and whose other entries are noise of size \(D^{1/2}\); statistics asks when a planted value on \(H^{1/k}\) of \(H\) coordinates is detectable, and higher-order scan statistics detect below the second-moment threshold when the noise has light tails. A theorem there would give an exponent below \(1-1/(2k)\). Smallest test: what the light-tail hypothesis is here. Answers. It would replace the second moment by a finer statistic. Test. Falsified: light tails for the noise are bounds on higher moments of the family, and by prop:moment-extraction-exponents such bounds below the large-sieve range already contain the saving for every member.
  • The spiked random matrix transition. Translation: in the Gram model an off-line pair is a rank-two perturbation of size \(e^{\delta\Lambda}\) of a form whose bulk has norm controlled by local counts; random matrix theory says a spike separates from the bulk exactly above a threshold in its strength relative to the bulk's edge. A theorem there would say at which bandwidth the pair's eigenvalue leaves the bulk, with no second-moment bound. Smallest test: whether the prime side's form has a bulk edge that can be bounded. Answers. It would turn exclusion into an edge bound for the prime-side form. Test. Not run: the edge bound is the eigenvalue premise of prop:extreme-eigenvalue-detection, for which lem:prime-side-form-bound gives only the trivial bound; no theorem is named that would bound it; no source was sought because the bridge has no named theorem to seek, only a phenomenon, so it is recorded as an idea without a test and is not picked.

Architecture review

Necessary target. For every hypothetical zero \(\beta+i\gamma\) with \(\beta>1/2\): a proved inequality it violates. Downstream. Through cor:sparse-off-line-exclusion and cor:shell-off-line-exclusion the target at one height is: the saving \(\tau^{-1}\) for prime sums on lengths at least \(\tau^{2}\), plus fewer than \(\kappa\log\tau\) off-line zeros in a shell; the consequence is exclusion at that height for large \(\tau\), which is weaker than the goal by the cluster exception and by the threshold. Through the import's first step (unaudited, read) the target is a power saving \(D^{1/2+\epsilon}\) for Möbius sums at every scale, whose consequence is the goal. Through prop:moment-extraction-exponents no moment hypothesis on a twisted family reaches it without containing it.

Extra requirements. The one-height form demands a premise on all lengths above \(\tau^{2}\) and a count of hypothetical zeros; the height-free form demands neither. It is not a weaker endpoint: by the theorem of Littlewood that the import cites, unread here, it is equivalent to the hypothesis, a restatement. Its use is as the form in which a device like the import's is stated. The route carried the one-height form for four entries because the supplied book states it, and the book already had its sharp form.

Independent alternative. Before the route was revealed, the review's medium reviewer was given the goal and the results of the period, with no registered open conjecture to list, and asked for a different architecture (brief and reply). Its verdict: none found that reaches the line without an unproved statement equivalent to or stronger than the hypothesis. Its points, from memory unless stated. A pre-check: the import, if it admits the trivial character, claims a zero-free half-plane for \(\zeta\) itself and should be treated as probably wrong until its mean square is read line by line; I checked the admission in the preprint's first theorem, which says "every Dirichlet \(L\)-function, including \(\zeta(s)\)". On denser recurrence: none, since a residue symbol of order \(k\) is identically one on all \(n\le D\) only at essentially \(k\)-th powers; the floor \(1-1/(2k)\) is \(7/8\) at \(k=4\), which matches the companion's exponent, and the quadratic case looks out of the device's reach. On the weakest premise without a cluster exception: for each fixed \(\tau\), a bound \(y^{1/2+\epsilon}\) for the prime sums as \(y\to\infty\) with no uniformity in \(\tau\), which makes the tail of \(-\zeta'/\zeta(s+i\tau)\) analytic to the right of the line; it is equivalent to the hypothesis, and its only content is that uniformity and the range \(y\approx\tau^{2}\) are unnecessary, the cluster exception being intrinsic to that range. Two proposals: all moments of the family at a fixed number of rows, expected to fail at the fourth moment because the dual length after Poisson summation exceeds the number of rows; and a local density bound for the shell, against which stand the Harnack obstruction and Jensen's constant. Compared with the route: its second point is a height-free restatement for prime sums; its pre-check turns the decisive test into a reading with a sceptical purpose. It also gave the floors \(5/6\) at \(k=3\) and \(3/4\) at \(k=2\), and two audit points: the open implication is unregistered, and the power of the logarithm in the one-height premise had not been checked against what the hypothesis gives (checked above against the book's converse: \(A\ge3\)).

Rank normalization. Not a dimension count. The analogous question, whether loss and gain are measured on the same scale, is answered by the moment entry: the extraction's gain \(H^{1/k}\) and the mean square's size \(DH\) are both counted in rows, and the ratio is what fixes \(1-1/(2k)\).

Decision. Core. The open statement stays as it is. Its restatement as a power saving for Möbius sums at every scale is recorded as an equivalent form, not a weaker one, and the next cycles go to the one thing on record that claims such a saving: the import's key proposition, which is to be read in its proof, not its outline, and read as a claim to be broken: it asserts a zero-free half-plane for \(\zeta\) that the reviewers do not know from the literature.

Decisive test. Read the import's proof of its key proposition far enough to answer one question the outline does not: where the rows that are sixth powers, which already carry the saving, are handled, that is, which step of the proof would fail if the Möbius sum \(A_1(D)\) were as large as \(D^{11/12+\eta}\). Outcomes: if a step is found that visibly uses arithmetic of the sextic symbols at such rows (the diagonal after Poisson summation, or the cube-removal step), the route has its first mechanism to study, and the next entries test it on the simplest case; if the proposition's proof assumes or imports a bound of the same strength for some sum, that sum is the real premise and the route's statement of the obstacle changes to it; if an error is located, which is the likeliest outcome for a claim that covers \(\zeta\), the import leaves the route and the entry records the step; if the proof cannot be followed from the retained files, the entry says which statement is the first not checkable, and source validation becomes the highest-risk item. By the moment arithmetic the best outcome is a mechanism for a half-plane, not the line. Trivial cases. The quantity compared is the size of \(\sum_{N(u)\le H}|A_u|^2\) against \(D^{1+\epsilon}H\). Under the trivial transformation of replacing the coefficients by ones the left side is at least \(D^2H^{1/6}\), which exceeds \(D^{1+\epsilon}H\) when \(H<D^{6/5}\), the proposition's range \(\vartheta\le1/10\) included; so in that range the proposition is false for general coefficients, and no trivial instance gives the first outcome; a proof that never used the Möbius function would be wrong, which is a check on the reading. Power. Recorded limits on the test's object: prop:moment-extraction-exponents says what the proposition gives and that it cannot be a general large sieve; the family-optimum entry says the companion's second stage has no slack. The test is a reading; its outcomes differ in what the next entry studies, and the retained TeX is complete for this preprint (about four thousand lines), so the reading is within reach. Record search. The registry was searched for sextic Gauss sum Poisson summation diagonal sixth power rows Mobius mean square, and for cubic theta function cube removal Mobius inversion large sieve dual: the first results are unrelated claims of other notebooks; the registered statements that bear on it are the imported theorem and prop:moment-extraction-exponents. The earlier reviews' tests concern the companion's scales, the Harnack obstruction and the book's proof, and are different objects.

Proposed next step. Route item: rh-exclusion. The Decisive test above, as a research entry on the item queued from the user's suggestion, with an Architecture test paragraph.
