What was read

In the retained TeX of the preprint of 5 October 2026: lines 934 to 1030 (the definition of the dual mean square, the lemma removing exclusions with its proof, and the statement of the reduction proposition with the first lines of its proof) and lines 1254 to 1332 (the statements of the dual estimate and of the completed mean square). The proofs of the reduction, of the dual estimate, of the completed mean square and of the transfer estimate are not read.

The statements

  • The dual mean square. With \(a_\xi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\xi(n)\) (the preprint's line 807; the identity trading the Möbius function is in its outline, read earlier), \(\gamma_2\) the normalized Gauss sum of the cubic character \(\chi_n^2\), \(\alpha(n)=n/|n|\), \(\xi\) a ray class character: \(E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)=\frac1{XF}\sum_{F\le N(f)<2F}\sum_{0<N(k)\le\mathcal H}\big|\sum_na_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(N(n)/X)\big|^2\), the sums over \(f\) and \(n\) restricted to squarefree elements prime to the excluded set, the sum over \(k\) unrestricted.
  • The reduction. If \(E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)\ll D^{\epsilon}XF\) for \(X=D/(BF)\), \(\mathcal H\le CD^2/(HB^2)\), \(H=D^{1+\vartheta}\), \(0<\vartheta\le1/10\), all \(B,F\ge1\), with the constant uniform in a norm of finitely many derivatives of \(W\), then the mean square for the Möbius family holds. The preprint says its proof applies Poisson summation directly to the Möbius sums.
  • The dual estimate. For \(\mathcal H,X,F\ge1\), \(\Sigma=XF\le D^{C_0}\), \(\mathcal H\le\Sigma D^{-\kappa}\): \(E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)\ll D^{\epsilon}\Sigma\). Its proof is announced from the next statement and a transfer proposition.
  • The completed mean square. For the completed sums \(T(X;k,f)\) of theta coefficients, normalized by \(X^{-1/2}\): \(\sum_{0<N(k)\ll\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(f)/X)\) for \(1\le\mathcal H,X,N(f)\le D^{C_0}\).

Where the saving is

If \(A_1(D)\) were large, the Möbius mean square would fail at the rows \(p^6\) (the extraction read earlier), hence by the reduction the dual estimate would fail for some admissible scales, provided the reduction's ranges lie inside the dual estimate's: \(\mathcal H\le CD^{1-\vartheta}/B^2\le(D/B)D^{-\kappa}\) needs \(\kappa<\vartheta\), which the dual estimate allows; and the reduction quantifies over all \(B,F\ge1\), so \(X=D/(BF)\) below one occurs there while the dual estimate asks \(X\ge1\), a point for the reading of the reduction's proof. Given the reduction, the saving is carried by the dual estimate. At \(F=1\) it reads \(\sum_{0<N(k)\le\mathcal H}|\sum_na_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)W(N(n)/X)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}X^2\) for \(\mathcal H\le XD^{-\kappa}\): the bound a large sieve of optimal strength \(X(X+\mathcal H)\) would give, for these particular coefficients, with rows \(k\) not restricted to squarefree ones. The Möbius function has left the statement; what is claimed is a property of cubic Gauss sums.

What the repeated rows force

My draft's two tests concluded that the dual estimate asserts no cancellation in a single sum and is not excluded for general coefficients. Both conclusions were wrong; the reviewer's corrections, whose arithmetic I checked, replace them.

  • Sixth-power rows. For \(k=m^6\), \(\chi_n(k)=1\) when \((n,m)=1\), so about \(\mathcal H^{1/6}\) rows repeat the untwisted sum up to a coprimality condition. The dual estimate at \(F=1\) then gives, on average over \(m\), \(|\sum_na_\xi(n)W(N(n)/X)|\le X\mathcal H^{-1/12}D^{\epsilon}\); at \(\mathcal H=X^{1-\kappa}\) this is \(X^{11/12+\kappa/12+\epsilon}\). So the dual estimate does assert cancellation in the untwisted sum of Gauss coefficients, with the same exponent \(11/12\) the Möbius sums get. Likewise the rows \(k=m^2\) give about \(\mathcal H^{1/2}\) purely cubic twists and \(k=m^3\) about \(\mathcal H^{1/3}\) quadratic ones.
  • General coefficients. With \(a(n)=1\) the same rows contribute about \(\mathcal H^{1/6}X^2\), more than \(D^{\epsilon}X^2\). So the dual estimate at \(F=1\) is false for general coefficients, and a proof of it must use the Gauss coefficients; the check that a proof never using its coefficients would be wrong applies to it as well as to the reduction.
  • One row of the completed mean square. Taking \(\mathcal H\) of order one, which the statement allows: \(|T(X;k,f)|\ll D^{\epsilon/2}(1+N(f)/X)^{1/2}\) for \(k\) of bounded norm, square-root cancellation for the completed sum. The reviewer's point, from memory and unread: sums of cubic Gauss sums have a main term of order \(X^{5/6}\) from a pole, which without the angular factor \(\overline{\alpha(n)}\) would make \(T(X;1,1)\) of order \(X^{1/3}\) and contradict this; the source removes the polar term by a device at its lines 3305 to 3358 (a derivative in \(\bar z\) at \(z=0\) making a Mellin transform entire), and another device at lines 1711 to 1717 removes a constant term at one cusp only. Whether the polar term is removed at every cusp, uniformly in \(k\) and \(f\), is the hinge. I had written that square-root size is "expected"; that was a belief and is withdrawn.

Sub-ideas

  • Audit the hinge: read the source's proof of the completed mean square, in particular the cusp expansions and the removal of the polar term (its lines 3296 to 3362 and the passage at 1690 to 1720), and decide whether the Mellin transform used is entire at every cusp, uniformly in the row \(k\) and in \(f\); then the transfer proposition (line 1487), which must handle \(\mathcal H^2>X\).
  • Lower-risk checks, after the hinge: the reduction's proof, including the scales with \(X<1\), and the identity trading the Möbius function tested on single primes of \(\mathbb Z[\omega]\) by exact computation. Retrieve and read the theorem of Dunn and Radziwiłł the preprint cites; by the reviewer's arithmetic, unverified, a sharpness example for the cubic large sieve does not contradict the dual estimate, since only square rows twist cubically and they give at most \(X^{5/3}\mathcal H^{1/3}\le X^2\).

Follow-up. Continuing: the saving is located in one statement; breaking or checking it is the item's remaining application.   • Other extractions. Not attempted in this entry. Follow-up. Continuing: after the audit steps above.

11 October 2026 — The hinge of the import read: the polar term of the cubic theta function is removed by a derivative in \(\bar z\) at \(z=0\), which is also what produces the angular factor; three derivative identities are checked and the cusp expansions are taken from the source; for a fixed twist the step holds, which tests nothing about the dependence on the row, and the claim is still neither broken nor checked

General statement. None of mine. The statement examined is the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its intermediate preprint; unaudited.

What was read

In the retained TeX of the preprint of 5 October 2026: lines 1639 to 1730 (the theta function's normalization with its constant term, the twisted theta function, the lemma realizing the completed sum, and its proof) and lines 3284 to 3372 (the archimedean transform: the Dirichlet series of the completed sum, the Mellin transform of a derivative of the twisted theta function, and the argument that it is entire), with the two displays at lines 3161 to 3170 that give the automorphy law and the cusp coordinates. The derivation of the automorphy law, the cusp expansions (line 1769) and everything after line 3372 are not read.

The device, as read

  • The theta function is \(\theta(z,v)=\frac{3^{5/2}}2v^{2/3}+\sum_{\ell\ne0}\tau(\ell)vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)e^{2\pi i(\ell z+\overline{\ell z})}\): a constant term in the horizontal variable, and Fourier modes. The twisted function \(\Theta_k\) multiplies the coefficient at \(\ell\) by a periodic function \(\phi_k(\lambda^3\ell)\) with \(\phi_k(0)=0\), and equals \(\sum_h\widehat\phi_k(h)\overline{\theta(z+\lambda^2h/q,v)}\); since \(\sum_h\widehat\phi_k(h)=\phi_k(0)=0\), the constant term at infinity cancels.
  • The completed sum carries the angular factor \(\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}\). The source obtains it by differentiating in \(\bar z\): each mode acquires \(2\pi i\bar\ell\), and with the factor \(|\ell|^{-1}\) from the Bessel integral this is \(\overline{\alpha(\ell)}\). It defines \(\mathcal J(s)=\int_0^\infty\partial_{\bar z}\Theta_\Psi(z,v)|_{z=0}v^{2s-1}dv\) and shows \(\mathcal J(s)=\frac{3^{5/2}}4(27/(2\pi)^2)^s\Gamma(s+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s+\tfrac23)\mathcal T(s,\Psi)\), where \(\mathcal T\) is the Dirichlet series of the completed sum, the product of the series of \(\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)\) over squarefree \(n\) and the series of \(\overline{\alpha(b)}^3\Psi(b)^3N(b)^{-3s+1/2}\).
  • The claim that \(\mathcal J\) is entire: near each cusp \(a/c\) the automorphy law writes \(\overline{\theta(z+a/c,v)}\) through \(\overline\theta\) at the point with coordinates \(z'=-\delta'/c-\bar z/(c^2(v^2+|z|^2))\), \(v'=v/(N(c)(v^2+|z|^2))\); at \(z=0\), \(\partial z'/\partial\bar z=-1/(c^2v^2)\), \(\partial\overline{z'}/\partial\bar z=0\), \(\partial v'/\partial\bar z=0\); so the derivative in \(\bar z\) is \(-(cv)^{-2}\partial_{z'}\) with no derivative in the height, the constant mode at that cusp is removed, and the other modes decay exponentially as \(v\to0\) because \(v'\to\infty\).

The check

From the displayed coordinates, with \(Q=v^2+z\bar z\): \(\partial_{\bar z}(\bar z/Q)=1/Q-\bar z\,z/Q^2\), which is \(1/v^2\) at \(z=0\), so \(\partial z'/\partial\bar z=-1/(c^2v^2)\); \(\overline{z'}=-\overline{\delta'}/\bar c-z/(\bar c^2Q)\) and \(\partial_{\bar z}(z/Q)=-z^2/Q^2=0\) at \(z=0\); \(\partial_{\bar z}(v/Q)=-vz/Q^2=0\) at \(z=0\). The three identities hold. Their consequence: a function of the height \(v'\) alone, such as a constant term proportional to \(v'^{2/3}\) at a cusp, has vanishing \(\bar z\)-derivative at \(z=0\); a mode \(e^{2\pi i(\ell z'+\overline{\ell z'})}\) times a function of \(v'\) is multiplied by \(2\pi i\ell\cdot(-1/(c^2v^2))\). So the removal of the constant mode at every cusp is correct given the automorphy law and the coordinates as displayed. The reviewer read the preceding lines 3020 to 3139 and reports, which I cite and did not read myself: the coordinates follow by a direct computation from the factorization of the group element, and the law from it; the map \(H\) is settled there as a statement of the source, the expansion being of \(\overline\theta\circ H\) in the coordinates \((z',v')\) with constant term \(\tfrac{3^{5/2}}2v'^{2/3}\) at one type of cusp and none at the two others, a function of the height only, imported from Patterson and from Dunn and Radziwiłł; the set of cusps is finite for a fixed twist; the decay \(\exp(-c'/v)\) beats the factor \((cv)^{-2}v^{2\Re s-1}\). So for a fixed twist the entireness holds given the cited cusp expansions. Not checked by anyone here: those cusp expansions in their sources, and the uniformity in the row \(k\) and in \(f\).

A consistency observation

Mine, unverified beyond the algebra. If \(\mathcal T(s,\Psi)\) is entire, the series of the Gauss coefficients alone is \(\mathcal T(s,\Psi)\) divided by the second factor, which is a partial Hecke \(L\)-function at \(3s-\tfrac12\) if \(\Psi^3\) is a Hecke character on primary \(b\), as I assume from lines not read; its zeros have \(\Re(3s-\tfrac12)\le1\), that is \(\Re s\le\tfrac12\). So the sum of \(\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)\) over squarefree \(n\) against a smooth weight of scale \(X\) would be \(\ll_\Psi X^{1/2+\epsilon}\) by a contour shift, the only unchecked input being the vertical growth of \(\mathcal T\). That is far below the exponent \(11/12\) that the dual estimate forces at the sixth-power rows (previous entry), so at those rows the dual estimate asks nothing the entireness would not give. This is the cube-removal step seen in one row; the source's difficulty must lie in doing it on average over rows with conductor growing, which is its transfer proposition.

Then the uniformity in \(k\) and \(f\) of the cusp expansions (line 1769 and the appendix), including the map \(H\) in the automorphy law.

11 October 2026 — The proof of Turán's exact local implication read: by its author's statement it uses the Lindelöf hypothesis at one point only, to bound the zeros in a fixed box to the right of the line; this notebook's conditional exclusions for \(\zeta\) rediscover its method, in a form weaker in the lengths needed

Question. The missing implication is an estimate excluding one off-line zero. The book's Theorem 36.1 reaches the exponent \(b/a\) under the Lindelöf hypothesis. How does its proof treat the other zeros near the height, and what exactly does it take from that hypothesis?

New mathematics. None. The theorem is registered as thm:turan-lindelof-exact-implication, published, read and not audited, and three recorded corollaries are related to it as rediscoveries in a weaker form.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The second outcome of the Best outcome paragraph holds: the published proof has no device against the other zeros beyond a count. It discards zeros far in height and zeros near the line by the decay of a kernel, and for the rest it applies the second main theorem with their number bounded by \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\), which it takes from the Lindelöf hypothesis, in the author's words "here, and only here". So the cluster exception of this notebook's corollaries corresponds to the published theorem's use of its hypothesis, and nothing in Sections 36.1 to 36.7 removes it. That the hypothesis is used nowhere else is the author's claim; the pages read show no other use (the bound on the line \(\Re z=-1\) and the count \(c(\delta)\log\tau_0\) are unconditional), but the kernel lemma of Section 34 and the reasoning of Section 35.3 that the proof repeats "word for word" were not read. General statement. thm:turan-lindelof-exact-implication, for all \(\delta,a,b\) in its stated ranges and all sufficiently large \(\tau_0\); a published statement, not a result of this notebook.

The statement

P. Turán, On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications (1984), Section 36, read as page images from the copy supplied by the user (statement and the whole proof, Sections 36.1 to 36.6). Assume the Lindelöf hypothesis, \(|\zeta(s)|\le c(\epsilon)|t|^{\epsilon}\) for \(\sigma\ge1/2\), \(|t|\ge2\). Let \(0<\delta<1/4\) (the bound is hard to read in the scan; the proof states \(0<\delta<1/4\) and uses \(1-\delta/3\ge11/12\)), \(6\delta^2\log(3/\delta)\le b\le1\), \(\delta\le b/a\le1/2\). Theorem (thm:turan-lindelof-exact-implication). If for a \(\tau_0>10\) \[|Z(\tau_0,N_1,N_2)|<c\,\frac{N\log^{10}N}{\tau_0^{\,b}}\quad\text{for}\quad\tau_0^{a}\le N\le N_1<N_2\le\min\big(2N,\tau_0^{a(1+2\delta)}\big),\] with \(Z(\tau,N_1,N_2)=\sum_{N_1\le n\le N_2}\Lambda(n)e^{-i\tau\log n}\), then \(\zeta(s)\ne0\) on the segment \(\sigma\ge1-\frac ba(1-2\delta)\), \(t=\tau_0\), if only \(\tau_0>c(\delta)\).

The proof, as read

  • The kernel. With \(s_0=1+\frac\delta2\frac ba+i\tau_0\) and an integer \(k\) between \(\frac{b}{1-\delta/3}\log\tau_0\) and \((1+\frac{2\delta}3)b\log\tau_0\), it integrates \(\varphi(w)^k\frac{\zeta'}{\zeta}(s_0+w)\) with \(\varphi(w)=e^{(a/b)w}\frac{3b}{\delta aw}\sinh(\frac{\delta a}{3b}w)\). By a lemma on the Fourier transform of such powers, the Dirichlet series side involves only \(n\) in an interval inside \([\tau_0^{a},\tau_0^{a(1+2\delta)}]\); the hypothesis bounds it by \(c(\delta)\tau_0^{-b}\log^{11}\tau_0\).
  • The zeros. Shifting the line of integration to \(\Re w=-2-\mu\), with "a well-known inequality for \(\zeta'/\zeta\)" on the line \(\Re z=-1\), gives \(|\sum_\rho z_\rho^{k}|<c(\delta)\tau_0^{-b}\log^{11}\tau_0\) with \(z_\rho=\varphi(\rho-s_0)\), the sum over all zeros; the residue at the pole, \(\varphi(1-s_0)^k\), is below \(\tau_0^{-b}\).
  • Zeros far in height. For \(|\gamma-\tau_0|>\frac{18}\delta\frac ba\), \(|z_\rho|\le1/\nu\) on the \(\nu\)-th block of heights, and their total is below \(c(\delta)\tau_0^{-b}\log\tau_0\).
  • Zeros near the line. For \(|\gamma-\tau_0|\le\frac{18}\delta\frac ba\) and \(\beta\le\frac12+\frac\delta2\frac ba\), each \(|z_\rho^{k}|\le\tau_0^{-b}\), and there are at most \(c(\delta)\log\tau_0\) of them.
  • The rest. The zeros with \(\beta>\frac12+\frac\delta2\frac ba\), \(|\gamma-\tau_0|\le\frac{18}\delta\frac ba\). The book: "We again need \(N\), the upper bound for \(n\), the number of terms in the power sum. Here—and only here—we shall use the Lindelöf conjecture (or rather its well-known consequence)", namely that the number \(H_\eta(T)\) of zeros in the parallelogram \(\sigma\ge\frac12+\eta\), \(T\le t\le T+1\), \(\eta\) positive and arbitrarily small, satisfies \(H_\eta(T)/\log T\to0\) (a displayed limit, paraphrased here); from it the book takes \(N=\delta^3\log\tau_0\) for the zeros of this group, which lie in a window of heights of length up to \(36/\delta\cdot b/a\) at the fixed \(\eta=\frac\delta2\frac ba\). The second main theorem with \(m=\frac{b}{1-\delta/3}\log\tau_0\) then bounds the power sum below by the hypothetical zero's term times a loss of at most \(\tau_0^{-3\delta^3\log(3/\delta)}\), and \(6\delta^2\log(3/\delta)\le b\) makes that loss at most \(\tau_0^{-b\delta/2}\). The book's final displays, as I read them, give the zero's term above \(\tau_0^{-b+b\delta(5/6)}\) and the power sum above \(\tau_0^{-b+b\delta/3}\); the reviewer's recomputation of the zero's exponent is \(-b(1+\tfrac{2\delta}3)(1-\tfrac{3\delta}2)\), which is consistent with that; I did not redo it. Contradiction with the upper bound.

Against this notebook's corollaries

  • The hypothesis on zeros. By its author's statement the proof uses the Lindelöf hypothesis only to have at most \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\) zeros with \(\beta>\tfrac12+\tfrac\delta2\tfrac ba\) and \(|\gamma-\tau_0|\le\tfrac{18}\delta\tfrac ba\): a count with a fixed constant in a fixed box, not a unit window and not a little-o. cor:sparse-off-line-exclusion and cor:shell-off-line-exclusion assume a count \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) of zeros in a disk, or in a shell, lying to the right of the midpoint between the line and the zero. So the cluster exception of those corollaries is not something the published theorem escapes: the published theorem needs a count of the same kind and obtains it from a conjecture. Neither statement implies the other: this notebook's carry no conjecture and count in a different region; the published one needs prime sums on a short range only. That the count follows from the Lindelöf hypothesis is the book's statement ("its well-known consequence"); I have not read a proof of it.
  • The lengths. The published theorem needs the prime sums on \([\tau_0^{a},\tau_0^{a(1+2\delta)}]\) only; the corollaries here need all lengths above \(\tau_0^{a}\). The kernel \(\varphi^k\) is what localizes, in place of this notebook's plain derivative of order \(k\). The published form is stronger in this respect.
  • The exponent and constants. Both reach \(1-\beta<b/a\) up to a factor \(1-2\delta\) or a margin. The published count \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\) plays the part of \(\kappa\log\tau_0\); at \(b/a=1/2\), \(x=1/4\) this notebook's \(\kappa_s=0.02\) (the saved constants of the shell entry) is of the size of \(\delta^3\approx0.016\) at \(\delta=1/4\), the edge of the published range. Neither is usable at an accessible height.
  • The shell. lem:shell-zero-sum-inequality restricts the count to zeros nearly as close to the centre as the nearest; the published proof restricts it instead to a fixed box to the right of \(\tfrac12+\frac\delta2\frac ba\). I do not find the shell form in the pages read; it is a small variation and I claim nothing for it.
  • The growth. The published proof moves the contour to \(\Re w=-2-\mu\) and uses a bound for \(\zeta'/\zeta\) to the left of the strip; lem:two-radius-zero-sum-inequality stays in \(\sigma\ge1/4\) with an elementary bound. A difference of technique, not of strength.

Degenerate cases. \(\delta\to0\): the range of lengths shrinks to a point and \(b\ge6\delta^2\log(3/\delta)\) allows \(b\to0\); the count \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\) and the threshold \(c(\delta)\) degenerate. \(b/a=1/2\): the segment is \(\sigma\ge\tfrac12+\delta\), the nearest the theorem comes to the line. A height with no zero to the right of \(\tfrac12+\frac\delta2\frac ba\) in the box: the power sum is empty and the conclusion is trivially true.

Nonvacuity. The parameter ranges are non-empty: \(\delta=0.2\), \(b=1\), \(a=2\) satisfy \(6\delta^2\log(3/\delta)=0.65\le1\) and \(\delta\le b/a=\tfrac12\). Whether the theorem's two hypotheses can hold together is not known; under the Riemann hypothesis both should, by the book's converse (its statement read, its proof not) and because the Riemann hypothesis implies the Lindelöf hypothesis, a classical statement I have not read.

The remarks after the proof

Section 36.7, read: the theorem's "semilocal" corollary for all heights in \(|\tau_0-T|\le\tfrac12T^{b}\), giving a zero-free parallelogram; and the converse specialized to \(\Theta=1-\frac ba(1-2\delta)\), \(B=b\), which from that parallelogram gives the bound for the prime sums on lengths above \(T^{a/(1-2\delta)}\). The book says the comparison "shows an almost completely inverse character" under the hypothesis, and that without it only qualitative inverse theorems for small \(b\) follow. A further corollary begins on the last page read. No unconditional substitute for the count is given in these pages.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the rest of Section 36 after the page reached here (the third corollary and what follows), which Section 35.7 announces as including remarks on unconditional results, for anything that replaces the count; and decide from a read source whether a count \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\) at one fixed \(\eta\) is known for any \(\eta<1/2\) (the reviewer recalls that the count for every \(\eta\) is equivalent to the Lindelöf hypothesis, by a theorem of Backlund, unread).

Remark. The published proof, with its one sentence on the Lindelöf hypothesis replaced by the assumption it draws from it, at most \(\delta^3\log\tau_0\) zeros in the box \(\beta>\tfrac12+\tfrac\delta2\tfrac ba\), \(|\gamma-\tau_0|\le\tfrac{18}\delta\tfrac ba\), gives the same conclusion at that height. This is bookkeeping on a published proof I have not audited, recorded as a remark and not registered.

Follow-up. Continuing: the comparison is made and the rediscovery recorded; the item's remaining application to the open statement is whether anything replaces the count, the sub-idea above. General statement. None of mine. The statement examined is the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its intermediate preprint; unaudited.

What was read

In the retained TeX of the preprint of 5 October 2026, lines 1334 to 1424 (the cube cutoff, the cube-reduction lemma and the first half of its proof) and lines 1469 to 1612 (the transfer estimate's statement and the whole proof of the dual estimate). Not read: the second half of the cube lemma's proof (lines 1424 to 1468), the proof of the transfer estimate (its Section 7, about 470 lines), the proof of the completed mean square beyond what the previous entries read.

The three inputs

  • The completed mean square (read in the earlier entry): \(\sum_{0<N(k)\ll\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(f)/X)\).
  • The cube-reduction lemma. With \(H_c^3=\min(X,X^2/\mathcal H^2)\) and \(L_b=X/N(b)^3\): for \(1\le\mathcal H,X,F\le D^{C_0}\), \(\mathcal H\le\Sigma=XF\), \(E(\mathcal H,X,F)\ll D^{\epsilon}\big(\Sigma+\sup_bE(\mathcal H,L_b,F)\big)\), the supremum over \(b\) with \(N(b)>H_c\), \(L_b>1\), empty when \(\mathcal H^2\le X\).
  • The transfer estimate. For \(1\le\mathcal H,L,F,\Sigma\le D^{C_0}\), \(\max(\mathcal H,LF)\le\Sigma\): the smoothed mean square at column scale \(L\), which dominates \(E(\mathcal H,L,F)\), is \(\ll D^{\epsilon}\Sigma(1+\sup E(\mathcal H',X',F')/\Sigma')\) over scales with \(\mathcal H'\le\mathcal HL/(\Sigma F)\), \(\mathcal H'/\Sigma'\le\mathcal H/\Sigma\), \(\Sigma'=X'F'\le L\), with test weights of bounded norm on an enlarged interval.

What is checked

  • The short half of the cube inversion. The Gauss-coefficient sum is written by Möbius inversion over the cube index as \(\sum_h\frac{\mu(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^3\Psi_k(h)^3}{N(h)}T(X/N(h)^3;k,f)\), since \(\sum_{h\mid b}\mu(h)\) vanishes unless \(b=1\). For \(N(h)\le H_c\), Cauchy–Schwarz with weights \(1/N(h)\) and the completed mean square at scale \(X/N(h)^3\) give \(\big(\sum_{N(h)\le H_c}\frac1{N(h)}\big)\sum_{N(h)\le H_c}\frac1{N(h)}\big(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2FN(h)^3/X\big)\ll\log\cdot\big(\log\cdot\,\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2FH_c^3/X\big)\), the completed mean square being applied with range \(C_0+1\) and at scales \(X/N(h)^3\ge1\), which holds because \(H_c^3\le X\). With \(H_c^3\le X^2/\mathcal H^2\) the second term is at most \(XF=\Sigma\), and \(\mathcal H\le\Sigma\). So the short part is \(\ll D^{\epsilon}\Sigma\): correct as arithmetic. The Möbius function appears here only as a bounded coefficient; no cancellation in it is used.
  • The induction. Claim for \(\mathcal H\le D^{j\kappa}\), by induction on \(j\). Base \(j=0\): \(\mathcal H\le1\), the trivial bound \(E\ll\mathcal HX\le\Sigma\) since \(F\ge1\). Step: for \(\mathcal H\le D^{(j+1)\kappa}\) apply the cube lemma; if its supremum is not empty then \(\mathcal H^2>X\), \(H_c^3=X^2/\mathcal H^2\), and for each \(b\) the transfer estimate applies at column scale \(L_b\) because \(\mathcal H\le\Sigma\) and \(L_bF\le XF\). The scales it produces satisfy \(\mathcal H'\le\mathcal HL_b/(\Sigma F)=\mathcal H/(N(b)^3F^2)<\mathcal H\cdot\mathcal H^2/(X^2F^2)=\mathcal H(\mathcal H/\Sigma)^2\le D^{-2\kappa}\mathcal H\le D^{j\kappa}\), using \(N(b)^3>H_c^3\) and the hypothesis \(\mathcal H\le\Sigma D^{-\kappa}\); and \(\mathcal H'/\Sigma'\le\mathcal H/\Sigma\le D^{-\kappa}\), \(\Sigma'\le L_b\le D^{C_0}\). So the induction hypothesis covers them, with a derivative order fixed before the step and constants depending on \(j\); \(j\) runs to \(\lceil C_0/\kappa\rceil\), finitely many steps. Each line of this I recomputed; the induction is sound given the three inputs. The uniformity it needs holds, as the reviewer confirmed: the hypothesis is for every interval with a derivative order independent of the interval, the characters \(\xi'\) range over a finite group, and the supremum over \(b\) is over finitely many \(b\) with constants independent of \(b\). The loss per step is a factor \(D^{\epsilon}\) with \(\epsilon\) chosen per step, which is harmless for finitely many steps.

What this leaves

The reviewer's reading, which I cite and did not make (the preprint's lines 1424 to 1468 and 2217 to 2686). The long half of the cube lemma is elementary bookkeeping with a loss of a power of the logarithm and uses no cancellation in the Möbius function. In the transfer estimate no error was found: the common factor of the two column indices is handled exactly; the zero frequency of the first Poisson step gives the diagonal, of size \(O(\mathcal H)\); the family closes because the identity \(\gamma_1\gamma_2=\mu\alpha G\), with \(G\) a class function, turns Gauss coefficients into Möbius coefficients and back, the reviewer having compared it with the Hasse–Davenport relation from memory; the average over \(f\) exists to absorb the factor \(\chi_n(w)^4\) that removing a coprimality condition produces; the scales and the second diagonal were recomputed; the only signed step is an exact local count, after which a condition is dropped by positivity; no cancellation is assumed. Its conclusion: the transfer and the long half can be counted as read with no error found, conditional on two auxiliary lemmas (the preprint's arithmetic identities and its separation of variables in smooth weights), whose appendix proofs nobody here has read.

So for the dual estimate the remaining weight is the completed mean square, whose proof rests on the theta function's transformation and a quadratic large sieve over the number field and was read only up to the removal of the polar term, together with those two lemmas. For the import's theorem the reduction and the cited theorems remain as well. On the repeated rows nothing new is forced beyond the previous entries.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the proof of the completed mean square (the preprint's lines 1632 to 2216): the transformation formula for the twisted theta function with its dependence on the row \(k\) and on \(f\), and the application of the quadratic large sieve to the dual sums; check the dual length \(N(k)^2N(f)/X\) and the bound \(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(f)/X\) on the rows where the twist is trivial, quadratic or cubic. Then compare the final mean square with the lower-bound example of Dunn and Radziwiłł for the cubic large sieve, from its source.

Question. The import's dual estimate rests on its completed mean square, \(\sum_{0<N(k)\ll\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(f)/X)\). How is it proved, does the proof hold, and what happens on the rows that are not squarefree?

New mathematics. None of mine. The proof of the import's completed mean square from three lemmas stated there and one cited large sieve is read and its arithmetic checked; the local factors of its transformation formula are rederived in a sketch from one recalled property of the theta function.

Goal progress. Unchanged. No error is found. The device is now identified, as read: coefficients that become theta coefficients once the Möbius function is absorbed by a Gauss-sum identity, and a sextic row twist that the theta transformation turns into a quadratic one. The source needs no transplant to reach \(\zeta\): its main theorem gets \(\zeta\) through \(\zeta_K=\zeta\cdot L(\chi_{-3})\) over the Eisenstein field (the reviewer's reading of the preprint's line 74), and a height would enter only through the weight, \(W(x)x^{-i\tau}\) having \(C^J\) norm about \(\tau^J\). So if the import is right it already speaks about \(\zeta\), with no uniformity in the height; whether its scheme has a version over the rationals directly is an untested question, on which the reviewer's recalled reason against is listed below. Converted. Nothing at the goal's parameters. Bearing. None; the cycle was spent on it because the fourth review made this audit the decisive test and the user asked what the method's device is.

General statement. None of mine. The statement examined is the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its intermediate preprint; unaudited.

The proof, as read

  • Rows. Write \(k=u_0sv^2\) with \(s\) squarefree. Since \(\chi_n(v^2)=\chi_n(v^2)^4\) (the exponent \(8\equiv2\) modulo six), \(T(X;u_0sv^2,f)=T(X;u_0s,fv^2)\): the square part of the row is moved into the auxiliary twist \(g=fv^2\). The proposition follows from a lemma for squarefree rows with a general \(g\), bound \(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(g)/X\), applied with row bound \(\mathcal H/N(v)^2\): \(\mathcal H/N(v)^2+\mathcal H^2N(f)N(v)^2/(N(v)^4X)=(\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(f)/X)/N(v)^2\), summable over \(v\). Checked.
  • The transformation. For a twist \(\Psi=\Psi_0\prod_p\chi_p^{j_p}\), the source's transformation formula (stated, proved in its appendix, unread) writes \(T(X;\Psi)\) as \(O(2^{|\mathcal P|})\) dual sums (in the application divisor-boundedly many in \(tg\), every prime of \(k_0\) being active) \(C\sum_\ell\frac{d(\ell)\alpha(\ell)}{\sqrt{N(\ell)}}\psi(\lambda^4\ell)\prod_{p}B_{p,j_p}(\lambda^4\ell)V^\sharp(N(\ell)X/N(c)^2)\) over coefficients \(d\) of the theta function at three cusps, with \(c\) the product of the active primes and local factors \(B_{p,j}(x)=\chi_p(x)^{-j-2}\) for \(j\ne0,4\), \(N(p)^{-1/2}(-1+N(p)1_{p\mid x})\) for \(j=4\), \(N(p)^{-1/2}\chi_p(x)^{-2}\) for \(j=0\). By reciprocity the row \(k\) and \(g\) give \(j_p\equiv v_p(k)+4v_p(g)\).
  • The key case. A prime of the squarefree part \(k_0\) of the row prime to \(g\) has \(j_p=1\), and \(B_{p,1}=\chi_p^{-3}=\chi_p^{3}\), a quadratic character. So the dual sum is twisted by the quadratic character of \(k_0\), has length about \(N(c)^2/X\), and bounded coefficients.
  • The other primes. For \(j_p=4\) the factor is \(-q^{-1/2}+q^{1/2}1_{p\mid n}+q^{1/2}1_{p\nmid n,p\mid b}\) on \(\ell=u\lambda^mnb^3\), and the substitutions \(n=pn'\), \(b=pb'\) return sums of the same form; the source tracks a coefficient bound \(a\) and a length \(Y\) with \(a^2Y\) growing by at most \(N(p)^2\) at primes of odd exponent and by \(N(p)\) at \(j_p\in\{0,4\}\). I checked the three updates for \(j_p=4\): \((a^2/q,q^2Y)\), \((a^2,qY)\), \((a^2/q,Y/q)\), each with \(a^2Y\) multiplied by at most \(q\); and the total \(a^2Y\ll\mathcal H_0^2N(t)^2N(g)/X\).
  • The large sieve. After a dyadic splitting and a Mellin separation of the weight, the cited quadratic large sieve over the field (Goldmakher and Louvel, their Theorem 1.1 as the source states it: \(\sum_{k}^*|\sum_n^*\beta(n)\chi_k(n)^3|^2\ll(\mathcal HU)^{\epsilon}(\mathcal H+U)\sum|\beta|^2\), both variables squarefree) gives a mean square \(a^2(\mathcal H_0+U)\) with \(U\lesssim Y\), and the dyadic sums converge by the decay of \(V^\sharp\). Result \(\mathcal H_0+a^2Y\le\mathcal H+\mathcal H^2N(g)/X\). The variable \(b\) is not squarefree; it is fixed before the large sieve is applied and summed with weights \(1/N(b)\). Checked.

The repeated rows

  • Sixth powers, \(k=w^6\): \(s=1\), \(g=fw^6\), \(j_p\equiv0\) at \(p\mid w\). The lemma for the single row states \(|T|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(1+N(g)/X)\); its tracked costs give, by my deduction, \(1+N(\operatorname{rad}(fw))/X\): for \(X\) beyond the conductor the completed sum without row twist (the twist \(\xi\chi_n(f)^4\) remains) is bounded, which is what an entire series with a functional equation gives, in line with the entireness read earlier. No conflict.
  • Squares, \(k=v^2\): \(j_p=2\) at primes \(p\nmid f\) with \(v_p(v)\not\equiv0\) modulo three, where the dual factor is \(\chi_p^{-4}=\chi_p^{2}\), a cubic character, for which no large sieve is used: the row is alone (\(\mathcal H_0=1\)) and its bound \(1+N(f)N(v)^2/X\) is the trivial bound for the dual sum of length \(Y\). So on square rows the statement asserts nothing beyond the functional equation, and there are only \(\mathcal H^{1/2}\) of them.
  • Cubes, \(k=w^3\) with \(w\) squarefree: \(s=w\), \(v=w\), \(g=fw^2\), \(j_p\equiv1+8=3\) at \(p\mid w\); such primes are in \(t\), not in \(k_0\), cost \(N(p)^2\), and again the row is bounded trivially.

So the saving comes only from the squarefree part of the row prime to \(g\), where the twist \(\chi_n(k_0)\) is sextic of exponent exactly one.

A sketch of the local factors

Mine, and resting on one recalled and unread property: the multiplier of the cubic theta function is a cubic residue symbol. Twist the coefficients by \(\chi_p^{j}\), \(j\ne0\): by finite Fourier inversion the twist is a combination of translates \(\theta(z+h/p)\) with weights proportional to \(\chi_p^{-j}(h)\) times a Gauss sum. Moving the cusp \(h/p\) to infinity multiplies by a cubic symbol in \(h\), that is \(\chi_p(h)^{\mp2}\), and gives the mode \(\ell\) a phase \(e(-\ell\bar h/p)\). The sum over \(h\) is then a Gauss sum of the character \(\chi_p^{j+2}\) in \(\bar h\) (for one of the two signs) evaluated at \(\ell\): it equals \(\chi_p(\ell)^{-j-2}\) times a Gauss sum when \(\chi_p^{j+2}\) is non-trivial, and a Ramanujan sum \(-1+N(p)1_{p\mid\ell}\) when \(j=4\). The two Gauss sums over \(N(p)\) have modulus one, and for \(j=4\) the product is \(N(p)^{-1/2}(-1+N(p)1_{p\mid\ell})\). This reproduces the source's \(B_{p,j}\) for \(j\ne0\), including the exceptional case. It is a consistency check of the stated formula, not a proof of it. The reviewer read the appendix (the preprint's lines 2874 to 3479) and reports that it derives the factors this way: the multiplier is Kubota's cubic symbol, cited from Dunn and Radziwiłł; because the conjugate theta function is used the sign is \(\chi_p(h)^{-2}\), and the other sign would put the exceptional case at \(j=2\), so the sign matters; the case \(j=0\) is consistent; the constant has modulus at most \(1/81\) and the dependence on \(k_0\) is a unit scalar and a ray class, so no growth in the row is hidden.

Where the dual estimate stands against the trivial and the ideal bounds

From the definitions read (the preprint's lines 934 to 953 and 1000 to 1012): per \(f\), the dual estimate asserts \(\sum_{N(k)\le\mathcal H}|\sum_{N(n)\sim X}a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}X^2F\) on average over \(f\sim F\), in the ranges \(XF=D/B\), \(\mathcal H\le CD^{1-\vartheta}/B^2\). An ideal large sieve for squarefree rows would give \((\mathcal H+X)X\), which is smaller, since \(X\le XF\) and \(\mathcal H\le CD^{1-\vartheta}/B^2\le XF\) once \(D^{\vartheta}\ge C\). So on squarefree rows the dual estimate asks less than an ideal large sieve; what it asks beyond the trivial bound \(\mathcal HX^2\) is a factor \(\mathcal H/F\). Whether it exceeds what is known for general coefficients, and whether a published lower bound for cubic large sieves bears on it, needs sources not read here; the observation above says only that the lower-bound examples would have to live on rows with a sextic twist of exponent one, since the square rows, where the twist is cubic, are bounded trivially.

What this leaves

For the completed mean square, the main-text proof uses the transformation formula with its local factors, the lemma on uniformity in the twist, the bounds for the cusp coefficients and the transformed weight, the realization of the sum by the theta function, and two lemmas on smooth weights. I read none of their appendix proofs; the reviewer read all of them and found no error and no hidden growth in the row. What the reviewer did not verify and the source asserts briefly: one case of the multiplier's reduction (lines 3179 to 3196), the support of the coefficients at two cusps, and the coefficient formula, the last two imported from Patterson and from Dunn and Radziwiłł. And the cited quadratic large sieve, whose statement both of us have only as the source gives it. For the import's theorem: besides these, two auxiliary lemmas of the transfer, the reduction from the Möbius mean square by Poisson summation, the Gauss-sum identity absorbing the Möbius function, and the deduction of the zero-free half-plane from the bound for one sum.

Sub-ideas

Then say at which scales \(F\), \(B\) a known sextic large sieve would already give the dual estimate, from its source.

Owed attempt. The item's previous entry, Lindelöf exact reading, named: read the rest of Section 36 for anything that replaces the count, and decide from a read source whether a count at one fixed distance from the line is known. Made: Sections 36.7 to 36.9 and the first four pages of Section 37 are read. The first question is answered by the book's Section 37.2; the second in part.

Best outcome. Written before the reading: at the goal's own parameters, the best outcome is an unconditional count of zeros of the kind the conditional exclusions need, valid at distances from the line that matter; the other outcome is that every such count is the Lindelöf hypothesis.

Question. The exclusion of a zero at one height from prime sums needs few other zeros near it. What does the book offer in place of the Lindelöf hypothesis for that, and does what it offers hold unconditionally anywhere useful?

New mathematics. None proved. The book's weak Lindelöf hypothesis and its density theorem are registered as thm:turan-weak-lindelof-density, published, statement read and not audited. A heuristic comparison with the shell exclusion is recorded and labelled as such.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The outcome lies between the two named: the count for every distance is the Lindelöf hypothesis, as the book states from Backlund and Littlewood; but a count localized at the edge of a zero-free rectangle is stated to hold unconditionally with a fixed constant, and when the excluded zero is taken rightmost in a window that is the kind of count the shell exclusion asks for. By the comparison below the stated constant is far too large for the shell exclusion: nothing follows at it. In the book's words a small constant away from \(\sigma=1\) is the tangential vanishing of a local \(\mu\)-function at the edge of a zero-free rectangle, which it calls much weaker than the Lindelöf hypothesis and does not prove. Converted. Nothing at the goal's parameters: both the count with a small constant and the bound for prime sums are unknown. Bearing. None; the cycle was spent on it because the count of nearby zeros is one of the two unknown inputs of the only implication on record from prime sums to the absence of a zero, and the book's substitute for it had not been read.

General statement. thm:turan-weak-lindelof-density, for all \(\tfrac12\le\alpha\le1\) and large \(T\) under its hypothesis; a published statement, not a result of this notebook.

What was read

P. Turán, On a New Method of Analysis and Its Applications (1984), as page images from the copy supplied by the user: the book's pages 354 to 361 (the end of Section 36 and Sections 37.1 to 37.4). Not read: the rest of the proof of Theorem 37.1 (Section 37.5 on); the book's own passage (35.6.6) on the case \(\sigma_0=1\), which is in the supplied copy and which I did not turn back to, for no better reason than that the reviewer pointed to it after the reading; and the two papers of the author it cites for the unconditional count and for the last remark of Section 36.

  • Sections 36.7 and 36.8. Corollaries of the exact implication: for all heights in a window \(|\tau_0-T|\le\tfrac12T^{b}\) it gives a zero-free parallelogram, and with the converse theorem a statement that a bound for the prime sums on a short range of lengths implies the same bound on all longer lengths at slightly fewer heights; and a sense in which the parallelogram is sharpest. All under the Lindelöf hypothesis. The book remarks of its second corollary there, the converse statement about primes alone, that it sees no proof of it "while remaining in the realm of integers".
  • Section 36.9. Under the Lindelöf hypothesis, for \(0\le b\le1\), \(b/a<\tfrac12\) strictly, with the bound \(N\log^{100}N/|\tau_0|^{b}\) for all heights and all \(N\ge|\tau_0|^{a}\), the supremum of \(b/a\) over admissible pairs determines the abscissa \(1-\sup b/a\) to the right of which only finitely many zeros lie. The book adds that this was proved in a paper of its author "assuming a much weaker form of the Lindelöf conjecture", described in the next section.
  • Section 37.2. "Backlund and Littlewood showed that the L.H. is equivalent to \(N(\alpha,T+1)-N(\alpha,T)=o(\log T)\) for each \(\alpha>\tfrac12\)", part of which Section 36 used. Then the weak hypothesis: for every \(A>0\) there is \(0<\delta=\delta(A)<A\) such that the number of zeros in the circle \(|s-s_0|\le\delta\), \(s_0=\sigma_0+i\tau_0\), is less than \(A\delta\log\tau_0\) whenever \(\zeta(s)\ne0\) in \(\Re s>\sigma_0\), \(|\Im s-\tau_0|<\log\tau_0\), with \(\sigma_0\ge\tfrac12+10\delta\), \(\tau_0\ge c(A,\delta)\). The book says it follows trivially from the Lindelöf hypothesis in the form above; that "in this form, however, it follows from general function theoretic considerations with \(A=0.71\) for all \(\delta<\tfrac1{10}\)", citing a paper of its author; and that it is proved with arbitrary \(A\) at \(\sigma_0=1\), by the tangential vanishing of the Lindelöf \(\mu\)-function there. It adds that without the restriction to a zero-free rectangle even a much weaker count "would be just as difficult" as the Lindelöf hypothesis, and that the weak hypothesis calls for a proof that the "local \(\mu\)-function" vanishes tangentially at any point of the strip with a large zero-free rectangle to its right. The constant \(0.71\) is read from a small scan and I could not check it against a second use, so it is uncertain; that it exceeds \(0.01\) is consistent with the theorem below needing a hypothesis.
  • Theorem 37.1 (thm:turan-weak-lindelof-density). If the weak hypothesis holds with an \(A<0.01\), then \(N(\alpha,T)<T^{2(1-\alpha)+5\sqrt A}\) for \(\tfrac12\le\alpha\le1\), \(T>c(A)\). Its proof begins with the kernel of Section 36 and a mean square over heights; I read it to the first mean-value bound only.

Answers to the owed question

A count of off-line zeros in unit windows for every distance from the line is the Lindelöf hypothesis itself, by the equivalence the book states. For one fixed distance the pages read give nothing beyond that. What the book does offer is a count of a different shape: not all zeros to the right of a line, but zeros in a small disk centred on the edge of a region free of zeros to its right. This is weaker than the Lindelöf hypothesis, and with a fixed constant it is stated as known.

A heuristic comparison with the shell exclusion

Not a proof; orders of magnitude only, with the constants of lem:shell-zero-sum-inequality replaced by their shapes. Take the hypothetical zero \(\rho^*=1-x+i\tau_0\) rightmost among zeros with heights within \(\log\tau_0\) of \(\tau_0\); this needs the prime-sum premise at every height of such a window, the book's semilocal form. The shell exclusion with centre \(1+\mu+i\tau_0\), least distance \(d=\mu+x\), derivative order \(k\approx2\mu\log\tau_0\) and shell \(e^{\varepsilon}d\) needs at most \(\kappa\log\tau_0\) zeros in the shell, where, writing \(L\) for the logarithm in the second main theorem's loss (at least \(\log8e\approx3.1\)): the loss \(e^{-KL}\), \(K=\kappa\log\tau_0\), must fit in the margin between the premise's saving \(\tau_0^{-1}\) and the zero's term, \(\kappa L\le g(\mu)=1-2\mu\log(1+x/\mu)\), and the zeros outside the shell must fall below it, \(\varepsilon\approx\kappa L/(2\mu)\). With \(\rho^*\) rightmost, the zeros in the shell lie in a lens to the left of \(\Re s=1-x\) of half-height about \(\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\,d\) and small depth; covering it by disks of radius \(\delta\) centred on the edge, the weak hypothesis with constant \(A\) bounds their number by about \(A\sqrt{2\varepsilon}\,d\log\tau_0\). So the requirement is \(A(\mu+x)\sqrt{\kappa L/\mu}\le\kappa\), that is \(\kappa\ge A^2(\mu+x)^2L/\mu\), together with \(\kappa L\le g(\mu)\): \[A^2L^2\frac{(\mu+x)^2}{\mu}\le1-2\mu\log\Big(1+\frac x\mu\Big).\] My draft read off from this that the constant \(0.71\) allows \(x\) up to about \(0.02\). The reviewer showed that this is wrong, and I withdraw it: the two requirements together force \(\varepsilon\ge A^2L^2(\mu+x)^2/(2\mu^2)\ge A^2L^2/2\), which at \(A=0.71\) and \(L\ge3.1\) is at least \(2.4\), so the shell is not thin, the lens is not a lens, and the comparison has no consistent regime. A thin shell needs \(AL\ll1\), roughly \(A\) below one twentieth. So at the stated unconditional constant the comparison gives nothing. Further defects the reviewer found in its shape: the margin \(g(\mu)\) drops a parameter of the shell corollary (its \(t<1\), which at \(\mu=x\) leaves a margin of about \(1-6x\)), so the optimization in \(\mu\) is unjustified; disks centred on the edge reach only depth \(\delta\), so the covering needs \(\delta(A)\) not too small, an extra assumption the hypothesis does not give; a rightmost zero near the end of a window is not rightmost in its own neighbourhood, so the semilocal premise is circular and the all-heights form of Section 36.9 is the one that works; and \(L\) is not bounded (it was \(9.4\) in the shell entry). What survives, as an untested expectation only: as \(A\to0\), with \(\mu\) fixed first, the count suffices, in line with the book's remark that its author proved the statement of Section 36.9 under the weak hypothesis. The comparison also never asked how it stands against the recorded published width \(1/1600\) at these exponents; with its number withdrawn there is nothing to compare.

Degenerate cases. Of the published statement: at \(\alpha=1\) the bound is \(T^{5\sqrt A}\), not the zero count's truth; at \(A\to0\) it tends to the density hypothesis; a height with no zero in the disk makes the weak hypothesis vacuous there.

Nonvacuity. The weak hypothesis has instances: under the Riemann hypothesis every \(\sigma_0\ge\tfrac12+10\delta\) satisfies its condition and the disk is empty. Whether it holds with a constant below \(0.01\) is not known to me.

Sub-ideas

  • Read the book's passage (35.6.6) on the count at \(\sigma_0=1\) and the rest of Section 37, and derive the unconditional constant from the convexity of the \(\mu\)-function and Jensen's formula as the reviewer sketches (it obtained about \(2.7\), not \(0.71\)); then say, from a read source, how the constant behaves at \(\sigma_0=1-x\) for small \(x\), where a bound \(\mu(\sigma)\le c(1-\sigma)^{3/2}\) is recalled to make it small.

What the six entries did. Four audited the import. The first located the saving in a dual estimate free of the Möbius function; the second checked how the theta function's polar term is removed; the third checked the induction that proves the dual estimate, its reviewer reading the transfer estimate; the fourth checked the completed mean square in the main text, its reviewer reading the appendix. No error was found by anyone. What the goal needs. An inequality that a hypothetical zero off the line violates, at every height. The import, if right, supplies one for real parts above \(11/12\) (and \(7/8\) in its companion) and nothing below; the book's implication supplies one conditionally on a bound for prime sums and a count of nearby zeros, both unknown.

Falsification attempt. Of the period's own conclusions. That "no error was found" in the import does not validate it. The parts read are the induction, the functional equation and the use of a cited large sieve; the dual estimate they lead to itself asserts cancellation (exponent \(11/12\) for untwisted sums of Gauss coefficients at the sixth-power rows) and could be the false step, through the parts only a reviewer read or the cited statements nobody read. Three steps of the proof are unread by anyone here: the identity that turns Möbius coefficients into theta coefficients, the Poisson reduction that produces the dual family, and the deduction of the half-plane from the bound for one sum. The period audited where the reading was easiest to check. Of the readings of the book: the entry on the weak hypothesis attached a numerical conclusion that its own inequalities refute, caught by its reviewer; what remains of it is a reading, with one constant uncertain. The claim that the conditional exclusions "rediscover" the book's theorem was first written as identity and corrected to a rediscovery of method, neither statement implying the other.

Assessment of this line. The line still has no approach to the open statement. What the period established is negative or contextual: the notebook's one-height implication is published in better form; its missing count is the Lindelöf hypothesis unless localized at the edge of a zero-free region, where the known constant is too large; the import's mechanism, as far as read, uses sextic symbols and coefficients that become automorphic, and whether it has a form elsewhere is untested (a reviewer's recalled reason against is on record, unverified). Seven entries in a row declare no bearing. Either way it does not approach the line \(\tfrac12\): by this notebook's prop:moment-extraction-exponents, not by any statement of the import, an extraction of its kind stops at \(1-1/(2k)\).

Through thm:turan-lindelof-exact-implication (published, read) the target at one height is a saving for prime sums on a short range of lengths together with the Lindelöf hypothesis's count; the consequence is exclusion at that height to the right of \(\tfrac12+\delta\), weaker than the goal by the two unknown inputs. Through the import (unaudited) and prop:moment-extraction-exponents the consequence is a half-plane \(\sigma>1-1/(2k)\), weaker than the goal.

Extra requirements. The one-height route demands a count of zeros that the height-free route does not; the height-free route demands a power saving for Möbius sums at every scale, which is the hypothesis itself at exponent \(\tfrac12\). The import's architecture demands a family in which the sum recurs and whose dual coefficients are automorphic; the recurrence costs the factor that stops it at \(1-1/(2k)\). Nothing here can be weakened without a new idea: the count is intrinsic to lengths near \(\tau^{2}\), and the recurrence to residue symbols.

Independent alternative. Its verdict: none found that reaches the line. Its reasons, derived from the brief: the book's implication and its count are transfers and not inputs, a prime-sum saving with \(b/a\) near \(\tfrac12\) at every height being essentially the goal; the one arithmetic input, the import, sits at the ceiling \(1-1/(2k)\) and its limit is structural, at best \(\tfrac34\) from a quadratic symbol; and the one non-circular combination, the import's half-plane supplying the count for the book's implication, is unconditional only where nothing is left to exclude. Decisive test. Read the preprint's deduction of the half-plane from the bound for one sum (its first sections, to line 386), tracing the weight \(\nu\) from the final sum back to the dual estimate; its Gauss-sum identity (line 322 and proof); and its reduction proposition (line 1000 and proof). Three questions, each with two outcomes. (a) Is the identity \(\gamma_1\gamma_2=\mu\alpha G\) true: read its proof and test it numerically on Eisenstein primes and products of two primes of small norm, computing the Gauss sums from their definitions; a failure at any tested element breaks the import's proof at that line. (b) Do the ranges of rows, columns and auxiliary moduli that the reduction produces lie inside those in which the dual estimate is stated, including the factor \(\chi_n(f)^4\); a range outside locates a gap. (c) Is the weight \(\nu\) in the instance needed for \(\zeta\) one the mean square covers, and does the saving for one sum give the half-plane as stated; if not, the import's theorem does not concern \(\zeta\). An earlier draft of this test asked for "the line where the constant function fails" and inferred an error if none were found; the reviewer showed both halves unsound: the constant function fails at the identity by construction, and a failed search locates nothing. Trivial cases. The identity is trivial at units and, as far as the outline read says, has the Möbius function vanish with the Gauss coefficient at non-squarefree elements, so the test in (a) must use squarefree elements with at least two prime factors to test multiplicativity with its twist; a renormalization of the Gauss sums by a unit changes \(G\) and not the outcome. Questions (b) and (c) have no trivial instance: they compare stated ranges. Power. The method limits on record say nothing against any outcome: prop:moment-extraction-exponents bounds what the mean square gives, not whether it holds, and the reviewers' recollection that the half-plane is not in the literature is not evidence of an error. For (a) the reachable sizes are norms up to a few hundred, where Gauss sums are computed exactly in a few seconds by PARI/GP, and a wrong identity of this multiplicative shape differs from the right one already at the smallest primes of each residue class; a pass is evidence only for the identity, not for the theorem. For (b) and (c) the test is a reading of about five hundred lines. Record search. The registry was searched for Gauss sum identity absorbs Moebius function Poisson reduction mean square sixth power rows: no record answers the test; the dual mean square reading records the false case for the constant function and the completed mean square audit the question as a sub-idea.

11 October 2026 — The import's three arithmetic steps and its transfer proof, read by me: the single-element form of its Gauss-sum identity and its twisted multiplicativity hold numerically at the norms tested, the reduction's ranges fit the dual estimate's, and the deduction of the half-plane covers the trivial character; no error found; the identity's proof and the paired forms the proofs use are unchecked

(c) Is the weight in the instance for \(\zeta\) covered and does the saving give the half-plane: yes as read; the mean square is stated for every finite-order Hecke character of the field, the trivial one included, and the deduction is the standard Mellin argument. So the two questions answered have the outcome that leaves the import unbroken by this notebook, and the first is answered in part.

General statement. None of mine. The statement examined is the imported thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its intermediate preprint; unaudited.

The deduction

Lines 660 to 772, read. For a finite-order Hecke character \(\nu\) of \(K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})\) and a finite set \(S\) of primes containing those above \(2\), \(3\) and the conductor, \(A_u(D)=\sum_{(n,S)=1}\mu(n)\nu(n)\chi_n(u)W(N(n)/D)\). The mean square: \(\sum_{0<N(u)\le D^{1+\vartheta}}|A_u(D)|^2\ll D^{2+\vartheta+\epsilon}\). For primes \(p\) of norm in \((Y/2,Y]\), \(Y=H^{1/6}\), \(\chi_n(p^6)\) is the indicator of \(p\nmid n\), so \(|A_1-A_{p^6}|\ll D/Y\); the sixth powers are distinct rows of norm at most \(H\), about \(Y/\log Y\) of them; hence \(|A_1|^2\ll D^{2+\vartheta+\epsilon}/Y+D^2/Y^2\) and \(A_1\ll D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\epsilon}\). Recomputed: \(2+\vartheta-(1+\vartheta)/6=11/6+5\vartheta/6\). Then, for a zero \(\varrho\) with \(\Re\varrho>11/12\) and \(W(y)=y^{-\varrho}\phi(y)\), the integral \(\int A_1(D)D^{-s}dD/D\) is holomorphic for \(\Re s>11/12\) and equals \(\widehat W(s)/L_K^S(s,\nu)\), so \(\widehat W(\varrho)=0\), against \(\widehat W(\varrho)=\int\phi\,dy/y>0\). This is sound given the mean square. The trivial character is an instance, \(L_K(s,1)=\zeta(s)L(s,\chi_{-3})\), and nothing in the statement of the mean square excludes it. The implied constants depend on \(W\), hence on \(\varrho\): there is no uniformity in the height, which the argument does not need.

The identity

As stated in the outline (line 322): for squarefree primary \(n\) prime to the excluded primes, \(\mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\), with \(\gamma_j(n)=N(n)^{-1/2}\sum_{x\bmod n}\chi_n(x)^je(x/n)\), \(e(z)=\exp(4\pi i\,\Im z/\sqrt3)\), \(\alpha(n)=n/|n|\), \(G(n)=\overline{\chi_n(4)}\gamma_3(n)\). Its proof is in the appendix, unread. For a prime it is what two classical relations give, as I recall them and did not read: the relation between the Gauss sums of a character, its square and the quadratic character, and the cube of the cubic Gauss sum being \(\mu(n)N(n)\) times \(n\) or its conjugate.

Numerical check. Script and output: both sides computed from the definitions in PARI/GP at thirty digits, the sextic symbol through the residue field of each prime ideal, for seven primes (norms 7 twice, 13, 19, 31, and the rational primes 5 and 11 of norms 25 and 121), six products of two primes (norms 49, 91, 133, 175, 247, 403) and one of three (norm 637). In all fourteen the two sides agree to \(10^{-34}\) or better. The control, \(\alpha\) in place of \(\overline\alpha\), differs by more than \(1\) in the eleven cases where \(\alpha(n)\) is not real and coincides, as it must, in the three where \(n\) is rational. Second script and output: \(\gamma_2(vm)=\gamma_2(v)\gamma_2(m)\chi_m(v)^4\) for eight coprime pairs, the relation behind the import's rule \(a_\xi(vm)=a_\xi(v)a_\xi(m)\chi_m(v)^4\); agreement to \(10^{-35}\), and the control with \(\chi_m(v)^2\) differs by \(\sqrt3\) in the six pairs where the cubic symbol is not one. Nonvacuity. Fourteen and eight instances, each satisfying the hypotheses (squarefree, primary generators computed, prime to 6); a wrong sign, a wrong conjugation or a wrong exponent in either relation would show at the first split prime, as the controls do. The checks say nothing about the ray-class factor's dependence on the excluded set or about the paired form used in the proofs, which is the appendix's.

The reduction

Lines 1000 to 1253, read. Expand \(\sum_u\Phi(N(u)/H)|A_u|^2\); with \(g=(n_1,n_2)\), \(n_j=gz_j\), apply Poisson summation in \(u\) to the character \(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}\) with the exclusion \((u,g)=1\). The zero frequency needs the character trivial, which for coprime squarefree \(z_1,z_2\) happens only at \(z_1=z_2=1\), and gives the diagonal \(\ll H\) (after the normalization by \(1/D\)). At the other frequencies the Gauss sum of the product character, with \(\mu(z_1)\mu(z_2)\) and \(\nu\), becomes \(\sum_\xi c_\xi a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}\): this is where the Möbius function is used and where squarefreeness is used. Removing \((z_1,z_2)=1\) by a divisor \(v\) and changing variables \(b=g/e\), \(f=ev\), \(k=eh\) gives rows \(kf^4\), which is the origin of the factor \(\chi_n(f)^4\), with \(N(b)N(f)\ll D\) and \(0<N(k)\le CD^2/(HN(b)^2)\). In dyadic ranges \(X=D/(BF)\), \(\mathcal H=CD^2/(HB^2)\), \(XF=D/B\). Recomputed: \(\mathcal H/(XF)=CD/(HB)\le CD^{-\vartheta}\), inside the dual estimate's hypothesis \(\mathcal H\le XFD^{-\kappa}\) with \(\kappa=\vartheta/2\) for large \(D\); the shifts that remove the exclusion \((n,b)=1\) keep \(XF\) and \(\mathcal H\); the cases \(X<1\) or \(\mathcal H<1\) are trivial. With the dual estimate, \(\sum_{f,k}|S_U(b,f,k)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(XF)^2\), and the block is \((HB/D^2)\cdot B\cdot(D/B)^2=H\). The ranges fit and the arithmetic closes. Why it must fail for coefficients \(\mu(n)^2\): with them the mean square is false, the sixth-power rows alone giving about \(D^2H^{1/6}\), more than \(D^{2+\vartheta}\) for \(\vartheta<1/5\). The reduction goes through unchanged up to the dual coefficients, which are then \(\mu a_\xi\); so the dual estimate for \(\mu a_\xi\) must be false, and the step of the import that separates \(\mu\) from \(\mu^2\) is the one that needs \(a_\xi\) itself: the completed mean square, through the theta function. For coefficients \(1\), squarefreeness fails as well and the zero frequency is not only the diagonal.

The transfer proof

Lines 2217 to 2686, read by me; the reviewer of an earlier entry had read them. The smoothed mean square with Gauss coefficients, rows up to \(\mathcal H\), columns at \(L\), is expanded; Poisson summation in the row turns the paired Gauss coefficients back into \(\mu(u_1)\mu(u_2)\) times a ray-class factor, with new rows \(y=hf^2\) of norm up to about \(L^2F^2/\mathcal H\). The nonnegative form is then enlarged, by positivity, to rows up to \(Y_{C,d}=c\,\Sigma LFN(d)/(\mathcal HN(C)^2)\), larger by the factor \(\Sigma/(LF)\ge1\). A second Poisson summation, in \(y\), returns Gauss coefficients with rows \(k'\) of norm at most \(\mathcal HL/(\Sigma FN(r)^2)\) and an auxiliary modulus \(f'\). Recomputed by me: the weight \(w_{C,d}Y_{C,d}N(g)/(N(e)\ell)=c\,\Sigma N(r)/L^2\); the Fourier parameter \(c\,\Sigma FN(k')N(r)^2/(\mathcal HL)\); the block count \((\Sigma R/L^2)\cdot R\cdot(L/R)^2=\Sigma\); the zero frequencies \(\sum w_{C,d}Y_{C,d}\ell=c\,\Sigma\sum1/(N(C)^2N(t))\ll\Sigma\log\); and the one signed step, the sum of \(\mu(e)\mu(w)\) over the factorizations of \(f'\) at a prime, \((1+1_{p\mid k'})-1_{p\mid k'}-1_{p\nmid k'}=1_{p\mid k'}\). All as the source states; the reviewer confirmed the five and the lower bound on the new column scale. On the structure, my draft called the whole proof a recursion whose base is the completed mean square, each round of transfer shortening the rows; the reviewer corrected both points from the source. The proof is an induction on \(\mathcal H\) whose base is the trivial bound at \(\mathcal H\le1\); each step is the cube reduction, where the completed mean square (theta function and quadratic large sieve) enters, followed by the transfer; and the transfer alone gives only \(\mathcal H'\le\mathcal HL/(\Sigma F)\), no gain at \(L=X\), \(F=1\): the shortening \(\mathcal H'<\mathcal H(\mathcal H/\Sigma)^2\) comes from the cube index being large, \(N(b)>H_c\), in the cube reduction. What stands of my description: the two Poisson summations exchange Möbius and Gauss coefficients, which is why the transfer returns the same family. Not read by me inside this proof: the paired identity it uses, the multiplicity of the map \(y=hf^2\), and the lemma on smooth weights.

11 October 2026 — The single-row test of the import's theta input: with the angular twist the completed sum of cubic Gauss coefficients shows no growth up to \(X=65536\) and is below the random-walk scale from \(X=4096\) on, while without the twist it grows at a rate consistent with a polar term; the test does not separate the twist from its conjugate and could not see a polar term a fifth the size of the control's

Finite numerical test of one consequence of an unaudited import, with controls; no result about zeta zeros. Single-row test. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, low effort).

Where we stand. Unchanged: Where we stand is at its word limit and already links the import's status; this test changes no standing statement

The best outcome is growth of the completed sum with the import's twist, which would break the import's proof at its theta input and remove its half-planes from the standing position; the other outcome, no growth, is evidence for that one input only.

Question. At one row (\(\mathcal H=1\), \(f=1\), trivial character) the import's completed mean square asserts \(|T(X)|^2\ll D^{\epsilon}(1+1/X)\), with \(D\) any scale of which \(X\) is at most a fixed power, so \(X^{\epsilon}\) here, for \(T(X)=X^{-1/2}\sum_{n,b}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\overline{\alpha(b)}^3N(b)^{1/2}W(N(n)N(b)^3/X)\), the sum over primary \(n,b\) prime to 6 with \(n\) squarefree. Without the angular factors the sum is expected to have a polar term and \(T\) to grow like \(X^{1/3}\). Does the computed \(T(X)\) grow?

New mathematics. None. A computation at eleven sizes with four controls.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The computation finds no growth with the import's twist and growth without it, so the import is not broken at this input by this test, within the test's power stated below. Converted. Nothing at the goal's parameters. Bearing. None; the cycle was spent on it because the previous entry's reviewer named this as the cheapest test at the step where the import separates the Möbius function from its square.

General statement. None of mine. The statement tested is a consequence of the import's completed mean square, part of the unaudited thm:openai-seven-eighths-half-plane through its intermediate preprint.

The computation

Kernel (C with OpenMP; the run took six seconds on fourteen threads by the launcher's record in this entry's session archive). The Gauss sum \(\gamma_2\) is computed from its definition at every prime element of norm up to \(2\cdot10^5\): at a split prime through a primitive root of the residue field, at an inert prime by summing over the field of \(q^2\) elements; at a composite squarefree \(n\) by the twisted multiplicativity checked in the previous entry, with cubic symbols computed in the residue fields. That gives \(\gamma_2\) at all \(56434\) squarefree primary \(n\) prime to 6 of norm up to \(2\cdot10^5\). The weight is \(W(y)=\exp(-1/((y-1)(2-y)))\) on \((1,2)\), whose maximum is \(e^{-4}\approx0.018\), so all values below carry that scale. Control of the kernel. Sixteen values of \(\gamma_2\) (split primes, an inert prime, the rational primes 7 and 13 as products of two conjugate primes, five products of two distinct split primes, and, added at the reviewer's request, a product with an inert factor of norm 175 and a product of three primes of norm 637) were recomputed in PARI/GP directly from the definition, summing over all residues with the cubic symbol taken in each residue field (script, output, against the kernel's dump): all agree to the fifteen digits printed. This control does not share the kernel's method at composite \(n\), where the kernel uses the multiplicativity and PARI the definition. It does share the kernel's convention for the cubic symbol, so it cannot catch a symbol taken for its conjugate against the source; that is harmless here, because \(\gamma_2(\bar n)=\overline{\gamma_2(n)}\), so such a mismatch exactly exchanges the columns "import" and "conjugate twist" below, and both show no growth. The reviewer read the kernel line by line and found no bug, and the count \(56434\) matches the density of such \(n\).

Stated before the run (in my working notes of this cycle, not in the record): with the import's twist \(T\) should not grow, and if the functional equation acted with a small conductor it should decay; with no angular factor it should grow like \(X^{1/3}\); with the conjugate twist \(\alpha\) I expected the random-walk size.

Result

Output. Columns: \(X\); the number of terms; \(|T|\) with the import's twist; with \(\alpha\) in place of \(\overline\alpha\); with no angular factor; the random-walk scale (root of the sum of squares of the terms, same normalization).

\(X\)termsimportconjugate twistno twistrandom-walk scale
10242940.00790.00340.01280.0053
20485890.00890.00040.01320.0053
409611510.00110.00260.01510.0055
819223200.00110.00620.02190.0053
1638446510.00340.00780.02430.0053
3276892810.00140.00260.03880.0055
65536185760.00040.00190.04670.0054

The file has four smaller sizes and the two sums restricted to \(b=1\), which behave alike. Without the twist \(T\) rises from \(0.015\) at \(X=4096\) to \(0.047\) at \(65536\), a factor \(3.1\) over a factor \(16\) in \(X\), against \(2.5\) for \(X^{1/3}\), and ends at \(8.7\) times the random-walk scale: growth is visible and consistent with a polar term, the exponent not being determined by these sizes. With the import's twist \(T\) lies between \(0.07\) and \(1.7\) times the random-walk scale at all eleven sizes, at most \(0.63\) times it from \(X=4096\) on, with no trend. With the conjugate twist it lies between \(0.003\) and \(1.5\) times that scale, also with no trend.

What the test can and cannot say

  • It separates the twisted sum from the untwisted one: the growth the import must avoid is present without the angular factor and absent with it. That is what the import's removal of the polar term predicts (polar term audit).
  • It does not separate \(\overline\alpha\) from \(\alpha\). My expectation that only the import's twist would be small was not a prediction of anything read, and the data do not support a difference. The reviewer gives a reason, unverified, why none should exist: the two twisted series are Mellin transforms of the two horizontal derivatives of translates of the theta function, each of which kills the constant term at every cusp. If so, the test cannot separate them in principle.
  • No decay is seen with the import's twist. A decay is what a functional equation gives beyond the conductor. The reviewer estimated from the source's transformation formula where it should set in: the dual index lies in \(\lambda^{-4}\mathcal O\), of least norm \(1/81\), and the weight's argument is \((2\pi)^4N(\ell)X/(27N(c_0)^2)\), so decay needs \(X\) well above about \(1.4\,N(c_0)^2\); for a twist periodic modulo about 18 that threshold is between about \(2\cdot10^3\) and \(1.5\cdot10^5\), and later for this weight's large derivatives. So the absence of decay at \(65536\) is not a finding against the import or the kernel. I did not redo that estimate.
  • A weak hint, the reviewer's: from \(X=4096\) on the five values with the import's twist are all at most \(0.63\) of the random-walk scale on disjoint supports, which for a real Gaussian walk has probability about two percent. Not more than a hint.
  • Power. A polar term in the twisted sum with coefficient one fifth of the untwisted one's would be about \(1.7\) times the random-walk scale at the largest size and could not be told from the fluctuations seen at the smaller ones. So the test excludes a polar term comparable to the control's and nothing smaller. Ten times larger \(X\) would improve this by a factor \(2.2\) only; I do not run it.
  • One row says nothing about uniformity over rows, which is where the mean square's strength lies.

Nonvacuity. The sums have between 19 and 18576 terms; at the seven sizes tabulated the trivial bound is from 12 to 96 times the random-walk scale, so growth and its absence differ visibly, as the untwisted control shows.

Sub-ideas

Question. thm:resonance-free-bandwidth finds, for off-axis points at distinct heights, a bandwidth in every interval of length \(4M^2/(gm_*^2)\) at which the largest pair shows. How short can the interval be?

New mathematics. A theorem, a corollary and a proposition for the flat window. The interval may have length \((8\pi/\sqrt3)\sqrt{m'/m_*}/g\), with \(m'\) the largest multiplicity among the other points; for equal distances length \(2\pi\sqrt{8/3}/g\) suffices and recovers \(S=(\sum m_j^2)^{1/2}\) in place of \(m_*\); and for two equally spaced families the quantity through which both proofs work stays below their threshold on an interval of length \(\pi/g-t\). The theorem's final form, with \(m'\) in place of \(S\), is the reviewer's improvement of my draft, checked and adopted.
